NCA Administrative Law Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (It cannot displace correctness where the rule of law requires it, as for constitutional questions): Legislatures may choose the standard, but Vavilov holds that the rule of law preserves correctness for constitutional questions, questions of central importance and jurisdictional boundary disputes. A statute cannot lower the standard for those. Nothing prevents a legislature from prescribing correctness elsewhere.
  2. The correct answer is B (No, tribunals have only express or necessarily implied powers, and this statute's structure excludes costs): Tribunals have no inherent jurisdiction; they have only the powers given expressly or by necessary implication. Here the statute grants costs in one Part and is silent in the appeal provisions, which points against implying a costs power. The general 'any order necessary' wording is read in light of that structure and does not import the courts' inherent powers.
Case CHB1

Marisol Quintero runs a licensed home daycare in Hamilton. Under an extract of the fictional Child Care Standards Act, 'the Director may suspend a licence where satisfied that children's health is at risk, and shall give written reasons.' The Director suspended her licence after a complaint, gave no reasons, and did not contact her first. Five months later she consults counsel.

  1. The correct answer is C (File an application for judicial review, normally returnable in the Ontario Divisional Court): The Act channels challenges to a statutory power of decision into an application for judicial review, ordinarily heard by the Divisional Court. A damages action does not test the legality of the suspension itself, and the extract creates no right of appeal. Nothing in the extract makes reconsideration a precondition to review.
  2. The correct answer is D (As a discretionary factor, weighing the reason for delay and any prejudice to others): Without a fixed period, delay goes to the court's discretion to refuse relief, and the court looks at how long, why, and whether anyone relied on the decision or would be prejudiced. Thirty days is the federal rule and six months is Alberta's, so neither governs an Ontario application. A court can still refuse relief for unexplained delay even without a deadline.
  3. The correct answer is C (Quash the suspension and return the matter to the Director to decide again fairly): Quashing the suspension cures the unfair process while leaving the judgment about children's health with the Director, who must decide again after hearing from her. Mandamus to restore the licence or a substituted finding would take over a discretion the statute gave to the Director. A bare declaration leaves the suspension operating, which gives her no practical relief.
  4. The correct answer is A (Decline, because courts do not reweigh evidence and the tribunal explained its preference reasonably): On reasonableness review the court does not reweigh or reassess the evidence considered by the decision maker, absent exceptional circumstances. The tribunal gave a reasoned basis for its preference, which is what the review looks for. Palpable and overriding error is an appellate standard and does not apply here, and nothing requires a further medical assessment.
Case CHB2

Extract from the fictional Rental Housing Act. Section 5: The Minister may appoint members of the Rental Review Board for terms of up to one year, renewable at the Minister's pleasure. Section 6: The Minister may remove a member at any time. Facts: Ines Moreau, a tenant, faces eviction by the provincial housing corporation, a Crown agency that appears before the Board in about 40 per cent of its cases. Last year the Minister removed a member two days after she ruled against the corporation.

  1. The correct answer is C (Whether an informed person would reasonably perceive a lack of independence in the Board's structure): Structural independence is judged by the perception of a reasonably informed person looking at tenure, financial and administrative arrangements, not by proof of actual interference. Tribunal members do not need judicial tenure, and the old judicial or administrative label does not decide the question.
  2. The correct answer is D (Clear statutory language on tenure displaces the common law requirement, absent constitutional or quasi-constitutional protection): Under Ocean Port, a legislature may specify tribunal structure in clear terms and the common law cannot override it, absent a constitutional or quasi-constitutional guarantee. Common law independence does not invalidate statute, and the judicial or administrative classification is not what determines the result.
  3. The correct answer is A (The removal after an adverse ruling raises a reasonable apprehension that her panel lacks independence): The statute authorizes short terms and removal, so a bare structural attack fails under Ocean Port. The stronger path is case-specific: an apparent use of the removal power to punish an adverse ruling against a Crown agency party may ground a reasonable apprehension that her panel is not independent of the Minister.
  4. The correct answer is D (Correctness for the legal test, because a statutory appeal on law attracts appellate review): Where the legislature provides a statutory appeal, appellate standards apply: correctness for questions of law and palpable and overriding error for facts. The reasonableness presumption governs judicial review, not an appeal. The deferential fact standard does not extend to the legal test.
  5. The correct answer is A (Limits are enforceable, but disregarding key material without notice or a chance to respond is unfair): Tribunals may control their procedure and impose reasonable page limits, but a party should be told that material will not be considered and be allowed to comply or seek leave, particularly where the material is central. A practice direction does not remove the duty of fairness. The right to be heard does not depend on proving the outcome would have differed, and tribunals do not need express statutory authority to set reasonable limits.
  6. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because the decision contains an internal contradiction that the reasons do not resolve): Reasons that contradict each other on a point central to the outcome fail the test of internal rationality, because the reader cannot tell what the tribunal actually found. Deference on credibility applies to a coherent assessment, not to two incompatible ones. No fresh evidence is needed since the contradiction appears on the face of the decision.
  7. The correct answer is A (No, remedies are discretionary, so the court may refuse relief despite a reviewable error): Prerogative and statutory remedies are discretionary, and conduct of the applicant and the seriousness of the error can justify refusing relief. A privative clause is not required for that discretion. The label jurisdictional no longer drives the analysis.
  8. The correct answer is C (Quashing the approval and sending the matter back so the Crown can consult meaningfully): A breach of the duty to consult is usually remedied by setting aside the unlawful approval and requiring the Crown to consult before deciding again. Haida Nation explains that consultation is a process of honourable dealing and gives an asserted-title claimant no veto, so a consent order is wrong. A declaration of title requires proof of title, which is not before the court, and damages alone would leave an unlawful approval operating.
Case CHB3

Priya Venkataraman, a single mother in Windsor, applied for disability support. A caseworker for the provincial program denied her after reviewing a medical report from the program's own physician, Dr. Lund, who concluded she could work part time. She received a one-page denial letter listing no reasons. Under the program's regulation (fictional extract): 'A review officer may confirm or vary a denial after considering the file and any written statement from the applicant.' Ms. Venkataraman was never sent Dr. Lund's report. Her own doctors say she cannot work, and Dr. Lund never examined her. The review officer upheld the denial on the file.

  1. The correct answer is D (No, without the grounds for denial she could not prepare a meaningful response): Notice must tell the person enough of the case to answer it. A bare outcome with no grounds and no mention of the physician's report left her unable to respond usefully. The right to send a statement is only valuable if she knows what to address, and fairness does not depend on a formal statutory notice of hearing.
  2. The correct answer is D (Disclosure is owed in time to answer it, since the denial rested on that report): A person must be given the material the decision-maker relies on, early enough to respond. The report was the basis for the denial, so it had to be sent before the review. A regulation's silence does not remove the common law duty, a conclusion alone does not let her challenge the reasoning, and the tribunal record in judicial review comes too late.
  3. The correct answer is A (Probably yes, as the dispute concerns documentary medical opinions rather than her credibility): An oral hearing is generally needed when credibility is in issue, and here the conflict is between written medical opinions that can be compared on paper once she has seen and answered the report. High stakes alone do not demand an oral hearing. The regulation is relevant but does not settle fairness, and cross-examination is not required in every contested file.
  4. The correct answer is A (Clear statutory language excluding a hearing displaces the common law duty, absent a constitutional constraint): Parliament may define the procedure a federal body follows, and the common law duty gives way to clear and express contrary language, as Ocean Port confirms. The Baker factors set the content of the duty only where the legislature has not already fixed the process. Constitutional limits are not invoked on these facts.
  5. The correct answer is B (No, because Mark received notice of the supervision issue and a real opportunity to answer): Fairness asks whether the person knew the case in substance and had a real chance to respond, not whether formal pleading rules were observed. Here the panel gave notice of the supervision issue, it grew out of testimony already heard, and a two-day adjournment let Mark prepare. The adjournment cured the surprise rather than evidencing prejudice, and the lack of a formal amendment is not fatal where substance was met.
  6. The correct answer is C (Whether an informed person would perceive bias in a substantial number of cases from the structure): An institutional bias claim attacks the structure rather than one decision-maker, so the court asks whether a well-informed observer, viewing the matter realistically and practically, would apprehend bias in a substantial number of cases. A case-specific inquiry or proof of a formal ministry direction does not fit a structural allegation.
  7. The correct answer is D (They are factors weighed together in a flexible, generous and purposive way, not rigid prerequisites): In Downtown Eastside the Court held that the factors are to be considered cumulatively and in light of the purposes behind standing limits, with a flexible and generous approach rather than a checklist. It did not narrow the test to federal constitutional challenges. It also did not require proof that no directly affected person exists, since that would turn one factor into an absolute bar.
  8. The correct answer is C (The statute authorizes this panel makeup, so a common law bias argument resting on it fails): When the legislature expressly prescribes a panel's composition, the common law cannot be used to invalidate that structure absent a constitutional or quasi-constitutional limit. The farm's remaining recourse would be an allegation about how particular members behaved, which it does not make. A mandatory 'shall' is not read as merely directory to defeat its own purpose.
  9. The correct answer is C (On the whole record, sustained hostility may ground apprehension, though firm questioning alone would not): Conduct at a hearing is assessed in context and over the whole record, because one blunt question proves little while a pattern of derision can show a closed mind. Tribunals may question vigorously, so firmness is not enough. Bias can be inferred from demeanour and need not be confessed in the reasons.
  10. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness, where the statute's text and its limits on authority strongly constrain the decision): After Vavilov, a decision-maker's statutory authority is a legal constraint within reasonableness review, so no separate jurisdictional category or correctness standard is needed. An order the Act does not authorise cannot be justified in light of the governing legal constraints and will fail on reasonableness. The trader succeeds on the merits without the old label.
  11. The correct answer is B (No, because reliability findings are factual and fall outside an appeal limited to law): An appeal restricted to questions of law gives the court no authority to reassess how a tribunal weighed evidence. Palpable and overriding error is a standard for appeals that include fact, which this one does not. Only an error of law, such as applying a wrong legal test to the evidence, would open the finding to challenge.
  12. The correct answer is A (Prohibition, to stop the panel proceeding while the apprehension of bias remains unanswered): Prohibition prevents a tribunal from proceeding or deciding and suits a case where no decision has yet been made and bias is raised. Certiorari works on a decision already made, so it is premature at this stage. Mandamus would dictate how the municipality constitutes the panel, and quo warranto concerns entitlement to office rather than a risk of biased adjudication.
  13. The correct answer is B (Section 7 is engaged, since state removal of a child seriously impairs a parent's psychological integrity): The Supreme Court has held that state interference with the parent-child relationship through child protection proceedings seriously affects the parent's psychological integrity, and so engages security of the person. Section 7 is not confined to physical confinement, and a proceeding concerning the child's welfare still directly burdens the parent. State action in child welfare is subject to the Charter.
  14. The correct answer is B (As permissive, leaving a discretion still bound by reasonableness and the statute's purpose): Under federal interpretation legislation "may" is permissive, so Dawn cannot compel a grant just by satisfying the conditions. Permissive does not mean unreviewable, though: discretion must be exercised reasonably and for statutory purposes, and the absence of reasons is a ground of review rather than a reason to rewrite the word.
  15. The correct answer is C (No appeal lies unless the Federal Court judge certifies a serious question of general importance): The immigration statute restricts appeals on these judicial reviews to cases where the judge certifies a serious question of general importance. The general appeal as of right under the Federal Courts Act is displaced. Leave from the appeal court cannot substitute for certification.
Case CHB4

Extract, Rent Stabilization Act, s 52: A party may appeal a decision of the Rent Review Board to the Divisional Court on a question of law only. Brightwater Properties applied to raise rents because of capital expenditure on a new roof. The Board refused, ruling that 'capital expenditure' in s 12 requires an asset life of at least twenty years, a threshold found nowhere in the Act. It also found, on invoices, that the roof cost $180,000, not the $240,000 claimed.

  1. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because it is an interpretation of the statute raised on a law-only appeal): The twenty-year rule is a general statement of what the Act's words mean, so it is a pure question of law. Section 52 sends the court to appellate standards, and correctness governs legal questions on appeal. Expertise in rent regulation does not move this question into the deferential category.
  2. The correct answer is A (No, because a law-only appeal excludes findings of fact unless they rest on a legal error): Section 52 confines the appeal to questions of law, so the court cannot reweigh the invoices or substitute its own cost figure. A factual finding becomes open only if it was produced by a legal error that can be identified and separated out. Brightwater points to no such error in the cost finding itself.
  3. The correct answer is B (Allow the appeal and remit to the Board to reconsider the expenditure properly): Once the legal error is corrected, the open questions of qualification and recovery are fact-dependent matters that the legislature assigned to the Board. Remitting respects that allocation, while a court substituting its own figure would overstep a law-only appeal. A reasonableness-range argument has no place because correctness governs the legal issue and the error is not harmless.
  4. The correct answer is D (Strike it, because the court assesses the reasons actually given and the record, not after-the-fact explanations): Reasonableness review looks at the decision as made and its reasons; a decision-maker cannot repair a gap by later testimony about her thought process. Such an affidavit would also intrude on adjudicative deliberative confidentiality. Treating it as a mere clarification or assigning it less weight still lets the tribunal supplement its reasons.
  5. The correct answer is B (Prerogative powers are reviewable for constitutional compliance, though courts defer considerably in foreign affairs): Prerogative powers remain subject to the Constitution, so a court can review their exercise for Charter compliance, though the Supreme Court has recognized wide deference to the executive in foreign affairs and limits remedies accordingly. The argument that the power comes from the Crown rather than Parliament is outdated, and review is not limited to administrative law grounds. Courts do not substitute their own foreign policy judgment for the executive's.
  6. The correct answer is B (Only Naomi's claim may meet the threshold, since her harm is profound, not ordinary stress): The threshold asks for state-imposed harm that is serious and profound, and a parent separated from an infant by state action with a documented decline is of that character. Marcus describes humiliation and lost sleep, which are the ordinary consequences of a public finding. Physical injury is not required, and deliberateness of the state act does not replace the seriousness requirement.
  7. The correct answer is A (No general right to funded counsel exists; fairness requires only a chance to present her case): Canadian law recognizes no general right to state-funded counsel before administrative bodies, and section 7 does not protect purely economic interests such as a licence. A simple dispute can be fairly presented by the applicant herself. Representation by the other side does not by itself create a right to funded counsel.
Case CHB5

Extract from the fictional Land Titles Review Act, s 40: A party to a decision of the Land Titles Tribunal may appeal to the Court of Appeal, with leave, on a question of law or jurisdiction. Pemberton Farms Ltd applied to register an easement over a neighbour's field. The Tribunal found as fact that the easement deed was unsigned by one owner. It also read s 12 of the Act as barring registration of any easement not signed by both owners. Pemberton obtained leave and now appeals.

  1. The correct answer is A (It rebuts the presumption of reasonableness, so the court applies the appellate standards): Under Vavilov a statutory right of appeal to a court signals that the legislature wants appellate review, so the court applies the Housen standards rather than reasonableness. Leave is only a gateway and does not alter this. Correctness is not applied to everything, since facts attract deference.
  2. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because the reading of s 12 is a question of law under the appellate standard): Housen assigns questions of law to correctness, and the appeal provision makes that framework the one that governs. Palpable and overriding error is for facts and for mixed questions without an extricable legal error. Central importance is not a precondition to correctness on a statutory appeal.
  3. The correct answer is B (The appeal covers only law or jurisdiction, so a purely factual challenge is outside it): Section 40 confines the appeal to questions of law or jurisdiction, so a pure factual challenge is outside it. Palpable and overriding error is the Housen standard only where facts are appealable at all, which this appeal does not allow. Reasonableness is a judicial review concept and is displaced by the appeal scheme.
  4. The correct answer is C (Yes, as general background that helps the court understand the issues, not the merits): Courts recognize an exception for neutral background that orients the reviewing judge. It cannot be used to supplement the evidence on the merits. Adding an opinion on the conclusion would take the affidavit outside the exception.
  5. The correct answer is C (An order targeting one named company is not general legislation, so a fairness duty can arise): The legislative versus administrative line turns on the substance and target of the decision, not the form of the instrument. A decision that singles out one named company on the basis of submissions about its conduct resembles an administrative decision about an individual, so the Baker factors can apply. That does not mean a full oral hearing: the content of the duty depends on context, and notice of the case with a chance to respond may suffice.
Case CHB6

Extract, Hospital Privileges Appeals Act (fictional): s 6(1) An appeal shall be heard and decided by a panel of three members. s 6(2) A panel member who does not attend the whole hearing shall not take part in the decision. Dr. Reyes appealed the revocation of her surgical privileges. Over four days, the panel of Members Singh, Cho and Alder heard testimony. Alder missed the final day, when the hospital's chief of surgery testified on the key conduct allegations. All three signed the decision, which upheld the revocation.

  1. The correct answer is B (The decision is invalid because Alder took part despite missing the final day's testimony): The extract requires a panel of three and forbids a member who missed part of the hearing from taking part. Alder did both things the statute forbids, so the decision was not made by a validly constituted panel. It does not help that two members heard everything, since the statute does not allow a majority to carry a tainted decision.
  2. The correct answer is A (It fails, since the statute bars participation outright and transcript review cannot cure the breach): Where the legislature has expressly prohibited participation by a member who missed part of the hearing, the court does not ask whether the member's presence actually changed the result. Transcript review and unanimity are the kind of after-the-fact reassurance the prohibition rules out, because no one can know how a tainted member shaped the deliberations.
  3. The correct answer is A (Whoever hears the evidence must decide, so a member missing key testimony generally cannot take part): Even without the express prohibition, the common law rule that the persons who hear a case must decide it would bar Alder from joining the decision. Consent could change the result only if both parties agreed, not just the hospital. Briefing from the chair is no substitute for hearing the evidence directly.
  4. The correct answer is B (Yes, because a federal board includes bodies exercising powers conferred by a prerogative order): The statutory definition expressly covers jurisdiction or powers conferred by an order made under a prerogative of the Crown, so the passport officer qualifies and the Federal Court hears the case. Modern law does not shield prerogative powers from review simply because of their source. Passport officers are not among the bodies listed in s 28(1).
  5. The correct answer is C (Adequacy is judged within reasonableness review, by asking whether reasons and record justify the outcome): Vavilov treats reasons as the main way a decision is justified, so a shortfall is assessed inside reasonableness review and not as a separate ground. The court reads the reasons together with the record but may not write better reasons itself. Treating brevity as a fairness breach reviewed for correctness is the tempting error here.
  6. The correct answer is B (Refusal likely breaches fairness, since her licence is at stake in a serious proceeding): There is no automatic right to counsel, but where a professional licence is at stake and the allegations are serious, fairness will usually permit counsel. A silent statute does not remove the common law duty. Section 7 is not engaged in the blanket way the last proposition claims, since licence loss alone is not a security of the person interest.
  7. The correct answer is B (Disclosure was owed, since the memo assessed his facts and urged an outcome): Tribunal members may consult staff or colleagues on questions of law and policy, but a memo that evaluates the claimant's own facts and recommends an outcome goes beyond that and should be disclosed so he can respond. The privilege argument is weak because the tribunal is not his lawyer's client. Disclosure is not owed for every internal document, so the facts-and-outcome content decides it.
  8. The correct answer is D (It must be consistent with text, context and purpose, though no formal court-style analysis is required): Vavilov states that the governing statute is a fundamental constraint, so the interpretation must be consistent with its text, context and purpose. A decision maker need not conduct a court-style exercise, but it must show it grappled with the statutory language. Reviewing the meaning for correctness would revive the pre-Vavilov category approach.
  9. The correct answer is C (Decision's nature, statutory scheme, importance to the individual, legitimate expectations, and the agency's procedural choices): Baker lists five non-exhaustive factors that fix the content of procedural fairness: the nature of the decision and process, the statutory scheme, the importance to the individual, legitimate expectations and the agency's own procedural choices. The judicial-or-administrative label belongs to the older classification approach. The other lists describe discretionary remedy factors and the old standard-of-review inquiry.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because wide discretion must still serve the statute's purposes and a donation history is extraneous): Broad discretionary wording does not free a decision-maker from the statute's purposes, as Roncarelli and Baker confirm. Reasonableness review under Vavilov asks whether the decision is justified in light of the legal constraints, and the Minister's stated basis is extraneous to licensing. Proof of bad faith is not a separate requirement, and a fairness complaint is a different inquiry.
  2. The correct answer is B (To ask whether the balancing is justified, transparent and intelligible, without reweighing the factors): On reasonableness review the court examines the reasons and result for justification and does not reweigh the factors or substitute its own balance. It does more than check a list of mentioned factors, and proportionality analysis applies where a decision engages Charter protections, not to all statutory balancing.
  3. The correct answer is B (Under Housen, for palpable and overriding error, because factual findings attract appellate deference): On an ordinary appeal from a court, Housen sets the standards: correctness for questions of law and palpable and overriding error for findings of fact and inferences of fact. A misreading of logs is a factual complaint. Vavilov and Dunsmuir address review of administrative decisions, not appeals from trial judges.
  4. The correct answer is D (It is a mandatory statutory precondition, so the regulation may be declared invalid for noncompliance): A statutory consultation requirement is a legislative condition on the power to make the regulation, so its breach is a question of statutory interpretation rather than common law fairness. The word 'shall' and the purpose of meaningful public comment point toward a mandatory precondition, so the regulation may be invalid. Vavilov reasonableness review addresses the merits and cannot cure a failure to meet a precondition to the power.
  5. The correct answer is A (The court assesses fairness itself in context, owing no deference to the board's view): Procedural fairness is not reviewed for reasonableness. The court asks whether the process met the level of fairness required in the circumstances, with the Baker factors informing content, and it owes the decision-maker no deference on that question. The patent unreasonableness approach and jurisdictional labels belong to the older framework.
  6. The correct answer is A (Whether its reading of the conditions power is justified by text, context, purpose and burden imposed): After Vavilov the scope of a delegated power is reviewed for reasonableness, asking whether the regulator's interpretation is justified by the statutory text, context and purpose. Where a decision places a heavy burden on the affected party, the justification must be correspondingly strong. The label jurisdictional error no longer triggers correctness, and statutory silence does not by itself grant a power.
  7. The correct answer is B (A direct financial interest in the outcome disqualifies her automatically, without any proof of actual bias): A direct and material financial stake in the result disqualifies a decision-maker automatically, because the law presumes such an interest taints the decision whether or not actual bias is shown. A smaller or remote holding would instead be tested under the reasonable apprehension standard. Disclosure alone does not cure the defect when the objector has not agreed to her sitting.
  8. The correct answer is A (A serious issue to be tried, irreparable harm without relief, and the balance of convenience): Applications to stop conduct pending a final decision follow the RJR-MacDonald framework: a serious issue, irreparable harm and the balance of convenience. The stronger prima facie standard applies only to mandatory interlocutory injunctions that compel action, and this order merely prohibits clearing. Proven title is not a precondition, because the duty to consult protects asserted rights.
  9. The correct answer is D (The interpretation was not justified in light of the text, context and purpose of the provision): After Vavilov, a court reviewing an interpretation asks whether the decision maker justified its reading by engaging with the text, context and purpose of the provision. Jurisdictional error and patent unreasonableness are retired labels, and a finding based on the court's own preferred reading belongs to correctness review rather than reasonableness.
  10. The correct answer is B (Unreasonable, because broad discretion must stay within the statute's purpose and cannot rest on political reprisal): Discretion is always constrained by the statute that grants it, and a reasonable exercise must be consistent with the statute's purpose. Penalizing a family for its political choices has nothing to do with the public interest in food-truck regulation, a point made famously in Roncarelli v Duplessis. Wide wording and the mere giving of reasons do not rescue a refusal based on an irrelevant, improper consideration.
  11. The correct answer is A (The officer's notes can serve as the reasons if they disclose the basis for the decision): Baker held that the officer's notes, supplied when reasons were requested, constituted the reasons for decision, since fairness looks to substance and not to form. Reasons were required there because of the importance of the decision to the applicant. A discretionary label does not remove procedural protection.
Case CHB7

Extract, Ontario Ferry Operators Act (fictional): 8(1) The Registrar shall give an operator written notice of any proposed suspension and 30 days to respond in writing. 8(2) The Registrar may determine the procedure for any further inquiry. The Act does not mention hearings. Marcus Dyer's notice said only that a complaint alleged unsafe docking. He responded in writing. The Registrar then suspended his licence without showing him the complainant's statement or holding a hearing.

  1. The correct answer is D (The express notice and response provisions in the Act, which set the minimum process owed): The enabling statute is the first source, and its notice and response requirements set the minimum process. The common law supplements rather than outranks it. The Statutory Powers Procedure Act is not a universal code, and no unwritten principle overrides clear statutory procedure on these facts.
  2. The correct answer is D (No, because the Act does not require a hearing, so that Act is not engaged): The Statutory Powers Procedure Act applies only where a law requires a hearing or an opportunity for one. Neither the written-response scheme nor the discretion over further inquiry creates that requirement. A single decision-maker can be a tribunal, so that is not why the Act is inapplicable.
  3. The correct answer is A (Yes, the common law supplements the silent statute, so disclosure of the case is required): The statute says nothing about disclosure, and a suspension of a licence is a serious decision, so Baker calls for knowing the case to be met. A general power over procedure does not clearly oust the common law. A written response means little when the operator was never told the complaint's details, and no prior request is required.
  4. The correct answer is B (Delay abuses process only if inordinate and so prejudicial that it undermines fairness or public confidence): Blencoe holds that delay supports a stay only where it is inordinate and directly causes significant prejudice, either impairing a fair hearing or making the proceeding so unfair that it brings the system into disrepute. Stress and stigma alone are ordinary consequences of facing a complaint and do not meet that standard. Nor does delay by itself, or a missed guideline, remove the tribunal's authority.
Case CHB8

Extract, Immigration Act, s 25.1: 'The Minister may grant permanent residence to a foreign national where humanitarian and compassionate considerations justify it, taking into account the best interests of any child directly affected.' Adaeze Nwosu has lived in Halifax for nine years. Her son Chidi, aged seven, was born in Canada and receives treatment for a speech impairment at a local clinic. A psychologist's report in the record says comparable treatment is unavailable in her home country. The officer refused her application, writing that 'the child's interests are outweighed by immigration objectives', and did not mention the report.

  1. The correct answer is B (Where the impact is severe, the reasons must reflect those stakes and justify the result): Vavilov explains that the more harshly a decision affects an individual's life, the more the reasons must demonstrate that the decision maker grappled with those consequences. This is a feature of reasonableness review itself, not a trigger for a new standard. Reweighing the interests would replace the officer's role with the court's.
  2. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because the statute requires meaningful consideration of the child's interests, not a bare conclusion): The extract tells the officer to take the child's interests into account, and Baker requires that they be considered with attention and sensitivity. Announcing that they are outweighed, with no analysis of what they are, does not show that the factor was weighed. The extract does not, however, make the child's interests decisive.
  3. The correct answer is A (Unreasonable, because the report contradicts the finding and its omission suggests the evidence was disregarded): A decision maker is generally presumed to have considered the whole record, but the presumption weakens when unmentioned evidence directly contradicts a stated finding. A finding of no evidence in the face of a specific report shows the decision may rest on a fundamental misapprehension of the record. The court reaches that view without reweighing the report itself.
  4. The correct answer is A (It cannot compel renewal, but entitles Northridge to notice and a chance to respond): Canadian law treats legitimate expectations as procedural only, so a promise of a result does not bind the new minister to deliver it. It can, however, require fairness before the promise is reversed, which is the strongest available argument. The English substantive approach is not Canadian law, and the fettering objection ignores the procedural protection.
  5. The correct answer is B (Credibility concerns must be put to them to answer, though a written exchange may suffice): Where an officer's concern goes to the genuineness of the applicants' account, fairness generally requires giving them a chance to respond to it. That does not necessarily mean an interview, since a written fairness letter can do the job. Having the burden of proof does not remove the duty, and a general form warning is not a specific opportunity to address the discrepancies.
  6. The correct answer is D (Statutory permission does not displace fairness, which can require reasons given the decision's significant impact): Common law fairness operates unless the legislature clearly displaces it, and permissive wording about reasons does not do so. The impact on Leon's housing and income supports a duty to give reasons. The old distinction between rights and privileges no longer limits fairness.
  7. The correct answer is D (Permissible if discussion stays on law and policy and each member decides independently): Full board consultation on issues of law and policy supports consistency and is allowed, provided it does not involve untested evidence and does not coerce a member's decision. Here the evidence was not discussed and members stayed free to disagree, and no express statutory authority is needed.
Case CHB9

The Harbourview Residents Coalition, a registered non-profit with 300 members and a record of advocacy on housing, challenges a provincial regulation that bars anyone without a fixed address from receiving social assistance. The affected people are homeless, frequently move, and lack money or legal help. A single homeless man, Rennie, has said he will not sue for fear of losing his shelter bed. The Attorney General argues the Coalition has no direct interest and should be refused standing.

  1. The correct answer is A (A flexible, purposive assessment of a serious justiciable issue, genuine interest and a reasonable, effective means): Downtown Eastside confirmed that public interest standing turns on a serious justiciable issue, a genuine interest, and whether the suit is a reasonable and effective means, weighed flexibly and purposively. A personal-rights requirement belongs to private interest standing. Charter personal-infringement rules and rigid substitution tests would defeat the purpose of public interest standing.
  2. The correct answer is A (They support the Coalition: vulnerable persons are unlikely to sue, so the law could evade review): The third factor asks whether the suit is a reasonable and effective way to put the issue before the court, and courts consider whether those directly affected are realistically able to sue. Vulnerability, mobility and lack of resources make that unlikely. Waiting for an individual plaintiff would leave the regulation unreviewed, and genuine interest is shown by the Coalition's own record rather than by a request from those affected.
  3. The correct answer is B (Grant public interest standing, since all three factors favour hearing the challenge on its merits): The issue is serious, the Coalition's record shows a genuine interest, and affected persons are unlikely to sue, so each factor favours standing. Downtown Eastside warns against a rigid approach that would shield legislation from review. Requiring a personal right, demanding Rennie's consent, or deferring the question misreads the flexible, purposive approach.
  4. The correct answer is A (The court may refuse relief in its discretion, given unexplained delay and prejudice to innocent buyers): Relief on judicial review is discretionary, and a court may refuse it for unexplained delay, particularly where third parties have relied on the decision and would be prejudiced. A procedural defect does not oblige the court to quash. Refusing relief because the outcome was inevitable is a separate ground and is not the only one.
  5. The correct answer is B (It cannot appeal, because the Act gives the right to parties, which excludes interveners): Statutory appeal rights belong to the persons the statute names. Participation as an intervener does not make the group a party under this definition. Public interest standing is a judicial review concept and cannot enlarge a statutory right.
  6. The correct answer is A (Read the words in context, harmoniously with the Act's scheme, object and legislative intention): Canadian courts and tribunals follow the modern principle: text, context and purpose are read together, and the words take their ordinary sense in harmony with the scheme of the Act. Strict construction of licensing powers and reliance on foreign or customary meanings displace that approach, and the registrar's practice cannot fix the meaning of the words.
  7. The correct answer is B (Section 7 is not engaged, because ordinary stress and stigma are not serious state-imposed psychological harm): In Blencoe the Supreme Court held that section 7 reaches administrative proceedings, but that security of the person requires serious, state-imposed psychological harm. The stress and stigma ordinarily attending a human rights complaint fall short of that threshold, and delay alone does not engage the section. It is wrong to say section 7 is confined to criminal matters.
  8. The correct answer is A (It should decline review, because the statutory appeal is an adequate remedy that covers fairness): Judicial review is discretionary, and courts ordinarily decline to hear it where an adequate alternative remedy such as a statutory appeal exists. Here the appeal expressly covers fairness, so it can fully address Farid's complaint. The court retains jurisdiction, so dismissal for want of jurisdiction is wrong, and a fairness breach is not exempt from the rule.
  9. The correct answer is A (Where the investigation effectively decides the complaint, its subject must know the substance of the evidence): When the investigator's conclusion in practice disposes of the complaint, the complainant is entitled to the substance of the case against it and a chance to respond. Treating the officer as a mere fact-collector ignores that no later hearing will occur. Disclosure does not depend on the union first proving falsity.
  10. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because a statutory appeal on a question of law attracts appellate standards): Under Vavilov, a legislated appeal on a question of law sends the court to the appellate standard of correctness for legal questions, including the meaning of the tribunal's own statute. The presumption of reasonableness gives way to that signal. The mixed-question standard would apply only to applying settled law to facts, not to fixing the meaning of the definition.
  11. The correct answer is C (It may refuse relief because unexplained delay that prejudiced the employer can outweigh the error): Remedies on judicial review are discretionary, and a court may withhold relief even after finding an error. Unexplained delay combined with prejudice to others who relied on the order is a recognized ground for refusal. The tempting view that a proven error carries relief as of right fails because the applicant's own conduct and the third-party reliance weigh against it here.
Case CHB10

A provincial College of Dental Hygienists discipline panel heard allegations that Daniel Fortin, a hygienist, billed for services not performed. Daniel received notice of the hearing three days earlier. On the hearing date he asked for an adjournment to retain counsel, which the panel refused. The panel had before it an investigator's report that was never given to Daniel. It then found misconduct and revoked his registration in a one-sentence decision: "The panel finds the allegations proven and orders revocation." The statute gives a right of appeal to the Court of Appeal on questions of law only.

  1. The correct answer is D (A person facing revocation must know the case to meet, including material the panel relied on): The right to know the case to be met is central to fairness, and it extends to material the decision-maker actually relies on. Revocation of registration is a very serious consequence, which raises the content required. Privilege or a formal request requirement does not justify withholding a report the panel used to decide against him.
  2. The correct answer is A (Reasons were required given the severe impact, and a bare conclusion does not explain the decision): Where a decision has a profound effect on a person's livelihood, as revocation of registration does, fairness requires written reasons that explain the result, as Baker held and Vavilov also recognizes for decisions with harsh consequences. A statement of the outcome alone leaves Daniel and any reviewing court unable to see why the panel decided as it did. Statutory silence does not remove the duty.
  3. The correct answer is B (A law-only appeal cannot cure serious breaches that deprived him of a fair first hearing): A statutory appeal can cure an earlier breach only where it offers a full rehearing with adequate remedial power, which a law-only appeal on the record does not. Daniel lost the chance to know the case, obtain counsel and receive reasons at first instance, and those losses are not errors a law-only appeal reaches. The existence of an appeal bears on whether judicial review should proceed, but it does not decide the question.
  4. The correct answer is D (No stay: delay she caused or accepted weighs against her, and no prejudice to fairness appears): Abrametz directs the tribunal to look at the causes of delay, including delay the applicant caused or waived, when deciding if it is inordinate. With intact evidence and a self-inflicted portion of the wait, there is neither inordinate delay nor significant prejudice. Reputational harm by itself does not meet the Blencoe threshold.
  5. The correct answer is B (Review for reasonableness, asking whether the reading is justified by text, context and purpose): Under Vavilov the presumption is reasonableness, and a tribunal interpreting its home statute is not held to the court's preferred answer. The central-importance category does not fit an issue confined to one scheme, and the remedial purpose is something the tribunal must justify, not a licence for the most generous reading.
  6. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness for the meaning of resident, and correctness for the question about federal power): The meaning of a term in the board's own bylaw falls under the presumption of reasonableness. Whether the bylaw respects the division of powers is a constitutional question and is reviewed for correctness. The constitutional exception applies issue by issue, so it does not spill over into the interpretive ruling.
  7. The correct answer is A (As an extricable question of law reviewed for correctness, since it concerns the legal test): Housen separates an extricable legal error, such as stating the wrong test, from the application of a correct test to the facts. The first is a question of law reviewed for correctness and is properly raised on a law-only appeal. The owner is not attacking how the facts were weighed but which test governed.
Case CHB11

Extract, Travel Security Act, s 8: 'The Minister may cancel a passport where satisfied on reasonable grounds that the holder poses a risk to national security.' Section 9: 'The Minister may rely on information that would injure national security if disclosed, and shall give the holder a summary of that information that does not itself injure national security.' Mr. Haddad's passport was cancelled. His summary says only that he 'associated with persons of concern'.

  1. The correct answer is D (They may limit disclosure, but he must still receive enough to know and answer the case): National security is a recognised reason to withhold some information, but the individual must still be given the gist of the case so a response is possible. Courts continue to supervise the fairness of such processes. A subjective statutory test affects the substance of the decision, not the existence of procedural protections.
  2. The correct answer is D (A bare conclusion without facts denies him the gist of the case, so he cannot respond): Section 9 requires a summary, and fairness gives that word content: it must tell him enough to answer. A label with no names, dates or conduct leaves nothing to contest. The statute's wording does not oust the common law, and the Minister's view of harm does not decide whether the summary meets the minimum.
  3. The correct answer is C (The court decides whether the process was fair in context, taking security concerns into account): Vavilov's standards of review concern the substance of administrative decisions, while the court itself asks whether the procedure met the level of fairness required in the particular context. Security concerns shape what fairness requires but do not give the Minister the final word. A privative clause does not transfer that question to the decision-maker.
  4. The correct answer is D (Cabinet owed little procedural content for a policy decision, since the company was already heard below): Cabinet's power to vary a regulator's order is a broad policy power, and in Inuit Tapirisat the Court held that such a petition carries little procedural entitlement, particularly where the petitioner was fully heard by the regulator. Baker's reasons requirement and the Knight content respond to individual rights decisions, not policy choices of this kind. The claim that cabinet is wholly immune from review goes too far, because its decisions remain reviewable for legality and reasonableness.
Case CHB12

Mr Ian Kowalchuk received provincial disability benefits until the Social Benefits Tribunal terminated them. Two medical reports were before it: his treating physician said he could not work; the government's physician said he could. The tribunal preferred the government report, calling the treating physician's report "not credible" with no explanation. It also never mentioned an uncontested vocational assessment finding no suitable jobs available. He seeks judicial review.

  1. The correct answer is C (No, because the court does not reweigh or reassess evidence absent exceptional circumstances): Vavilov directs reviewing courts not to reweigh or reassess the evidence considered by the decision-maker, absent exceptional circumstances. Choosing between competing medical reports is a core tribunal function. Patent unreasonableness is a pre-Vavilov standard, so the no-reweighing rule does not rest on it.
  2. The correct answer is A (Yes, if the omission shows the tribunal misapprehended or failed to account for central evidence): Although the court will not reweigh evidence, a decision may be unreasonable where the decision-maker fundamentally misapprehended the evidence or failed to account for evidence that is central to the outcome. An unexplained omission of uncontested evidence that bears directly on the issue can show that. Not every omission qualifies, so the blanket error-of-law view overshoots.
  3. The correct answer is C (Set the decision aside and remit it to the tribunal for redetermination with the court's reasons): The usual remedy is to remit the matter so the legislatively chosen decision-maker can decide again. A court substitutes its own decision only where the outcome is inevitable, which is not so here because the two medical reports conflict. Directing the tribunal to prefer one report would amount to the reweighing the court has already refused to do.
  4. The correct answer is A (The Attorney General of Canada, or anyone directly affected by the matter in issue): Section 18.1(1) names two groups: the Attorney General of Canada and anyone directly affected by the matter for which relief is sought. It does not require participation in the original proceeding or a prior complaint. Broader public interest standing exists as a separate discretionary doctrine and is not what the section itself authorizes.
  5. The correct answer is C (Not reviewable, because the corporation acted under contract and not under a statutory public power): The question is whether the particular decision exercised a power conferred by statute or prerogative. A termination under a commercial clause is the exercise of contractual rights. Origin in an Act of Parliament does not decide it, and Crown corporations are not excluded as a class, so the analysis turns on the nature of the decision.
  6. The correct answer is D (Disclosure must come early enough to allow a real response, so an adjournment was warranted): Disclosure must be timely enough that the person can understand and answer the material, and a long technical report handed over minutes before the hearing does not allow that. An adjournment was the natural remedy. Cross-examination on the day is no substitute for time to prepare, and the old patent unreasonableness standard does not govern a fairness question.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (A full hearing de novo on appeal can cure the earlier unfairness, so review likely fails): Where an appeal is a true rehearing with full disclosure and an opportunity to be heard, an earlier procedural defect can be cured, as Harelkin illustrates, and the court may also refuse relief in its discretion. The result turns on the adequacy of the appeal, not on a finding by the Review Board that the first stage was unfair. A denial of natural justice otherwise generally leaves the original decision open to being set aside, so the cure depends on the appeal being a full fresh hearing.
  2. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because privilege's scope is a question of central importance to the legal system): Vavilov lists general questions of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole as a correctness category, and the scope of solicitor-client privilege is its standard illustration. Frequency before the commissioner and expertise do not displace that. The boundary category concerns overlapping authority of two administrative bodies, not a body and a claimant.
  3. The correct answer is C (Thirty days from the initial notice of the decision, extendable by a judge for proper reasons): Section 18.1(2) of the Federal Courts Act sets thirty days from the time the decision was first communicated, and a judge may extend that period. The clock is not tied to written reasons, and the board has no power to extend it. A six month period belongs to some provincial regimes, not to the federal statute.
  4. The correct answer is C (Fairness is met if the company knows the substance of the case and can answer it): Confidentiality of a source can justify withholding identity where the party still receives the substance of the allegation and a real chance to respond. Disclosing the dates and photographs lets the company test the allegation. Withholding the content as well would leave it unable to answer, and cross-examination is not a universal requirement.
  5. The correct answer is D (The tribunal files the record of proceeding, including notices, filed exhibits, any transcript and its reasons): In Ontario the tribunal whose decision is under review files in court the record of the proceeding, which includes the originating documents, notices, exhibits, any transcript and the decision with reasons. It is not compiled by the applicant, and it does not extend to internal deliberations. Limiting it to the decision alone would leave the court without the evidence the tribunal had.
  6. The correct answer is D (The regulation exceeds the authority delegated by the enabling Act and so is invalid): A regulation is valid only if it falls within the statutory grant, and a ban on sale for environmental reasons goes beyond a power limited to safe packaging. There is generally no duty of fairness in making regulations of general application. Vavilov's reasons requirement does not apply to subordinate legislation, and s 7 does not protect purely commercial interests.
  7. The correct answer is C (Expertise does not displace the legislature's choice of an appeal, so the appellate standards apply): Vavilov treats a statutory appeal to a court as a clear indication that appellate standards govern, whatever the tribunal's expertise. The contextual weighing no longer exists. Facts are still reviewed deferentially for palpable and overriding error, so correctness does not apply to everything.
  8. The correct answer is A (Expertise no longer chooses the standard, though it still informs the reasonableness analysis itself): Vavilov grounds the presumption of reasonableness in the legislature's choice to delegate decision-making, not in the decision-maker's specialized expertise, so expertise no longer picks the standard. Expertise still matters inside reasonableness review, for example in judging whether the reasons reflect the practical realities of the field. Counsel is wrong about the basis, and the applicant is wrong that it has no role.
  9. The correct answer is C (Two applications: Federal Court for the federal refusal, Divisional Court for the provincial refusal): Forum follows the source of each decision-maker's power. The federal officer falls within the Federal Court's exclusive jurisdiction and the provincial Ministry within the provincial superior court's. Convenience cannot enlarge either court's jurisdiction, and a shared factual background does not create a combined forum, however tempting one filing may be.
  10. The correct answer is D (The clause describes the tribunal's mandate, not a prescribed standard, so the presumption of reasonableness stands): A provision conferring authority to decide questions of law and fact simply describes the decision-maker's role, which is already the basis for the presumption of reasonableness. A standard is prescribed only when the legislature says how the court must review. The claimant's reading would turn every mandate clause into a correctness mandate.
  11. The correct answer is C (An informed person could reasonably apprehend a lack of independence, since renewal rests with a litigant): With no statutory answer, the common law test asks how a reasonably informed person would see the arrangement. Short terms renewable at the pleasure of a frequent litigant before the same board create a strong appearance that outcomes may affect renewal. Valente is not the automatic measure for tribunals, and a challenge to institutional independence does not wait for a single adverse ruling.
Case CHB13

Chen Wei applied to renew his work permit. An immigration officer refused renewal after relying on an internal memo, never shown to him, alleging that his employer was under investigation for document fraud. Mr. Chen requested his file under the Access to Information Act and received the officer's notes with the passages describing the memo blacked out, because the Act exempts information whose release could injure an ongoing investigation. He seeks judicial review of the refusal.

  1. The correct answer is A (Adverse extrinsic evidence unknown to the applicant must ordinarily be disclosed so he can respond): The case to meet includes extrinsic evidence the decision-maker relies on and the applicant could not be expected to know, because without it he cannot correct or answer it. The duty varies with context, but even a low-content duty requires the applicant to know the adverse material. Limiting disclosure to cases where the memo was the sole ground ignores that any material reliance is enough.
  2. The correct answer is C (The exemption governs access requests, not fairness, which still demands the gist of the case): The access to information scheme is a statutory mechanism for requesting records, and its exemptions do not purport to define what fairness requires in a decision about the requester. Where investigation confidentiality is a real concern, a decision-maker can often satisfy fairness by disclosing a summary or the gist of the adverse allegation. Treating the two regimes as one test would let an exemption written for access requests decide a common law question about the right to answer the case.
  3. The correct answer is C (They can stand as the reasons for the decision, and the court reviews them as such): Baker accepted an officer's notes as the reasons for a decision, and Vavilov likewise directs reviewing courts to read the reasons actually given, including informal ones, rather than demanding a polished document. No separate certification is needed. Courts review the notes themselves and do not invite the Minister to supply new reasons after the fact.
  4. The correct answer is A (Unreasonable, because it ignores the children's best interests, which are central to the humanitarian purpose): Baker holds that the interests of affected children must be well identified and examined with attention when a humanitarian decision is made. Unimplemented treaties inform the analysis but do not bind directly. Silence on the children shows the decision does not respond to the stakes for the family.
Case CHB14

Extract, Port Alder Vehicle-for-Hire By-law 14, s 9: The Licence Committee may revoke a licence where the holder has engaged in conduct that threatens public safety. Gurpreet Dhillon's taxi licence was revoked after a hearing. The Committee relied on an investigator's report about unpaid parking tickets and late meter inspections, which it never gave him. In its reasons it said only that his 'pattern of non-compliance' threatened public safety. No appeal lies from the Committee.

  1. The correct answer is B (Decide for itself whether disclosure was needed for a fair hearing, applying the Baker factors): Non-disclosure of material relied on goes to procedural fairness, which the court assesses itself in light of the interest at stake, here a livelihood. The Vavilov standard-of-review analysis applies to the merits, not to the fairness of the process. Fairness is normally decided first because a breach can end the matter.
  2. The correct answer is D (The decision is set aside and returned for a new hearing, without a ruling on merits): A decision reached in breach of the duty of fairness is ordinarily set aside, and the matter returns to the decision-maker for a fair hearing. The court does not examine the merits when the process was defective. Fixing disclosure after the fact would deprive Mr Dhillon of the opportunity to respond before the decision.
  3. The correct answer is A (The reasons do not explain how unpaid tickets and late inspections threaten safety under the by-law): Reasonableness review under Vavilov asks whether the decision is justified, transparent and intelligible in light of the statutory text and purpose. A bare conclusion that non-compliance threatens safety leaves a gap in the chain of reasoning. Correctness, an expertise argument and a jurisdictional label are not the applicable framework.
  4. The correct answer is B (The appeal may be inadequate if the original decision-makers hear it, so the court may proceed): Under Harelkin and Strickland the court weighs whether the alternative remedy is adequate, and an appeal heard by the very people accused of bias may not offer a fair or effective remedy. That makes review available in discretion, but the allegation does not remove the discretion. Expertise and the committee's ability to consider bias are factors, not rules requiring her to appeal first.
  5. The correct answer is D (No free-standing fairness right to funding exists; the refusal is a statutory discretion reviewed for reasonableness): Participant funding is created by statute or policy, not by the common law duty of fairness, so the question is whether the Board reasonably applied its own criteria under Vavilov. A discretionary program is still reviewable, which rules out immunity. A failure to explain how the expert would help supports the refusal.
  6. The correct answer is B (The court reviews the board's record, so fresh merits evidence is generally excluded): Judicial review is not a rehearing, and the reviewing court looks at what the decision maker had. The due diligence test is borrowed from appeals and does not control here. Allowing new merits evidence would turn the court into the primary fact finder.
Case CHB15

Extract, Northern Lakes Energy Act, s 22(3): The Board shall hear any person directly affected by an application, and may, in its discretion, hear any other person who has relevant information or expertise. Greta Olsen farms land the proposed 300 km transmission line would cross. The Lakeview Hikers' Club, with 400 members who use trails near the route, also applied. The Board admitted Olsen as a full participant and allowed the Club written comments only.

  1. The correct answer is A (Fairness protects those directly affected, and the statute lets the Board hear others at its discretion): The duty of fairness attaches to people whose rights, privileges or interests are affected, and the statute itself separates directly affected persons from others whom the Board may hear. A recreational club's general interest in trails does not oblige oral participation. Discretion is still reviewable, but nothing here shows it was misused.
  2. The correct answer is D (She is owed a meaningful chance to test the evidence, and written questions can suffice here): The content of fairness varies with context under the Baker factors, and a participant is entitled to know and respond to the case against her, not to a particular format. Technical evidence about route selection can fairly be tested in writing. The judicial versus administrative label does not decide the question.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness under Vavilov, asking whether the interpretation is justified by the statute's text, context and purpose): Vavilov presumes reasonableness when a tribunal interprets its home statute, and none of the correctness exceptions applies to a participation provision. Brevity of reasons does not license the court to decide the question afresh. The fairness argument is separate from the interpretation of the statutory phrase.
  4. The correct answer is B (Section 2(e) can apply, since it covers federal determinations of rights and obligations generally): Section 2(e) guarantees a fair hearing in accordance with the principles of fundamental justice for the determination of rights and obligations, and it is not tied to life, liberty or security. That is a key difference from Charter section 7. The licence need not qualify as property under section 1(a), and the provision is not limited to penal matters.
  5. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, because Vavilov presumes it governs a tribunal interpreting its own statute): Vavilov makes reasonableness the presumptive standard whenever a decision maker interprets its own enabling statute, and nothing here (no appeal route, no rule of law category) displaces it. The earlier contextual analysis and patent unreasonableness were retired. That the issue is a pure question of law does not by itself trigger correctness.
  6. The correct answer is B (Section 7 may require funded counsel where parental rights are threatened, judged by seriousness and complexity): In New Brunswick v G(J), the Supreme Court held that state removal of a child engages the parent's security of the person and that funded counsel may be required to make the hearing fair. The entitlement turns on the seriousness of the interests, the complexity of the case and the parent's capacity. It is not an automatic guarantee in all cases.
  7. The correct answer is C (Fairness likely required only notice of concerns and a chance to respond, which she received): Under Knight, an office holder dismissed at pleasure, including a probationer, is owed a lower level of fairness: reasons for the concern and an opportunity to respond. That opportunity can be written. A full trial-type process is not demanded, and the existence of a statutory office means the duty is not displaced by contract alone.
Case CHB16

Extract from the fictional Health Professions Act, s 40: "The Discipline Committee may revoke a registration where a member has engaged in professional misconduct." The Act also lists reprimand, suspension and conditions as available sanctions. Ifeoma Eze, 52, a nurse for 28 years, left a medication cart unlocked once during a night shift. No patient was harmed. The Committee revoked her registration, ending her career. Its one-paragraph reasons say the unlocked cart was "serious misconduct" and that revocation "protects the public".

  1. The correct answer is C (Greater impact on her demands reasons that justify the outcome in light of that impact): Vavilov says the stakes for the individual are part of the context that informs what reasonableness demands. Harsh consequences require reasons that respond to them. The severity does not change the standard to correctness, and expertise does not excuse the need to justify.
  2. The correct answer is B (The reasons do not explain why revocation, not a lesser sanction, suited one harmless incident): The gap is in justification: the Act lists lesser sanctions, and the reasons do not explain why the harshest was necessary on these facts. Discipline uses the civil standard, not the criminal one. The Act's silence on length of service does not bar the Committee from weighing it.
  3. The correct answer is D (Public protection is legitimate, but a severe sanction still needs justification as proportionate here): A valid statutory purpose does not by itself justify every means of pursuing it. Reasonableness still requires a rational link between the harshness of the sanction and the reasons given for it. Silence in the Act does not alter the standard, and a permitted sanction can still be unjustified on particular facts.
  4. The correct answer is A (Silence on renewal leaves fairness to the common law, which the cancellation right does not oust): The best route is the default common law duty, whose content depends on the Baker factors, with the express cancellation right not displacing it for a different decision. Stretching a statutory right to a different decision by analogy is weak interpretation. Commercial impact may raise the content owed, but it does not automatically yield the same procedure as cancellation.
  5. The correct answer is A (The penalty is unreasonable, because it exceeds the statutory maximum for one contravention): A decision must operate within the governing statute, and a penalty above the statutory ceiling breaches that constraint. This is a reasonableness argument, since Vavilov rejected jurisdictional error as a separate correctness category. Regulatory penalties may serve deterrence and need not mirror the harm.
  6. The correct answer is D (It weighs heavily against him, because a surprised party should object or seek time to prepare): A party taken by surprise at a hearing is expected to raise the matter and ask for an adjournment, and failure to do so weighs heavily against a later complaint of unfairness. Courts do not generally require a tribunal to adjourn of its own motion where the party is present, engaged and silent. The silence is not a formal waiver, but it suggests no real prejudice was apparent at the time.
  7. The correct answer is A (Dismiss it, because the duty to consult does not apply to the law-making process): Mikisew Cree (2018) held that the duty to consult is not triggered by the development of legislation, because parliamentary sovereignty and the separation of powers keep courts out of the law-making process. The effect of enacted legislation on treaty rights may still be challenged on other grounds. Treaty status does not change the analysis, so neither a declaration nor an order halting the bill is available.
Case CHB17

The Saskatchewan Institute of Registered Massage Therapists (the Institute) received a complaint that Kwame Boateng, a registered therapist, billed for treatments not given. Section 20 of its Act (fictional extract) says: 'The discipline committee shall hold a hearing and may determine its own procedure.' The registrar emailed Mr. Boateng on Monday that a hearing would occur Friday on 'billing concerns'. Before the hearing, the Institute's investigator wrote a report with patient interviews, which the committee read but did not give to Mr. Boateng. Mr. Boateng denies the allegations and says the patients are mistaken. The committee decided on written submissions and suspended his registration for twelve months.

  1. The correct answer is D (No, it gave too little detail and too little time for a serious licence sanction): Notice must give enough detail about the allegations and enough time to prepare, scaled to what is at stake. A twelve month suspension is serious, and 'billing concerns' on a few days' warning fell short. Notice before the decision alone is not enough, and fairness does not require personal service.
  2. The correct answer is A (Yes, he was entitled to the material before the committee, in time to answer it): A person facing suspension must be given the material the decision-maker considers, early enough to respond to it. Stinchcombe is a criminal case, but administrative fairness still demands disclosure scaled to the stakes, and a licence suspension is high. Mr. Boateng did not know the report existed, so a request cannot be a precondition.
  3. The correct answer is A (Credibility was in issue and the sanction was serious, so an oral hearing was required): Where a serious decision depends on whose account is believed, fairness generally requires an oral hearing, as in Singh. The statutory word 'hearing' does not itself mean oral, and the Act leaves procedure to the committee. There is no rule that every suspension needs an oral hearing, and 'inequality of arms' is a European concept that Canadian law does not apply.
  4. The correct answer is C (Yes, because prolonged segregation significantly restricts residual liberty and can harm security of the person): Inmates keep a residual liberty interest, and a decision that substantially tightens the conditions of confinement, such as extended segregation, can engage liberty and security of the person. The Charter sets no fixed number of days, so there is no threshold of that kind. Treating the sentence as the only deprivation would leave harsher internal conditions free from constitutional scrutiny.
  5. The correct answer is C (It does not bar review for unfairness; the court assesses fairness itself and may quash): Privative clauses do not oust the superior court's supervisory role over the legality of administrative action, and procedural fairness is assessed by the court without a standard of review. The jurisdictional-error exception and the patent unreasonableness standard belong to the pre-Vavilov framework.
Case CHB18

On 12 January the Discipline Committee of a provincial dental college found Dr. Amara Okeke guilty of professional misconduct and set the penalty hearing for 15 September. Extract, s 40 of the Dental Professions Act: 'A member may appeal a final order of the Discipline Committee to the Court of Appeal on a question of law within 30 days of the order.' On 20 June Dr. Okeke applied to the Court of King's Bench for judicial review, claiming a refusal of an adjournment on 5 January denied her a fair hearing. The statute sets no time limit for judicial review.

  1. The correct answer is D (The court may refuse review in discretion, weighing whether the coming appeal is an adequate remedy): A statutory appeal does not oust judicial review: the court keeps a discretion and, under Harelkin and Strickland, weighs whether the appeal is an adequate and convenient remedy. A fairness complaint is a question of law and could be raised on the coming appeal, so neither dismissal nor hearing is mandatory. The presumption of reasonableness review concerns the standard of review, not whether the court should hear the case.
  2. The correct answer is D (Decline to intervene before the process ends, absent exceptional circumstances, because early review fragments proceedings): Under C.B. Powell, courts do not interfere with an ongoing administrative process until it has run its course, except in exceptional circumstances, because piecemeal review adds delay and cost. A fairness complaint is not itself exceptional, since it can be raised on the appeal of the final order. Asking the committee to reconsider does not change the timing rule.
  3. The correct answer is C (Treat delay as discretionary, asking if it is explained and prejudices the College or the public): Where no statute fixes a time limit, unreasonable delay is a factor in the court's discretion to refuse a remedy, and the court looks at the explanation and at prejudice to the respondent or the public. The thirty day period in the Federal Courts Act applies only to federal bodies. Courts do not presume prejudice, and delay can matter even without a limitation period.
  4. The correct answer is A (A tribunal may set the scope of an intervenor's participation, since intervenors lack full party rights): Tribunals are masters of their own procedure and routinely tailor an intervenor's role to the issue it can usefully address. An intervenor is not directly affected in the way a party is, so fairness does not demand full party rights. Express statutory authority or consent is not needed for ordinary procedural conditions.
  5. The correct answer is A (A federal Minister deciding an admissibility matter under the Immigration and Refugee Protection Act): A Minister acting under a federal statute exercises a power conferred by an Act of Parliament, which is the core of the definition. A private arbitrator draws authority from contract, a superior court judge is excluded as a court, and a provincial official acts under provincial law.
  6. The correct answer is B (The Crown, which may delegate procedural steps but keeps ultimate responsibility for adequacy of consultation): The honour of the Crown cannot be delegated away. A delegate such as the Office may carry out procedural aspects, but the Crown remains answerable for whether the consultation was adequate, so the minister's approval is vulnerable.
