The proponent does not bear the duty, and the Nation's silence on one issue does not shift the Crown's responsibility to seek out and address the impacts on its rights.
  7. The correct answer is A (Severe consequences demand a stronger justification, so the reasons must show why the result is warranted): Vavilov states that the stakes for the affected person shape what reasonableness requires: the greater the impact on an individual's rights and interests, the more the reasons must justify the result. This does not change the standard to correctness or allow the court to reweigh the evidence. A thin letter resting on a general memo is unlikely to meet that burden.
  8. The correct answer is D (The officer failed to grapple with a central submission, so the reasoning does not show justification): Vavilov treats a failure to meaningfully address the central arguments of the parties as a sign that the decision maker was not actually alert and responsive to the case. The letters went directly to present risk, the statutory question. A conviction can be relevant to risk, the court does not ask what it would have decided, and reasonable grounds is a lower standard than proof beyond a reasonable doubt.
  9. The correct answer is D (Section 7 may require independence suited to the liberty interest despite the clear statutory wording): Ocean Port confirms that clear legislation displaces common law independence requirements, and the unwritten principle of judicial independence applies to courts rather than tribunals. The Charter, however, limits what legislation can do, and where liberty is engaged the principles of fundamental justice under s 7 may demand an independent decision-maker. The common law argument fails because it cannot override express statutory language.
  10. The correct answer is A (Read the reasons as a whole, since silence on minor points is not itself fatal): Vavilov directs reviewing courts to read reasons holistically and in context, with a measure of respect for the decision-maker's institutional setting. Decision-makers need not address every argument or provide a perfect account. A flaw matters when it shows a failure to meaningfully account for central issues, which is not the case here.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The Federal Court has exclusive original jurisdiction over federal boards, so the provincial court cannot act): Section 18 of the Federal Courts Act gives the Federal Court exclusive original jurisdiction to grant the prerogative-type remedies against a federal board, and the Agency officer exercises a federal statutory power. The Tax Court hears appeals from assessments, not judicial review of a discretionary relief refusal. No consent from the Attorney General can confer jurisdiction on a provincial court that Parliament has removed.
  2. The correct answer is D (She waived the objection by staying silent once aware and gambling on a favourable result): An allegation of bias must be raised at the first reasonable opportunity. A party who knows the facts, stays silent and takes the chance of a favourable outcome is taken to have waived the objection. Waiver can be inferred from conduct and need not be express, so the silence defeats her later challenge.
  3. The correct answer is A (Consultation must be meaningful but gives no veto, and adequacy turns on genuinely weighing concerns): Haida Nation and Mikisew Cree describe a duty of consultation scaled to the strength of the claim and the seriousness of the impact, which can require accommodation but does not give a veto or guarantee agreement. Notice alone is not enough, so economic benefit is not a complete answer either.
  4. The correct answer is B (Unreasonable, because wide discretion must serve the statute's purposes and political support is irrelevant): Roncarelli v Duplessis holds that no discretion is absolute and that it must be exercised for purposes consistent with the statute. Cancelling a licence over political support has nothing to do with regulating liquor service, and Vavilov confirms discretion is bounded by the statutory scheme. Wording such as the minister's opinion widens the discretion without removing those limits.
  5. The correct answer is A (The regulation survives on that ground, since the legislature fixed the consequence of noncompliance): The legislature may specify the consequence of breaching a procedural precondition, and here it said a regulation is not invalid on that ground alone. Mandatory-or-directory reasoning matters only when the statute is silent on consequences. The municipality may have other grounds, such as the regulation exceeding the enabling power, but the short consultation cannot itself support quashing.
  6. The correct answer is B (Fairness may require an oral hearing where credibility is central, despite the written-only rule): Tribunals may choose written procedures, and many written hearings are fair. Where serious issues of credibility arise, however, the Baker factors and Singh point toward an oral hearing, and a tribunal rule cannot oust that obligation. Allowing written submissions on the surveillance report is the tempting answer, but it does not let the adjudicator assess her demeanour or test the report.
  7. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, because an appeal to a minister is not a court appeal triggering appellate standards): Under Vavilov, reasonableness is the presumptive standard for review of administrative decisions, and appellate standards apply only where the legislature provides a statutory appeal to a court. An appeal to a minister is an administrative route, so the presumption is not displaced even though questions of law are involved. Patent unreasonableness no longer exists as a standard in Canadian law.
  8. The correct answer is D (Moving her to a more restrictive institution deprives her of residual liberty, testable by habeas): A transfer to a more restrictive institution is a significant change that deprives an inmate of residual liberty, so habeas can test its lawfulness. It does not depend on the superior court supervising sentences or on any exclusive jurisdiction. The lack of a fair process is part of the lawfulness inquiry, not the reason habeas is available.
Case CHB19

Extract from the fictional Prairie Water Act, s 12(1): 'The Minister may, on the Director's recommendation and having regard to the public interest, revoke a diversion licence if the holder has contravened a term of the licence.' Ayesha Karim Farms Ltd filed one monitoring form three days late, its first contravention in twelve years. The Minister revoked its licence by a three-line letter citing 'the public interest' and the Director's report. Revocation ends the farm's irrigation operations. The company seeks judicial review. It did not receive any prior warning.

  1. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness, since Vavilov presumes it governs a minister's decision under the minister's own statute): The Vavilov presumption of reasonableness applies to all administrative decision makers, including ministers acting under their enabling statutes. Lack of specialized expertise is not a reason to depart from it. Removal of a licence does not fall within the rule of law categories (constitutional questions, questions of central importance to the legal system, boundaries between bodies), and patent unreasonableness is gone.
  2. The correct answer is A (The Minister did not justify so harsh a penalty for a minor, first contravention): Vavilov says reasons must account for the impact of a decision on the person affected, and the harsher the consequence, the more justification is required. Revocation for a minor first lapse with no discussion of proportionality fails that test. Reasons need not be perfect or lengthy, the Baker factors concern procedural fairness rather than substance, and ministers may rely on staff advice.
  3. The correct answer is B (Quash the revocation and remit the matter to the Minister for fresh consideration): Vavilov says the usual remedy for an unreasonable decision is to remit it, and a court should decide the matter itself only where remittal would serve no useful purpose because a single outcome is inevitable. Here the statute leaves several responses open, so substitution and mandamus would usurp the Minister's role. Ministerial discretion is reviewable on the reasonableness standard.
  4. The correct answer is B (Contract and private law govern the dismissal, so wrongful dismissal in civil court is the remedy): A public body employing a manager under an ordinary contract, with no statute governing dismissal, is acting as an employer. The relationship is governed by the contract and private employment law, so the claim is for wrongful dismissal. Public status alone does not import public law duties, and there is no collective agreement to send the dispute to an arbitrator.
  5. The correct answer is C (A stay is appropriate because inordinate delay caused prejudice that prevents a fair hearing): When inordinate delay by the regulator has directly caused prejudice to the ability to answer the allegation, the hearing itself cannot be fair, and a stay is the appropriate remedy under the first category recognized in Blencoe. Treating the missing evidence as going to weight would leave Ines facing an allegation she cannot meet because of the college's own inactivity. A section 7 breach is not a precondition, since abuse of process stands apart from the Charter.
  6. The correct answer is D (Witnesses go to fairness, the misreading to substantive review, and setting aside to judicial review remedies): Refusing to hear witnesses concerns the process, so it belongs to procedural fairness. Whether the board's reading of its statute can stand is tested by substantive review under Vavilov, and the request to set the decision aside engages judicial review procedure and remedies. Swapping the first two is the most tempting error, since both are grounds of challenge that test different things.
  7. The correct answer is C (Step aside, because a family and financial tie would raise a reasonable apprehension of bias): The test asks whether an informed person, viewing the matter realistically and practically, would apprehend that she could not decide fairly. A sibling who is also a financial co-obligor meets that threshold even though no one has objected yet. A written disclosure after the fact does not remove the appearance, and the relative's role as witness is not what matters.
  8. The correct answer is C (Exclude it, because the court assesses the reasons actually given and not after-the-fact supplements): Reasonableness review examines the reasoning the decision maker disclosed, and a court should not fashion reasons the decision maker did not give. Late explanations would let a tribunal repair its own record. The standard of review does not change that point.
Case CHB20

Fisheries Licensing Act (fictional extract): s 9: The Director may cancel a licence if satisfied that a condition was breached. s 12: Before cancelling, the Director shall give the holder written notice and an opportunity to respond. The Director of the federal Fisheries Licensing Office cancelled the licence of Maple Ridge Aquaculture Ltd by email on 4 June, without notice. Anil Rao owns all the shares of the company. On 20 July, the company and Mr. Rao apply to the Federal Court for judicial review.

  1. The correct answer is A (It is late under the 30-day limit, though the court may extend time for good reason): Section 18.1(2) of the Federal Courts Act requires an application within 30 days after the decision was first communicated, and the clock does not wait for reasons. The court may extend time, asking whether the applicant always intended to challenge, has an arguable case, caused no prejudice and can explain the delay. A fairness breach does not suspend the limit, so the lateness must be answered through the extension power.
  2. The correct answer is C (He is not directly affected as a shareholder, since the cancellation bound only the licence holder): A person is directly affected only if the decision legally affects their rights or imposes obligations on them, and a decision against a company does not do so for its shareholders, whose losses are derivative. The company is the party best placed to bring the application. Public interest standing also requires a reasonable and effective way to bring the issue to court, which the company already supplies, so Downtown Eastside does not assist him.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quash the cancellation and remit it to the Director to decide again after giving notice): Where an administrator skipped a procedure the statute required, the usual remedy is certiorari quashing the decision and remitting it so the decision maker can decide again fairly. Whether a condition was breached is for the Director under section 9, so the court should not substitute its own finding or compel a particular outcome, as Vavilov explains for remedies. Mandamus and prohibition are poorly suited because the Director keeps a real discretion.
  4. The correct answer is A (Dismiss it, because the committee exercises no power conferred by an Act of Parliament): Being federally regulated does not turn a private employer's internal decisions into the exercise of statutory power. The committee acts under the employment contract, so it falls outside the definition of a federal board. The hearing-based reason is wrong because the definition looks at the source of power, not procedure.
  5. The correct answer is A (The duty to consult does not apply to the law-making process, so the claim fails): The Supreme Court held in Mikisew Cree (2018) that the duty to consult does not extend to the legislative process, including ministers' development of a bill. The honour of the Crown can still matter for later executive action under the Act. Splitting preparation from enactment was the lower-court approach that the Court rejected.
  6. The correct answer is C (Ask whether the delay was inordinate, then whether it caused significant prejudice to fairness or confidence): Abrametz sets a two-step inquiry: is the delay inordinate given the nature of the case and its causes, and does it cause significant prejudice. Dead witnesses and destroyed records bear directly on her ability to answer the complaint. Fixed criminal-law ceilings were not imported into administrative law, and intent is not required.
Case CHB21

Tavares Nunes applies for permanent residence as a skilled worker. His file includes a letter from a Calgary employer confirming a job offer. The visa officer concludes the letter is not genuine, noting that its address differs from the address on his application form. Mr. Nunes was never told of this concern and was not interviewed. The officer's notes say only: 'Offer not credible. Refused.' The governing statute states: 'An officer may decide an application on the written record and may require an interview.'

  1. The correct answer is B (A decisive adverse credibility finding requires notice of the concern and a chance to respond): A finding that a key document is not genuine is a credibility finding, and where it decides the outcome fairness calls for notice and a chance to answer. A statute that permits decision on the written record sets the form of the process but does not remove the common law duty. The extrinsic evidence rule is a separate trigger and does not limit the duty here.
  2. The correct answer is D (Notice of the concern and a reasonable opportunity to respond in writing): Under the Baker approach the content of fairness is flexible, and participation can be written where the stakes and statute do not call for more. An oral hearing is reserved for cases such as serious credibility disputes affecting life or liberty. Internal guidelines are not the case Mr. Nunes needs to meet.
  3. The correct answer is D (Set the decision aside and remit it for redetermination by a different officer): Where fairness was denied on a decisive credibility issue, the usual remedy is to quash and remit, because his answer might change the outcome. Mandamus and substitution are unavailable when the decision-maker has not properly exercised its discretion. A futility argument succeeds only where the result is truly inevitable, which cannot be said when the applicant never had a chance to respond.
  4. The correct answer is A (Yes, because it never disposed of the retaliation claim, so the matter remains open): Functus officio protects decisions actually made. Where a tribunal never decided a distinct issue properly before it, it has not disposed of that issue and may complete its task. The signed decision on one claim does not end jurisdiction over the other, and no statutory request is needed to finish a matter left undecided.
  5. The correct answer is C (A duty is owed since her interests are affected; broad discretion only shapes its content): A discretionary power does not remove the duty of fairness. Knight asks whether the decision is administrative and affects the individual's interests, and a long-standing grant clearly does. Wide discretion matters later, when the court sets how much procedure is owed, and a promise of a hearing is only one source of a duty that already exists.
  6. The correct answer is D (No bias arises; explaining procedure and clarifying evidence for a self-represented party is consistent with neutrality): Tribunals owe unrepresented parties enough help to participate meaningfully, such as explaining the process and clarifying their evidence. This does not breach impartiality unless the member becomes an advocate. The test looks at apprehension, not at whether the outcome changed, and does not depend on a timely objection.
Case CHB22

The Provincial Forestry Appeals Board has seven members. A three-member panel heard Maple Ridge Lumber's appeal over a cutting permit refusal. Before the panel released reasons, the chair convened a full-board meeting to discuss the policy implications of the pending decision. Maple Ridge was not told. At the meeting, members debated a new expert report that had not been filed in the hearing. The panel then issued reasons relying partly on that report. The statute is silent on full-board meetings.

  1. The correct answer is C (No, because members may consult on policy questions provided each keeps independent judgment on the outcome): Consolidated-Bathurst accepts full-board discussion of policy and legal questions as a way of promoting consistency, so long as the members who heard the case remain free to decide it. The parties need not attend such a meeting. The difficulty here lies in what was discussed, not in the meeting itself.
  2. The correct answer is B (The panel relied on new evidence without giving Maple Ridge notice and an opportunity to respond): One safeguard from Consolidated-Bathurst is that new facts or evidence arising in consultation must be put to the parties so they can respond. Maple Ridge never saw the report. Discussion with members who did not hear the case is permitted, and silence in the statute does not prohibit a full-board meeting.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quash the decision for a fairness breach and remit it for redetermination after disclosing the report): A breach of procedural fairness is assessed on its own footing, not through the Vavilov reasonableness framework, and the usual remedy is to set the decision aside and send it back so the applicant can answer the report. Ordering the permit issued would overreach, since the merits remain for the board. Leaving the decision standing ignores the harm the breach caused.
  4. The correct answer is A (Decide the matter itself and direct approval, since remitting would serve no useful purpose here): Vavilov makes remittal the usual remedy, so the respondent's request is a serious position. The Court recognized exceptions, though, where only one outcome is possible or delay would make remitting pointless. Here the record leaves one lawful result and the applicant has already waited years, so deciding the matter is justified.
  5. The correct answer is D (Review remains available, broad wording widens the reasonable range but the regulation must fit the Act): Wide enabling language gives Cabinet considerable room, and the court's reasonableness review will be accordingly generous. It does not oust review, because the regulation must still be consistent with the Act's scope and purposes. Correctness and a constitutional-only approach are both inconsistent with Vavilov's treatment of delegated legislation.
  6. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because the finding rests on a fundamental misapprehension of the evidence the tribunal ignored): A decision that fundamentally misapprehends the evidence, or fails to account for the only evidence on the point, lacks justification. A tribunal need not discuss every document, but it must deal with evidence that squarely contradicts its finding. Appellate deference standards do not govern here.
  7. The correct answer is B (Wrong, because power to decide questions of law carries a presumed power to apply the Charter): A tribunal that has the power to decide questions of law is presumed to have the power to apply the Charter unless the legislature has clearly withdrawn it. Silence does not withdraw the power, and an express reference to the Charter is not required. The question of how a court later reviews the ruling is a separate matter.
  8. The correct answer is C (Challenge the regulation as exceeding the power to prescribe licence conditions, since cancellation is outside it): If the regulation exceeds the enabling power, every cancellation made under it falls with it, so attacking its validity is the most fundamental step. Conditions of licence are not the same as a power to terminate the licence. The unreasonableness and fairness arguments remain available but depend on a regulation that is itself valid.
  9. The correct answer is B (Delay alone does not justify a stay; he must show inordinate delay causing significant prejudice): Under Blencoe and Abrametz, delay is not enough: it must be inordinate and produce significant prejudice, either to the fairness of the hearing or to public confidence in the process. Anxiety and embarrassment are the ordinary consequences of facing a proceeding and do not meet that bar. Bad faith by the regulator is relevant but is not a precondition for a stay.
  10. The correct answer is C (Palpable and overriding error, because appellate standards apply on a statutory appeal to a court): Where the legislature provides an appeal to a court, Vavilov directs the court to apply appellate standards: correctness for questions of law and palpable and overriding error for fact. Housen treats inferences drawn from the evidence as factual, so the inference about what Papadakis knew attracts deference. Treating it as mixed fact and law, or reading the breadth of the appeal as licence to retry the case, ignores that distinction.
Case CHB23

Extract, Northern Wildlife Act (fictional), s 12: 'The Director may issue an outfitter licence to a person who meets the prescribed qualifications. The Director may revoke a licence for a contravention of this Act.' Dena Whitehorse's licence was revoked by Director Paul Arsenault after he learned she had once published criticism of his department. No contravention of the Act was alleged. Dena was given no notice. The Act contains no privative clause.

  1. The correct answer is C (The Act permits revocation only for a contravention, and none was alleged): The question is about the limits of the power itself. The Act ties revocation to a contravention, so revoking without one is a step the statute does not authorize. Bias and lack of notice are real concerns but go to how the power was exercised, and the absence of a privative clause says nothing about whether the power was exceeded.
  2. The correct answer is A (An order of certiorari quashing the revocation as made without lawful authority): Certiorari quashes a decision made without authority, which leaves the original licence in place. Mandamus would only be needed if the Director were refusing to act, and prohibition looks forward to stop proceedings rather than undoing a completed decision. A broad injunction against the Department is not the targeted public law remedy for an invalid revocation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, because no legislated standard or rule of law category rebuts the presumption): Vavilov sets reasonableness as the presumptive standard for the merits of administrative decisions, displaced only by a legislated standard of review or one of the rule of law categories. The label of a statutory limit on power does not by itself trigger correctness, since the old jurisdictional category was abandoned. The contextual Dunsmuir factors and patent unreasonableness no longer govern, and the absence of a privative clause does not change the presumption.
  4. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable if the officer departs from established practice without explanation while the guideline still applies): Guidelines are not binding law, but they form part of the administrative context, and a departure from them calls for explanation. The silence is the flaw. Treating the guideline as a regulation overstates its legal force, and treating it as irrelevant ignores the constraint of established practice.
  5. The correct answer is C (Review is robust: the court tests the reasoning and outcome for justification, not just honesty): Vavilov describes reasonableness as a robust form of review that is deferential but not a rubber stamp. The court looks at the reasoning actually given and at whether the result is justified by it. Good faith or an imagined rational basis is not enough, and the clearly irrational threshold belongs to the old patent unreasonableness idea.
  6. The correct answer is B (A closed mind: disqualification requires that she could not genuinely consider the application's merits): Where a body performs a largely political or policy function, as a council does on rezoning, members may hold and voice views, and the threshold is a closed mind rather than the stricter reasonable apprehension test for adjudicators. Strong public support before a vote does not show she could not be persuaded. Bias review is not excluded for councils, but the bar is high and Brian has shown only an opinion.
  7. The correct answer is C (Examine the merits more closely, because the stay will in effect decide the application): RJR-MacDonald recognizes an exception to the low threshold where the result of the interlocutory motion will in practice amount to a final determination of the case. The judge then looks at the merits more closely than a bare serious issue inquiry. The three stages are not skipped, and a clear legal right belongs to mandamus, not to stays.
  8. The correct answer is D (It lacks the force of law, but it forms part of the legal context for review): Guidelines are not law, but Baker and Vavilov treat them as indicators of what is reasonable and as part of the context that a decision must respect. A decision-maker who departs from one should explain why. Reliance is relevant to legitimate expectations, not to the guideline's general status.
  9. The correct answer is A (Decline to consider it, since review is confined to the record that was before the decision-maker): Judicial review examines the decision actually made on the material the decision-maker had; it is not a rehearing. New evidence going to the merits is generally excluded, subject to narrow exceptions such as general background or a procedural defect. A new report cannot show the officer's decision was unreasonable when the officer never saw it.
Case CHB24

Extract from the fictional Municipal Taxi Licensing Act, s 9: '(1) The Registrar may cancel an operator's licence if satisfied that the operator committed a serious breach of the licensing by-law, and must give written reasons. (2) The Registrar may instead impose conditions or issue a warning.' Registrar Dana Pelletier cancelled the licence of Harbour Cabs Inc after one inspection found a driver's insurance certificate had expired three days earlier. The company had held its licence for 22 years and fixed the lapse that afternoon. Her two-sentence reasons say only: 'An expired certificate is a serious breach.' Harbour Cabs applies for judicial review.

  1. The correct answer is D (The reasons do not justify treating a brief, corrected lapse as serious given the harsh consequence): Reasonableness asks whether the reasons justify the decision in light of the statute and its consequences. The cancellation ends a business of 22 years, yet the two sentences never explain why a corrected three-day lapse is serious. The court does not compare the result with its own view, short reasons are not unreasonable in themselves, and the Act does not rule out cancellation for a single lapse.
  2. The correct answer is C (It starts from the Registrar's reasons and the record rather than deciding the issue afresh itself): Vavilov requires reasonableness review to focus on the decision actually made, beginning with the reasons read in light of the record. The court does not conduct its own de novo analysis first, and it does not fill gaps by inventing better reasons for the decision maker. Looking only at whether the outcome was acceptable was the pre-Vavilov approach and sidelines the reasons.
  3. The correct answer is C (Quash the cancellation and remit to the Registrar, because several outcomes remain open under the Act): Vavilov sets remittal as the usual remedy, since the legislature gave the sanction decision to the Registrar. Section 9 offers cancellation, conditions or a warning, so the court cannot say only one outcome is possible, and substituting its own sanction would take over her role. Broad discretion does not immunize a decision from reasonableness review.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness applies, with deference to council; it falls only if no reasonable body would pass it): Municipal by-laws are reviewed for reasonableness, with particular deference to elected councils because they weigh local interests and answer to voters. Catalyst Paper Corp v North Cowichan (District), 2012 SCC 2, describes invalidity only where no reasonable body would have passed the by-law. Disagreement with the policy is not a ground for quashing, and the older strict approach to municipal powers is no longer the test.
  2. The correct answer is C (Decline to supply missing reasons, and assess whether the reasons given justify the result): A reviewing court examines the reasons the decision-maker actually gave and does not fashion its own to bolster a decision. A sanction must itself be justified, so silence on length matters. Whether the omission requires remittal is a remedy question, not a finding of procedural unfairness.
  3. The correct answer is C (Reviewable in the Federal Court, as it exercised statutory power, with wide deference on policy): When the Governor in Council acts under a statutory power it is a federal board, and the Federal Court may review the order, with deference owed to broad policy decisions. Cabinet status does not immunize statutory decisions, no direct appeal route exists, and the jurisdictional-error ground is not the framework.
  4. The correct answer is B (Judicial review is the proper direct route for challenging the board's order, so the objection fails): A collateral attack is an indirect challenge in a proceeding not designed to review the order. Judicial review is the direct proceeding for challenging a tribunal's order, so the doctrine does not apply to it. Consent of the board is irrelevant, and judicial review does not become an appeal merely because the decision is final.
  5. The correct answer is D (The decision is a general policy choice, so it attracts little or no individual fairness duty): A decision of general public policy, such as closing a class of facilities, is treated much like legislation and attracts little or no duty to those affected. Personal impact on one worker does not turn it into an individual decision. The test looks to the character of the decision rather than to the type of body that made it.
  6. The correct answer is D (Notice of the alleged misconduct and a fair chance to respond before any adverse finding): A finding of misconduct can seriously damage reputation even without legal effect, so a person facing possible criticism must be told and given a chance to answer. The inquiry's non-binding nature lowers the content of fairness but does not remove it. Full party standing goes beyond what is needed to protect him.
Case CHB25

The Ministry of Natural Resources has received an application from Ridgeline Timber Ltd to renew a 25-year tree farm licence covering the Silverbirch Valley. The Oskaya Nation asserts Aboriginal title to the valley and has filed a well documented claim with the federal government, supported by oral histories and archaeological reports. The Ministry has known of the claim since 2019. The renewal would allow clearcutting of old growth stands the Nation says it relies on for cedar bark harvesting. The Ministry has sent the Nation a form letter advising that the renewal will issue in thirty days.

  1. The correct answer is C (Crown knowledge, actual or constructive, of the potential existence of the Aboriginal right or title): Haida Nation sets three elements: Crown knowledge of a potential claim, contemplated Crown conduct, and potential adverse impact. The Ministry's awareness of the claim since 2019 answers the first element only. The renewal decision and the risk to cedar harvesting answer the other two, and proof of title is not required at all.
  2. The correct answer is D (The deep end, requiring participation in decision making, written reasons and possible accommodation of concerns): Haida Nation ties the depth of consultation to the strength of the claim and the seriousness of the potential impact on the right. A well supported title claim and long term harm to old growth point to the deep end, where participation in decision making, reasons and accommodation may be needed. Consent is required only once title is proven, and a form letter reflects the low end, not these facts.
  3. The correct answer is A (The renewal is quashed and remitted for consultation, leaving the final decision with the Ministry): Where the Crown breaches the duty to consult, the usual remedy is an order setting aside the decision and requiring proper consultation, because the court reviews the process rather than deciding the merits. The court will not substitute the Nation's plan or declare title on a review application. Damages are not the usual remedy, since Haida Nation recognized that the duty can be enforced before title is proven.
  4. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, since lack of expertise on a general law question no longer rebuts the presumption): Under the earlier framework a general common-law question outside the decision-maker's expertise attracted correctness. Vavilov dropped that category, so the presumption of reasonableness holds unless the question is of central importance to the legal system as a whole, and a dispute that matters mainly to the parties is not. The lack of an appeal route confirms rather than rebuts the presumption.
  5. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because the regulator must respect the statutory scheme, including its express limits): Vavilov directs that a reasonable interpretation must be consistent with the text, context and purpose of the governing scheme, and a definitions section is part of that context. Expertise does not license a reading that disregards an express limit in the same statute. Correctness review does not apply, since the regulator is interpreting its own home statute.
  6. The correct answer is B (No jurisdiction, because the committee's powers are provincial; the provincial superior court hears it): The Federal Court's review power depends on the source of the decision-maker's authority, and a provincial college exercising a provincial statute is not a federal board. Marta's federal credential does not change who made the impugned decision, and the Federal Court has no general concurrent jurisdiction over provincial bodies.
  7. The correct answer is A (Quo warranto, which challenges a person's legal authority to occupy a particular public office): Quo warranto tests whether someone is lawfully entitled to hold a public office, which is exactly the resident's question. Certiorari attacks decisions rather than the holder's status, and prohibition prevents future action rather than determining entitlement. Mandamus presupposes that the seat is already vacant by statute, which is the very point in dispute.
  8. The correct answer is A (Ask whether a live controversy remains, then whether to exercise discretion to hear it anyway): Borowski sets a two step approach: first, whether the tension and live controversy have disappeared, and second, if so, whether the court should still exercise its discretion to hear the case. Mootness is not an automatic bar, and public importance is only one consideration in the second step.
  9. The correct answer is D (Set the decision aside and require the promised interview, without ordering a grant of parole): A clear representation about procedure from the decision-maker is enforced as a procedural entitlement, so the decision is quashed and redone with the interview. The doctrine never dictates the outcome, which stays with the Board. Reviewing the paper file does not honour the specific procedure the Board itself announced.
  10. The correct answer is A (Correctness, because appellate standards of review apply to a question of law on a statutory appeal): Where the legislature provides a statutory appeal, Vavilov directs courts to apply appellate standards, which means correctness for questions of law. Expertise and a leave requirement do not displace that. Palpable and overriding error applies to factual findings and mixed questions, not to a pure question of statutory interpretation.
  11. The correct answer is C (Decline to apply the provision to Ravi if it breaches s 7, without declaring it invalid): Under Conway, a tribunal that can decide questions of law may determine whether a provision infringes the Charter and decline to apply it to the case before it. That ruling affects only the parties and does not formally invalidate the law, because a general declaration of invalidity is reserved to the superior courts. The view that only superior courts may consider Charter challenges was rejected once the presumption of tribunal jurisdiction was recognised.
  12. The correct answer is C (Clear wording displaces common law bias rules, but section 7 still requires an impartial decider): Statutory authorization can override the common law, but it cannot override the Constitution. Segregation engages liberty, so section 7 requires a process consistent with the principles of fundamental justice, which include an impartial decision-maker. Section 7 is not confined to criminal prosecutions.
Case CHB26

Tomas Reyes, a foreign national, was arrested by border officers and held at an immigration holding centre for 14 months pending removal. His removal has stalled because his home country will not issue travel documents. Detention reviews every 30 days have continued the detention, citing flight risk. He has not been charged with a crime, and no removal date can be given. His lawyer is considering habeas corpus in the provincial superior court.

  1. The correct answer is A (Whether the detention is lawful, with the authority justifying it once a legitimate ground arises): Habeas corpus asks whether a deprivation of liberty is lawful. Once the applicant raises a legitimate ground for doubting lawfulness, the burden shifts to the detaining authority to justify the detention. Habeas is not a reasonableness review of the immigration decisions, nor a challenge to the merits of removal.
  2. The correct answer is B (The court should hear it, since the review process is less broad and advantageous than habeas): The Supreme Court has held that superior courts should hear habeas applications unless the statutory review is at least as broad and advantageous. Detention reviews do not offer the same scope or the same remedy, so they do not displace habeas. The Federal Court's role in judicial review does not bar a habeas application in the provincial superior court.
  3. The correct answer is D (Order his release from custody, since lawful authority for the detention has now ended): The traditional habeas remedy is release when detention is unlawful. Once detention has become indefinite, sending the matter back or merely declaring unlawfulness leaves Mr. Reyes confined. Mandamus to obtain travel documents would redirect the case to a different question, namely removal, rather than the lawfulness of his detention.
  4. The correct answer is D (Fairness is contextual, so notice and a chance to speak may suffice under the code): Band councils are subject to the duty of fairness, but its content depends on context, including the community's own code and governance practices. Written notice and a full chance to speak before the assembly gave him the substance of what fairness requires. Courts do review these decisions, and a trial-type hearing is not the default.
  5. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because division of powers questions require a single, final answer from the courts): Vavilov identifies constitutional questions about the division of powers as a category reviewed for correctness, because the rule of law demands a single answer. Board expertise does not change that. The Doré framework concerns the balancing of Charter protections in discretionary decisions, and it has no role in deciding which level of government has authority.
  6. The correct answer is A (Delay breaches fairness only if unacceptable and significantly prejudicial; stress and reputational harm alone are insufficient): Blencoe holds that administrative delay amounts to a breach of procedural fairness only where it is unacceptable and causes significant prejudice, such as impairing the fairness of the hearing, or amounts to an abuse of process. Stigma and stress, however real, are not enough on their own. Common law fairness can consider timing, and the judicial review time limit is unrelated.
  7. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness presumptively applies, because leave to commence judicial review is not a statutory appeal): Leave under section 72(1) is a threshold for starting an application for judicial review, not a right of appeal. With no legislated appeal, the Vavilov presumption of reasonableness governs. Granting leave does not convert the proceeding or let the judge choose a different standard.
  8. The correct answer is B (He lacked a meaningful chance to present his case, and the panel should have adjourned): Fairness requires a real opportunity to participate, and continuing in his absence deprived him of it. A virtual format is not unfair in itself. Procedural choices attract respect but are not immune from review, and a breach of fairness does not require proof that the outcome would have differed.
  9. The correct answer is B (Mandamus may order a decision if delay is unreasonable and unjustified, though not a particular outcome): Under the Apotex test, a court may compel performance of a public duty that is due, and for delay the applicant must show it is longer than the process requires, without satisfactory justification, and not caused by the applicant. Mandamus orders a decision, not a favourable one, because the substantive discretion remains with the officials. A generic backlog does not justify a delay this far past the published standard.
  10. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness, since no exception applies and the presumption covers a tribunal's reading of its own statute): Vavilov presumes reasonableness even for questions of law arising under the tribunal's own statute, and none of the rule of law exceptions is engaged here. Ambiguity of the text is relevant to whether the interpretation is reasonable, not to which standard applies. The home-statute exception belongs to the abandoned framework.
  11. The correct answer is C (Weighing is for council, and the court intervenes only if the outcome lacks justification): Where a statute lists factors without ranking them, the decision-maker assigns their weight, and a reviewing court will not reweigh them. The record shows council considered all four, so the complaint is only about weight. Reasonableness still requires a justified outcome, which is why municipal decisions are not immune from review.
  12. The correct answer is D (It asks whether the rule is consistent with the delegation's text, context and purpose): Delegated legislation must stay within the limits of the power delegated, which are found by reading the enabling provision in its full context and in light of its purpose. Compliance with procedure does not cure a rule that goes beyond the grant. Expertise and policy choice operate only inside the delegated scope, and the patent unreasonableness test is no longer the governing framework.
  13. The correct answer is A (Fairness is owed because the cancellation affects important interests, and discretion does not displace the duty): Statutory discretion remains subject to the common law duty of fairness unless clearly displaced, and cancelling long-held support affects the claimant's basic livelihood. The old privilege and right distinction no longer decides whether fairness is owed, and the administrative label affects only its content. Silence in the statute leaves room for implied procedural protections.
Case CHB27

Rani Gill sat on a three-member panel of the Maritime Pipeline Appeal Board hearing a landowners' appeal against Northeast Gas Ltd. Eight years earlier, Gill was a partner in a firm that acted for Northeast Gas on unrelated rate filings; she left the firm and has no financial tie to it. The landowners did not object at the hearing, lost, and learned of the former affiliation two weeks after the decision.

  1. The correct answer is A (Impartiality is presumed; only cogent evidence of real likelihood of bias displaces it, not mere suspicion): Decision-makers are presumed impartial, and the threshold for finding an apprehension of bias is high, requiring a real likelihood or probability and not mere suspicion. Proof of actual bias is not required. The onus stays on the party alleging bias.
  2. The correct answer is B (No: the link is remote in time and unrelated in subject, with no continuing tie): The test looks at how closely the past relationship relates to the present dispute and whether any live tie remains. A lapsed association on unrelated work, with no financial link, would not lead an informed person to think she would probably decide unfairly. Lack of knowledge goes to waiver, not to whether the connection is disqualifying.
  3. The correct answer is C (No: waiver requires knowledge of the facts, and they learned of the affiliation only afterwards): A party can waive an objection to bias only if it knew of the grounds and chose not to raise them at the first opportunity. The landowners did not know, so their silence is no waiver and the decision would be set aside. Waiver of bias is possible in principle, which is why the knowledge requirement matters.
  4. The correct answer is D (The 30-day period has run, so he needs a discretionary extension of time to proceed): Section 18.1(2) of the Federal Courts Act sets a 30-day period running from when the decision was first communicated. Mr. Grant filed after about seven weeks, so he needs an extension, which the court has a discretion to grant on a proper explanation. Waiting for counsel does not move the start date.
  5. The correct answer is D (She likely loses: interpretation concerns must be raised at the first opportunity, and silence waives them): Fairness requires continuous, precise, competent and contemporaneous interpretation, but an objection must be made at the earliest practical opportunity or it is waived. Counsel was present and understood the problem or could have. A party cannot hold the complaint back to see how the decision turns out.
  6. The correct answer is C (The claim indirectly attacks a regulatory order that remains valid until set aside by review): The strongest argument is the rule against collateral attack: the rate order stands until it is quashed or overturned through the proper statutory or judicial review channel, and the refund claim depends on its invalidity. Exclusive regulator jurisdiction would oust the court completely, which overstates the position. The standing and limitation arguments have no support in the facts.
  7. The correct answer is C (A member who missed evidence cannot take part without statutory authority or the parties' consent): The rule that those who hear must decide bars a member from joining a decision on evidence he or she did not hear, unless the statute authorizes it or the parties agree. Where credibility is central, a transcript is a poor substitute for seeing witnesses. Panel composition is not a purely administrative matter once a hearing is under way.
  8. The correct answer is D (Judicial review applies to delegated state authority, which a congregation's internal membership decision does not involve): Judicial review supervises the exercise of delegated state authority. A religious congregation's decision about its own membership is not an exercise of such authority, and incorporation, a tax exemption or the effect on a member does not make it public. Any remedy lies in private law, for example if a contractual or property right is at stake.
  9. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, the presumptive standard where the legislature has not prescribed a standard and no exception applies): Vavilov makes reasonableness the presumptive standard for the merits of administrative decisions, displaced only by a legislated standard, a statutory appeal, or a rule of law exception. None applies here. A privative clause is no longer needed to trigger deference, and the jurisdictional category was abandoned in Vavilov.
  10. The correct answer is B (Courts must give effect to the prescribed standard, so correctness governs the registrar's reading): Vavilov treats a legislature's explicit direction on the standard as a rebuttal of the presumption of reasonableness, and courts must follow it. It is not one factor in a balance. A privative clause is a separate matter and is not needed for the direction to bind.
  11. The correct answer is C (An informed, right-minded person who views the matter realistically and practically and has thought it through): The Committee for Justice and Liberty test asks what an informed person, viewing the matter realistically and practically, would conclude. The perspective is objective and reasonable, not the litigant's own, and it does not presume partiality from any link. Insider knowledge of motives is also outside the test.
  12. The correct answer is A (Only explicit statutory language can override privilege, and a general production power falls short): Solicitor-client privilege is a near-absolute rule of substantive law, so legislation can displace it only by clear, explicit and unequivocal words. A general power to compel 'any document' does not meet that standard. The Supreme Court has held that even looking at the document to decide the claim is not authorized by such general language.
  13. The correct answer is C (A serious flaw in justification, transparency or intelligibility, or in respecting legal and factual constraints): Reasonableness review asks whether the decision is justified, transparent and intelligible and falls within the constraints imposed by the law and the facts. The court does not ask what it would have decided, nor whether a competing reading is stronger. Not every legal misstep suffices; the flaw must be serious enough to undermine the decision.
  14. The correct answer is C (Openness is presumed, but anonymizing is justified if publication seriously threatens the dignity of sensitive information): The open court principle applies in some form to administrative tribunals, and a tribunal has implied power to control its own process, including by anonymizing. The test is demanding because publicity is presumed, but highly sensitive personal information such as a psychiatric diagnosis can meet the serious risk threshold. Privacy legislation does not automatically trump openness, and a bare request is not enough.
  15. The correct answer is D (Anyone directly affected by the matter in respect of which relief is sought): Section 18.1(1) allows the Attorney General or anyone directly affected by the matter in respect of which relief is sought to apply. A general interest in legality is not enough, and the test is wider than persons whose legal rights were formally decided. Hartley would have to show that the ruling affects him directly, not merely that he disagrees with it.
Case CHB28

Extract from the Radiation Safety Act (fictional): s 40(1) A person aggrieved by a decision of the Registrar may appeal to the Court of Appeal, with leave, on any question of law or fact. Dr Ana Petrov's licence was suspended after the Registrar held that 'supervised use' under the regulation requires a physician to be physically present in the room, and found on conflicting testimony that no physician was present during a scan. Dr Petrov appeals, challenging the Registrar's reading of 'supervised use', the finding that no physician was present, and the application of the rule to the scan.

  1. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because the Act creates a statutory appeal and this is a question of law): Where a statute provides an appeal, Vavilov directs appellate standards, and a pure interpretation of the regulation is a question of law reviewed for correctness. Expertise does not displace that direction, and a leave requirement is only a screening step. The Court of Appeal asks for itself what 'supervised use' means.
  2. The correct answer is D (Palpable and overriding error, because the finding is one of fact made on conflicting testimony): Appellate standards apply on a statutory appeal, and findings of fact are reviewed for palpable and overriding error. The court does not reweigh evidence or substitute its own view of credibility. Reasonableness is the default only for judicial review where no appeal lies.
  3. The correct answer is D (Palpable and overriding error, because applying a correct test to facts is mixed fact and law): On a statutory appeal, mixed questions of fact and law attract palpable and overriding error unless a legal question can be extricated. The Registrar stated the test correctly, so no extricable error of law is alleged and the dispute concerns application to the facts. Correctness would be available only if the Registrar had misstated the meaning of 'supervised use' itself, and reasonableness is not the standard when an appeal lies.
  4. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness applies, because commercial importance alone is not central importance to the legal system): The correctness category for general questions of law requires central importance to the legal system as a whole and a field outside the decision-maker's expertise. Employee status under a labour statute is the board's core task. Economic importance to an industry does not meet that threshold.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

Case CHB29

Statutory extract (fictional Rental Housing Licensing Act): s 14: A licensing officer may cancel a landlord's licence if satisfied the landlord breached this Act. s 15: No appeal lies from a cancellation. s 16: A landlord whose licence is cancelled may not reapply for five years. The Act is silent on procedure. Gurpreet Dhillon, who owns 40 rental units in Brampton, received a letter cancelling his licence on a tenant's complaint. He had not been told of the complaint beforehand.

  1. The correct answer is B (It raises the protections owed, because the decision is final and cannot be corrected on appeal): Baker states that greater protections are warranted when the statute provides no appeal and the decision is determinative. With no appeal, the first decision is the only chance to be heard. That raises the content of fairness but does not by itself convert the process into a full trial.
  2. The correct answer is A (It makes cancellation determinative with no later chance to answer the complaint, supporting stronger procedure): The role of a decision within the scheme matters: where it is final and the individual cannot revisit it, more procedure is owed up front. The reapplication bar reinforces that this is the decisive moment. A desire for speed does not oust the common law, which applies unless the statute clearly excludes it.
  3. The correct answer is A (He likely succeeds: statutory silence leaves the common law to supply notice and a reply): The common law duty of fairness is displaced only by clear statutory language or necessary implication, and silence on procedure is not enough. Quick finality cuts toward more up-front fairness, not less, since no appeal can repair a mistake. Notice and an opportunity to respond, often in writing, can satisfy the duty, so an oral hearing is not automatically required.
  4. The correct answer is C (Written submissions can supply meaningful participation, so no oral hearing was required here): Baker holds that fairness does not automatically require an oral hearing, and that the participation rights should suit the context. Detailed written submissions allowed Priya to put her case, and the officer made no adverse finding on whom to believe. The importance of the decision raises the content of fairness but does not by itself dictate an oral format.
  5. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because constitutional protections of Indigenous rights call for a single authoritative judicial answer): Vavilov lists the scope of Aboriginal rights under section 35 among the constitutional questions reviewed for correctness, because the rule of law requires a final and consistent answer. The regulator's expertise in resource matters does not displace that rule, and a sliding scale of deference is no longer part of the framework.
  6. The correct answer is A (The court may excuse late notice since hospitalization may be a reasonable excuse absent prejudice): Statutory notice requirements can validly condition claims against public authorities, and the Act itself supplies the relief valve. Mann must show a reasonable excuse, which a lengthy hospital stay may supply, and the absence of prejudice. Strict bar ignores the excuse clause, and actual knowledge alone does not satisfy the statutory test.
  7. The correct answer is C (Proximity: the Act imposes duties owed to the public, not to individual investors): In Cooper v Hobart the Supreme Court found no relationship of proximity between a registrar and investors, because the statute made the registrar answerable to the public interest and not to individual investors. Without proximity, no prima facie duty arises and the later questions of breach, policy immunity and remoteness are never reached.
  8. The correct answer is B (The refusal conflicts with the statute and denies a representation right the legislature granted): A tribunal may control its own process, but it cannot override a representation right that its enabling statute confers on parties. The word agent plainly covers a paralegal. Efficiency cannot justify defeating the statutory right, so the adjudicator's discretion over procedure does not rescue the ruling.
  9. The correct answer is A (An order requiring the Minister to decide the application, without dictating whether to approve it): Mandamus can compel a decision-maker to exercise a discretion that must be considered, but it does not dictate the result of that discretion. Delay therefore supports an order to decide, not an order to approve. Discretion is not beyond mandamus when the power is unexercised, and a deemed approval would again substitute the court's choice for the Minister's.
  10. The correct answer is C (It does not choose the standard, but it shapes what is reasonable in context): Vavilov removed expertise as a factor for selecting the standard of review, since the presumption of reasonableness rests on legislative choice. Reasonableness remains contextual, though, and the board's institutional experience informs what outcomes and reasoning are acceptable. It is therefore not irrelevant, and it does not push review toward patent unreasonableness.
  11. The correct answer is D (It grants procedural latitude, but the Commission's choices remain subject to the common law fairness duty): A power to set one's own procedure gives flexibility but does not authorize unfairness; courts defer to reasonable choices while still requiring that the process be fair. Displacing the common law needs clear words, not a general grant. Patent unreasonableness is a pre-Vavilov standard and does not govern fairness in any event.
  12. The correct answer is B (Correctness applies, since legislatures cannot shield constitutional questions from review by a superior court): Constitutional questions require a single definitive ruling, and a legislature cannot oust the superior courts' constitutional supervisory role by a privative clause. Patent unreasonableness no longer exists as a standard after Vavilov, which treats a privative clause as at most relevant to the presumption of reasonableness on non-constitutional issues.
  13. The correct answer is A (Appellate standards: correctness on questions of law, palpable and overriding error on fact and mixed questions): Where the legislature has provided a statutory appeal, Vavilov directs the court to apply appellate standards as in Housen v Nikolaisen: correctness for questions of law and palpable and overriding error for questions of fact and mixed fact and law. Reasonableness is the default only where there is no appeal. An appeal right is a legislative signal about the standard a court should respect.
  14. The correct answer is D (Quash and remit, since a gap on a central issue shows the decision is not justified): Under Vavilov a decision with a fundamental gap in its reasoning is unreasonable, and the usual remedy is to send the matter back to the decision-maker. Substituting the court's own decision is reserved for cases where only one outcome is possible, which the record here does not show. Bad faith is not a prerequisite to intervention.
  15. The correct answer is C (The officer ignored central submissions, so the reasons do not justify the refusal): Reasonableness review asks whether the reasons are transparent, intelligible and responsive to the central submissions put forward. Silence on the mother's dependence and a one-line treatment of the children suggest the key factors were not engaged. An oral hearing is not generally required, and the court does not reweigh the factors.
  16. The correct answer is C (The board has no duty of its own, but its statute may assign it consultation powers): The duty to consult is owed by the Crown, and an independent tribunal is not the Crown. A statute may nonetheless give a tribunal powers to carry out consultation or to assess whether the Crown's consultation was adequate. Neither ordinary procedural fairness nor an automatic Crown agency rule replaces that analysis of the tribunal's own enabling statute.
  17. The correct answer is A (Serious issue to be tried, irreparable harm, and balance of convenience, applied together on the evidence): Interim relief pending judicial review uses the three-part test from RJR-MacDonald: a serious issue, irreparable harm and the balance of convenience, assessed together. A strong prima facie case applies only in narrower settings, and the Haida duty goes to the merits rather than interim relief. The English arguable-case approach is not the Canadian test.
  18. The correct answer is D (Section 7 does not extend to a corporation's purely economic interests such as billing privileges): Section 7 protects life, liberty and security of the person, which are interests of human beings, and corporations cannot claim it. It also does not cover purely commercial or economic interests. The arbitrariness analysis comes only after a protected interest has been engaged, so it cannot create the interest. Northshore must rely on the common law duty of fairness instead.
  19. The correct answer is B (She may use either official language, whatever her skill in the other language): The right to be heard in the official language the party selects belongs to the party and is not conditioned on proficiency in the other language or on cost. The respondent's cost concern cannot override a statutory language right. Her counsel has the same independent right to choose, so the tribunal cannot link the two.
  20. The correct answer is B (Clear statutory language can displace the common law fairness rule, subject to constitutional limits): Parliament and the legislatures may authorize what would otherwise be apprehended bias or lack of independence, provided the language is clear and no constitutional guarantee is breached. Magic words are not required, since necessary implication also suffices. Common law fairness fills gaps in the statute and cannot contradict it.
  21. The correct answer is A (Fairness applies, because the order targets one company on findings about its conduct, whoever made it): The identity of the maker does not decide whether fairness applies; the nature of the decision does. An order that removes one company's permit on the basis of its alleged conduct affects an individual directly, so notice and a chance to respond are owed. Cabinet's status may affect the form of review, not the existence of the duty.
Case CHB30

Federal Import Licence Guideline 4 states that renewal 'will ordinarily be granted' to importers with three years of clean inspections. Maple Valley Importers Inc., of Saskatoon, has a clean record for five years. A delegate of the federal Food Safety Branch refused renewal in a letter sent to its Saskatoon office on 2 February, with no reasons and no mention of the guideline. The company's counsel considers a challenge.

  1. The correct answer is D (It is in the wrong court, because federal boards are reviewed in the Federal Court): A delegate of a federal Branch exercises power under federal law and is a federal board, so the Federal Court holds jurisdiction under the Federal Courts Act. The company's location does not change the source of the power. The Federal Court of Appeal's first-instance review is confined to the bodies listed in section 28.
  2. The correct answer is D (The application is late, but the court may extend the 30-day period if delay is justified): Section 18.1(2) of the Federal Courts Act requires an application within 30 days after the decision was first communicated, and the letter went out on 2 February. Twenty March is well past that, but the court has discretion to extend time. The period does not wait for reasons or for a decision to sue.
  3. The correct answer is B (The decision is unreasonable, because the delegate did not explain departing from guidance framing the decision): Under Vavilov the decision must be justified in light of the legal and factual constraints on it, and relevant policy guidance is part of that context. Departing from it without explanation undermines reasonableness. Legitimate expectation in Canada yields procedural, not substantive, protection, and a guideline stating 'ordinarily' is not binding law.
  4. The correct answer is A (No, it governs the Board's review of the officer; the court presumes reasonableness for the Board): The prescribed standard tells the Board how to review the officer; it says nothing about how a court reviews the Board. The court therefore starts from the presumption of reasonableness. There is no court appeal here, so the Housen standards are not engaged.
  5. The correct answer is C (It departs from established tribunal practice without explanation, so the interpretation is not justified): Vavilov requires a decision maker that departs from longstanding internal practice to explain why; a bare departure leaves the interpretation unjustified. Tribunals are not bound by strict precedent, so the stare decisis framing overstates the point. Correctness is not triggered by an ordinary benefits question, and there is no general duty to forewarn a party of a legal reading.
  6. The correct answer is A (No, the duty looks forward to new impacts, and an unchanged renewal creates none): The duty is triggered by Crown conduct with the potential to adversely affect a claimed right in a new way. Historical grievances and the lingering effects of past wrongs are addressed through other remedies, not a fresh consultation duty. Because this renewal changes nothing, it has no new impact, though the result would differ if the renewal altered flows or expanded operations, and licensing decisions can trigger the duty when they do.
  7. The correct answer is B (When credibility is central and serious interests are at stake, an oral process may be required): Fairness is contextual, and an oral process may be required where a decision turns on credibility and affects important interests such as family life. Written processes are acceptable in many immigration settings, so there is no standard rule favouring oral hearings. Statutory silence does not exclude implied procedural duties.
  8. The correct answer is B (Failing to engage with evidence that bears directly on the outcome makes the reasoning unjustified): Under Vavilov a decision is unreasonable when the reasoning does not meaningfully account for the central evidence and arguments, because the court cannot trace a rational path to the outcome. A reviewing court may not fill the gap with its own rationale. Reasonableness review is the proper lens for the adequacy of reasons that were provided.
  9. The correct answer is A (Administrative convenience cannot defeat meaningful participation; the tribunal must adapt where she cannot otherwise present evidence): Tribunals have wide procedural latitude, but fairness requires that a participant be able to put her case, and efficiency or cost does not justify excluding someone who cannot use the prescribed method. Allowing paper filing is a modest adjustment. Improper purpose is not the test.
  10. The correct answer is D (Grant it: she showed a continuing intention to proceed, a reasonable explanation and an arguable case): On an extension of time the court asks whether the applicant had a continuing intention to pursue the application, whether there is an arguable case, whether the delay has a reasonable explanation and whether the other party is prejudiced. Each factor favours Cheryl here. The Minister's consent is not required, and she need not prove the merits at this stage.
  11. The correct answer is C (Legitimate expectation protects procedure only, so she may claim a fair process, not approval): In Canada a legitimate expectation yields procedural protection, such as notice and a chance to respond before a change, and not a guaranteed result. Courts in the United Kingdom have gone further, which is the tempting error here. A policy change does not erase the procedural claim, so insisting that approval must follow overstates the point.
  12. The correct answer is A (Paper screening is fair if it tests whether the claim could succeed without resolving credibility): A screening stage that asks only whether the allegations, taken at their highest, could succeed does not decide who is believed, so Singh does not require an oral hearing. If the tribunal in substance rejected her account, the analysis would change. The quasi-constitutional status of human rights law does not itself require oral process, and consent is not a precondition to a paper screening.
  13. The correct answer is D (Delay amounts to abuse of process because it compromises the fairness of the eventual hearing): Under Blencoe, delay alone does not offend fairness, but delay that causes significant prejudice to the fairness of the hearing, such as lost witnesses and records, can amount to an abuse of process justifying a stay. A stay for delay without prejudice is not the rule. Legitimate expectation concerns promises about procedure, not general expectations of speed, and confining delay to penalty ignores the damage to her defence.
  14. The correct answer is C (The doctrine cannot require the Minister to ignore factors that the statute makes mandatory): A representation cannot fetter a decision-maker's duty to follow the statute, and Canadian law does not enforce substantive promises in any case. The Minister therefore acted lawfully in declining to be bound. Revocation by cabinet is a procedural invention with no support.
  15. The correct answer is B (A decision unjustifiably limiting a Charter protection cannot be reasonable, since constitutional constraints bind it): Vavilov lists the constitutional framework among the legal constraints that bear on reasonableness, so a decision that unjustifiably limits a Charter protection cannot be reasonable. For discretionary decisions the court applies reasonableness through the Doré proportionality framework, not correctness. Wide statutory discretion never authorizes a decision-maker to disregard the Charter.
  16. The correct answer is C (A declaration, which states the legal position but carries no direct coercive force): A declaration states legal rights or positions without compelling anyone or changing anything already done. That matches Ms. Silva's wish for an authoritative statement without disturbing fines or enforcement. Prohibition, mandamus and certiorari are coercive or quashing remedies and would do more than she asks.
  17. The correct answer is C (An abuse of discretion for an improper purpose, carried out in bad faith): Using a statutory transfer power to punish a grievance is a purpose outside the statute, and describing it as an administrative necessity shows a lack of good faith. Fairness concerns about disclosure may arise in other transfers, but they do not capture the central wrong here. The warden had the power to transfer, so the problem is how and why it was used, not a missing power.
Case CHB31

Extract from the fictional Rental Housing Act, s 22: A landlord or tenant may appeal an order of the Rental Housing Tribunal to the Superior Court on a question of law or fact. Landlord Darius Mehta terminated the tenancy of Priya Nair and the Tribunal ordered him to pay $9,000 for an unlawful lockout. The Tribunal accepted Nair's evidence that Mehta changed the locks on 3 March while she was away, concluded that this was a lockout within s 8, and applied the damages formula in s 10.

  1. The correct answer is B (Palpable and overriding error, because accepting that evidence is a finding of fact under Housen): Housen requires deference to findings of fact, and a statutory appeal on fact does not turn the appeal into a rehearing. Reasonableness is the judicial review standard and does not apply on a statutory appeal. Inadequate reasons may be a ground of appeal but do not change the standard.
  2. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because the meaning of a statutory term is a question of law under Housen): Under Housen, questions of law are reviewed for correctness, and the statutory appeal supplies that framework. The specialist character of the Tribunal does not alter it. A constitutional question is a different ground for correctness and is not needed here.
  3. The correct answer is C (Palpable and overriding error, because applying a test to facts is mixed, with no extricable error): Housen treats the application of a correct legal test to facts as mixed fact and law, reviewed for palpable and overriding error unless a legal error can be extricated. Mehta alleges none. Reasonableness is not engaged because the scheme is a court appeal.
  4. The correct answer is C (Silence leaves room for the common law, so a fairness duty applies with context-specific content): Legislative silence on procedure is not an exclusion of the common law. Courts supply procedural fairness to fill the gap unless the statute displaces it by clear words or necessary implication, as Knight and Baker show. The general word 'inquiry' does not purport to limit fairness, and a licence suspension is serious for the owner.
  5. The correct answer is B (Mandamus can compel a decision but not a permit, since the Registrar's discretion remains unexercised): Mandamus requires a public legal duty to act, a clear right to performance, a demand and refusal, no adequate alternative remedy and a favourable balance of convenience (Apotex). The Registrar's duty is to consider and decide the application, not to issue a permit, because the statute leaves suitability to the Registrar's judgment. The court can therefore order a decision within a set time but cannot dictate its content, and certiorari needs an actual decision to quash.
  6. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because a discretion must serve the statute's purposes and criticism is extraneous): Even wide statutory discretion is bounded by the purposes of the enabling law, a principle running back to Roncarelli v Duplessis. Punishing criticism has nothing to do with orderly marketing, so the refusal is not justified by its governing statute. Neither proof of malice nor a distinction between ministers and tribunals is required for judicial review.
Case CHB32

Extract from the fictional Community Housing Act: "12. An administrator shall grant a housing subsidy to an applicant who meets the eligibility criteria in section 4." Ms Adeyemi, aged 68, applied and meets every criterion in section 4, as the ministry now concedes. The administrator refused her, relying solely on an internal policy excluding vehicle owners. Section 4 contains no vehicle criterion and no residual discretion. On judicial review, the court finds the refusal unreasonable.

  1. The correct answer is B (Remittal is the usual course, but the court may decide when only one outcome is possible): Vavilov treats remittal as the default, with an exception where remitting would serve no useful purpose because the result is inevitable. A mere likelihood of success is too low a bar. A court does not take over every decision it finds unreasonable.
  2. The correct answer is B (Mandamus requiring the grant, since eligibility is conceded and no discretion remains): Remitting with directions is defensible in general, but here the duty is mandatory and eligibility is conceded, so a further decision would be pointless. A declaration alone would not secure the benefit. The subsidy is not discretionary under section 12.
  3. The correct answer is D (The prior demand and refusal element, because she asked and was turned down): The application is the demand and the written refusal is the refusal. The duty and the right arise from the wording of section 12 and the conceded eligibility, not from the refusal. Balance of convenience is assessed separately on equitable considerations.
  4.
