The correct answer is C (Correctness, because a dispute over the line between two administrative bodies needs one final answer): Vavilov lists questions about the jurisdictional boundaries between two or more administrative bodies as a rule of law category reviewed for correctness. Two bodies each asserting a claim cannot both receive deference, so a court must determine the boundary itself. The expertise of either body is not the test.
  5. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because the board did not address a central argument that could have altered the outcome): A reasonable decision is responsive to the central arguments of the parties, and failure to grapple with a key submission can show the decision maker was not alert to the issues. The rule is not that every point needs a ruling, which is why the central, outcome-changing nature of this argument matters. Expertise is not a presumption that the board considered what its reasons omit.
  6. The correct answer is C (The Bill cannot be invoked, because Parliament expressly declared the Act operative despite the Bill): The Bill of Rights is an ordinary statute that Parliament may override by an express declaration that a law operates notwithstanding it. Section 40 is such a declaration, so the section 2(e) argument fails. The Charter's section 33 is a separate mechanism and has no bearing on the Bill, and a clear override leaves no room to treat the Bill as controlling or as a mere interpretive aid.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Examine the record and context to decide whether the refusal is justified without reasons): Where reasons were not required, the court looks to the record and the surrounding context to see whether the outcome can be justified. Absence of reasons does not alone make a decision unreasonable. The standard also stays reasonableness, since the lack of reasons does not change it.
  2. The correct answer is D (The officer retained the discretion, because consulting colleagues and manuals does not displace personal consideration): Consultation with colleagues and reference to internal guidance are ordinary features of administrative decision-making. What matters is that the officer personally weighed the evidence and stayed open to a different outcome, which the facts show. A suggestion from a senior officer is not dictation, and policy need not be published as regulation to be considered.
  3. The correct answer is D (State interference with a serious and profound effect on her psychological integrity, beyond ordinary stress): The test requires state-imposed interference that has a serious and profound effect on psychological integrity, going well beyond the ordinary stress and anxiety of a public proceeding. Reputation and economic loss are not protected interests under section 7 on their own, however well they are proved. A reasonable-person standard of any anxiety would lower the threshold the cases deliberately set high.
  4. The correct answer is A (He keeps the burden, but harsh consequences demand more justification from the decision-maker): The burden stays with the applicant and the standard stays reasonableness. Vavilov explains that the more severe the impact on the individual, the more the reasons must demonstrate that the decision-maker grappled with the consequences. A cursory decision that ignores the children is therefore vulnerable, but the stakes do not move the burden or the standard.
  5. The correct answer is D (A duty of fairness applies by default to administrative decisions affecting a person's rights or interests): Since Nicholson and Cardinal, the duty of fairness is a general common law duty owed by public authorities making administrative decisions that affect rights, privileges or interests. The old judicial and administrative labels no longer decide whether it exists; they only help set its content. The Charter's hearing guarantee under section 7 is much narrower.
  6. The correct answer is D (Relief is sought by a petition for judicial review, and the court grants the same remedies): British Columbia's Act removed the separate procedural forms: relief in the nature of the old writs is obtained by petition for judicial review, and the court can grant the remedies those writs provided. The English habit of pleading each writ separately no longer applies. The Provincial Court and the Court of Appeal do not supervise tribunals at first instance, while the Supreme Court does.
  7. The correct answer is A (One application for judicial review, in which the court may grant any of those remedies): The Act replaced the separate prerogative writ procedures with a single application for judicial review, on which the court can grant the old remedies as well as a declaration or injunction. The owner need not select a writ at the outset. An ordinary action is the wrong vehicle for reviewing a public body's exercise of statutory power, and the Act is not limited to appeals.
Case CHB33

Extract from the Northern Mines Act (fictional): s 14 gives the Resource Board authority over 'disputes arising from the closure of a mine site'. Section 22 gives the Labour Board authority over 'disputes arising from the termination of employment'. Kestrel Ridge Mining closed a site and dismissed 80 workers. The union filed a complaint with the Labour Board about the dismissals. The Labour Board declined jurisdiction, ruling that the dispute belonged to the Resource Board, and the Resource Board has stated that the complaint is for the Labour Board. Neither statute provides an appeal.

  1. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because the ruling fixes the boundary between the authority of two administrative bodies): A question about where the authority of one tribunal ends and another's begins is a rule of law category reviewed for correctness. Both boards cannot be right, so deference to either would leave the boundary unsettled. Expertise and the absence of an appeal route do not displace this category.
  2. The correct answer is A (Set aside the ruling and remit the complaint to the Labour Board for a hearing): Where a court finds that a tribunal wrongly declined authority, the usual course is to set aside the ruling and remit so the tribunal can perform the task the legislature assigned to it. A court ordinarily does not decide the merits itself or direct a particular outcome. Refusing relief on discretionary grounds fits poorly where the other board has already said the dispute is not its own.
  3. The correct answer is A (Defer to the evidentiary finding under reasonableness while deciding the boundary question afresh on correctness): Correctness governs the boundary question itself, but that category concerns the legal line between the boards. Factual findings made on the evidence are still approached with deference, and reviewing courts do not reweigh evidence absent exceptional circumstances. The court therefore decides the legal question afresh on the facts as reasonably found.
  4. The correct answer is B (Disclosure of the medical report and a chance to respond before the decision is made): The importance of the decision, the lack of an appeal and reliance on adverse evidence point to a right to know and answer the case. The Baker factors do not demand a trial-type hearing, because the dispute turns on a document that can be answered in writing. Reasons given afterward cannot replace the chance to respond before the decision.
  5. The correct answer is C (A delegate of statutory power cannot sub-delegate it without authority from the enabling statute): Power conferred on a named body must be exercised by that body unless the statute permits delegation, so rates set by an unauthorized sub-delegate are made without authority. Fairness and bias concerns are separate, and rate setting of this kind is closer to rule-making than adjudication. Courts will review whether an internal delegation was lawful, so organization is not beyond scrutiny.
  6. The correct answer is C (Statute makes the Crown vicariously liable for torts its servants commit in the course of employment): Crown liability and proceedings legislation abolished the old immunity and makes the Crown liable in tort, including vicariously for its servants acting in the course of employment. No petition of right or prior review is needed. The driver's personal liability does not exclude that of the Crown.
  7. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because the question is the jurisdictional boundary between two administrative bodies, requiring a single answer): Vavilov identifies the determination of jurisdictional boundaries between two or more administrative bodies as a rule of law category reviewed for correctness. The decision-makers cannot both have exclusive authority, so the legal system needs one answer. Expertise and ambiguity do not displace that category, and the subject matter of the complaint is not what triggers it.
  8. The correct answer is B (Certiorari is unavailable because the club's decision is private, though contract or membership law may apply): Judicial review and its prerogative remedies reach the exercise of public power, not private associations running their own affairs. Incorporation under provincial legislation does not turn a club into a public decision-maker. Mr. Okafor's route lies in contract or the law of voluntary associations, so reliance on the Baker duty fails even though his interest is important.
  9. The correct answer is D (A general estimate is not a specific assurance to her, so no expectation arose): A generic processing estimate is neither specific nor unqualified, and it is not directed at her case. The doctrine therefore does not apply, though long delay may be addressed through other tools such as mandamus. The explanation-only and proof-of-viewing options both assume an expectation that never arose.
  10. The correct answer is A (Palpable and overriding error, because the finding is factual and appellate standards apply here): Naming fact in the appeal clause lets the court review facts, but only on the appellate standard of palpable and overriding error. Extending the appeal to fact does not raise the intensity of review to correctness. The tribunal's competence matters under reasonableness, which is not the framework once an appeal is granted.
  11. The correct answer is C (It may still hear the moot application, weighing adversarial context, judicial economy and its proper role): Under Borowski, mootness is a doctrine of judicial discretion, not a bar to jurisdiction. Once the live controversy is gone, the court asks whether an adversarial context persists, whether hearing the case serves judicial economy, and whether doing so respects its proper law-making role. Consent of the parties is not the test.
  12. The correct answer is B (Mandamus, available where a clear duty exists, a demand was made, and delay is unreasonable): Where a public body has failed to act, mandamus orders it to perform its public legal duty, subject to the Apotex conditions including unreasonable delay without satisfactory justification. Certiorari quashes an existing decision, and prohibition restrains future action; neither compels a decision. A stay addresses a pending proceeding, not an application that was never decided.
  13. The correct answer is B (The analysis moves through steps that do not logically connect, so no rational chain exists): Internal rationality concerns whether the reasoning is rational and logical on its own terms, so a chain of steps that do not follow from one another is the paradigm failure. A misread statute and ignored evidence are failures to respect external legal and factual constraints. Harshness alone is relevant to the stakes but is not itself an internal flaw.
  14. The correct answer is D (No, because the flaw must be sufficiently central or significant, not merely peripheral to the merits): A reviewing court intervenes only where the decision has serious shortcomings, not for minor misstep or a flaw that does not touch the reasoning or outcome. The court is not looking for perfection. Palpable and overriding error is the appellate standard, whereas this is a judicial review governed by reasonableness.
  15. The correct answer is C (Fairness applies because the resolution targets one identifiable person and is administrative despite council's elected status): The legislative label attaches to decisions of general application, such as bylaws, not to a decision about one person's permit. Under Knight the trigger is an administrative decision affecting an individual's rights, privileges or interests, and the elected character of the body does not change that. The requirement that the function be judicial or quasi-judicial no longer governs after Nicholson and Cardinal.
  16. The correct answer is D (Ask if notice was reasonably sent, considering his own duty to keep his address current): The court asks whether the steps taken were reasonable to bring the case to the person's attention, and an applicant's own duty to keep contact details current is a legitimate part of that assessment. Proof of actual receipt is not the test. A bare mailing without regard to the circumstances is too thin, and the adequacy of notice is a fairness question, not a reasonableness one.
  17. The correct answer is B (Statutory discretion must be exercised for purposes connected to the statute, not extraneous political motives): Roncarelli v Duplessis holds that a discretion granted by statute cannot be used for reasons foreign to the statute, and the file notes show an extraneous motive. Fairness and legitimate expectations may also arise, but the facts point squarely at improper purpose, and no delegation is described.
  18. The correct answer is A (It cannot constitutionally bar superior court review of whether the board acted within its authority): The constitutional guarantee of superior court supervisory jurisdiction means a legislature cannot immunize a tribunal from review of whether it stayed within its lawful authority, however clear its wording. The notion that explicit language suffices is a common error, and review does not depend on a Charter breach or on procedural fairness alone.
  19. The correct answer is D (The exclusion rebuts the Conway presumption, so the tribunal cannot decide the s 7 argument): The Conway presumption that a tribunal with power over questions of law can apply the Charter is rebuttable. The legislature may withdraw that power by clear statutory language, and a plain exclusion of constitutional questions does so. Priya is not left without a remedy, because she can raise s 7 in a court, but the tribunal itself cannot decide it, whatever the general reach of the Charter.
  20. The correct answer is A (Section 1 rarely saves a section 7 breach, and administrative convenience does not justify one): A breach of section 7 can in theory be justified under section 1, but the Supreme Court has said this will be rare and confined to exceptional conditions such as national emergencies. Cost and administrative convenience are not enough to override the right to life, liberty and security. Vavilov concerns standards of review, not Charter justification, and section 33 is not a way of curing a breach.
  21. The correct answer is D (It decides for itself whether the process was fair, weighing the Baker factors, not applying reasonableness): Procedural fairness is not subject to the Vavilov standard-of-review analysis, which governs the merits of decisions. The court asks whether the process met the level of fairness the circumstances required, using the Baker factors. The panel's views on its own process may inform that inquiry but do not attract deference.
  22. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because the reasons do not show real attention to the children's interests): Baker requires that the interests of affected children be well identified and defined and that the decision-maker be alert, alive and sensitive to them. A bare assertion that they were considered shows no engagement, so the reasoning is not justified. The interests are an important factor, but they do not automatically outweigh everything else.
Case CHB34

Mei-Ling Zhao seeks judicial review in the Federal Court of a visa officer's refusal of her study permit. The officer's reasons and her application form are in the certified tribunal record. Her counsel proposes to file an affidavit containing: (a) her statement that she was never told the officer doubted her funds; (b) a university letter dated after the decision; and (c) an immigration consultant's opinion that her funds met the threshold.

  1. The correct answer is D (Her statement that she was never told of the funds concern, which goes to procedural fairness): Evidence outside the record is generally excluded, but an exception allows evidence about procedural defects not apparent from the record, such as a failure to disclose a concern. The later letter and the consultant's opinion go to the merits and would invite the court to reweigh the evidence. A certified record does not bar all affidavits.
  2. The correct answer is A (Serve a written request under the Federal Courts Rules for the officer to transmit the material): Under the Federal Courts Rules, an applicant may serve a written request asking the tribunal to transmit the material relevant to the application in its possession, and the tribunal then sends a certified copy. Discovery and subpoenas are tools of actions and trials. The applicant need not wait for the respondent to produce the record.
  3. The correct answer is A (Disregard the new explanations because later rationalizations cannot supplement the officer's own reasons): Reasonableness review under Vavilov examines the decision-maker's own reasons and the record, so after-the-fact justifications from others cannot fill gaps in the reasons. Respondents may file limited affidavits, for example on background or process, so a blanket ban is too broad. Judicial review is not a trial that permits fresh merits evidence.
  4. The correct answer is D (Whether an informed person, viewing the matter realistically and practically, would reasonably apprehend bias): Under the test in Committee for Justice and Liberty, a court asks whether an informed person, viewing the matter realistically and practically, would reasonably apprehend bias. The applicant's own feelings do not decide the question, and proof of actual bias is not required. English formulations of the test are not the Canadian one.
  5. The correct answer is A (No duty attaches to starting the proceeding, since the later hearing lets him respond fully): The notice of hearing only initiates a proceeding, and the later hearing with disclosure and cross-examination gives Mr. Gagnon a full chance to respond. Courts generally do not impose a prior hearing on the decision to start a proceeding. Reputational harm from the notice is addressed by the later process, and finality is not the test.
  6. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, asking whether the decision proportionately balanced s 7 protection against the statutory objectives): Where a discretionary decision limits a Charter protection, review is for reasonableness through the Doré proportionality framework, which Vavilov did not displace. The decision-maker must balance the protection with the statutory objectives. Correctness is reserved for constitutional questions about the validity or scope of laws, and fairness review concerns process rather than the substantive limit.
  7. The correct answer is B (Holistically and in context, asking whether the reasoning is intelligible, transparent and justified): Vavilov directs courts to read reasons holistically and contextually, and not to hunt for flaws or insist on the form of a court judgment. Administrative reasons written for specialist parties need not cite every precedent. Deciding the correct meaning first would be a correctness exercise, which reasonableness review rejects.
  8. The correct answer is C (The duty to consult does not extend to the law-making process, so the Nation's argument fails): In Mikisew Cree (2018 SCC 40) the Supreme Court held that the duty to consult is not triggered by the legislative process, because of parliamentary sovereignty and the separation of powers. Courts will not supervise how a legislature develops legislation. Executive actions applying the legislation later, such as granting a permit, can still trigger the duty.
  9. The correct answer is A (A detailed scheme does not clearly oust fairness, so disclosure depends on the Baker factors): Detailed statutory steps do not oust the common law unless the legislature's intention to do so is clear. The auditor's report was the case against the Society and loss of registration is serious, so the Baker factors strongly favour disclosure so the Society can respond. An access to information request is a parallel statutory route and does not replace the fairness entitlement.
  10. The correct answer is D (A meaningful level of fairness was owed, because the decision targets one company's alleged misconduct): Although cabinet acts, the order is aimed at a single company on stated grounds of misconduct, which makes it resemble adjudication rather than general policy. The licence is its only source of revenue, so the stakes are high. A claim that cabinet is beyond review, or that its policy role caps fairness at a minimum, ignores the individualized and punitive character of this decision.
  11. The correct answer is B (It does not reweigh the evidence and defers to the panel's factual findings absent exceptional circumstances): On reasonableness review, courts do not reweigh evidence or interfere with factual findings absent exceptional circumstances. Preferring one expert over another is the panel's task. Palpable and overriding error belongs to statutory appeals, and the substantial evidence test is American, not Canadian.
Case CHB35

Extract from a federal Removal Review Act, s 9: 'An officer may remove a person from Canada after reviewing the file.' Mr Alvarado, a refused refugee claimant, faces removal to a country where credible human rights reports document torture of opposition activists like him. The officer reviewed the file, relied on a confidential intelligence summary that Alvarado never saw, and rejected his sworn account as not credible without meeting him.

  1. The correct answer is D (Yes, since removal to a serious risk of torture engages security of the person): Section 7 protects everyone physically present in Canada, regardless of citizenship, and removal to a substantial risk of torture can engage security of the person. The harm being inflicted abroad does not defeat this, because Canada's decision to remove is the state act. The statute need not mention the Charter for it to apply.
  2. The correct answer is A (Disclosure of enough of the case to respond, and an oral hearing where credibility is central): Fundamental justice is applied in context, and where the stakes are as grave as torture the individual must know the case to be met and have a meaningful chance to answer it. Where a serious issue of credibility arises, a decision on paper alone is not enough. The Charter sets a floor that statutory text cannot lower, yet it does not demand the full apparatus of a criminal trial.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quash the removal decision and remit it to a different officer for a fresh redetermination): The usual remedy for a procedurally flawed administrative decision is to set it aside and send it back to the decision-maker, here a different officer, to be made fairly. The breach lies in how the discretion was exercised, so striking down the whole Act is unnecessary. A court does not ordinarily take over the merits or grant status, and damages would leave the unfair order standing.
  4. The correct answer is D (Binding precedent on the provision's meaning is a legal constraint that the member contradicted): Vavilov lists relevant precedent among the legal constraints on a decision maker. A contrary interpretation that ignores a binding judgment is untenable, not merely unexplained. The conflict does not change the standard to correctness, and no court pre-approval is ever required.
  5. The correct answer is A (Legislative decisions of general application ordinarily carry no common law duty to give reasons): Procedural fairness is concerned with decisions affecting identifiable individuals, and legislative decisions of general application, including bylaws, generally attract no common law duty to give reasons or to hold a hearing. Delegation of authority does not turn a bylaw into an individual adjudication. Effects on property values are shared by the whole district.
  6. The correct answer is C (Irreparable harm if removed, and that the balance of convenience favours granting the stay): A stay pending judicial review follows the three part test from RJR-MacDonald: a serious issue, irreparable harm and the balance of convenience. Having shown a serious issue, he must still prove the other two parts. Requiring probable success or exceptional circumstances sets a higher bar than the test demands.
Case CHB36

A provincial securities commission panel chaired by Gordon Faulkner is hearing allegations that adviser Mina Rao misled investors. Two other panel members sit with him. During a break, Faulkner tells a reporter: 'These advisers are all crooks, and I expect to find the same here.' Rao's counsel learns of the remark the next morning, before closing submissions. Faulkner's questions at the hearing have otherwise been balanced.

  1. The correct answer is A (Whether a reasonable, informed observer would apprehend that he cannot decide the matter fairly): Canadian law asks what an informed person, viewing the matter realistically and practically and having thought it through, would conclude. The English real danger test is not the Canadian standard, and requiring proof of actual prejudgment sets the bar too high. Statements made outside the hearing room can be as telling as those made in it.
  2. The correct answer is C (The remark prejudges this case, so later balanced questioning cannot restore the appearance of impartiality): The words 'I expect to find the same here' announce a conclusion about this very case before the evidence closed, which is prejudgment rather than a general policy view. Once a reasonable apprehension arises, good behaviour afterwards does not erase it. Statements made to the press are plainly relevant to how a fair-minded observer would see the panel.
  3. The correct answer is A (The decision is set aside and the matter returns to an unbiased panel, despite the evidence): A decision tainted by a reasonable apprehension of bias is not saved by the strength of the evidence, because the defect goes to the integrity of the process rather than to its result. Courts do not speculate about what an unbiased panel would have done. Quashing and a fresh hearing before a differently constituted panel is the ordinary remedy.
  4. The correct answer is C (Judicial review is generally confined to the record before the regulator, with narrow exceptions): Judicial review is not an appeal or a rehearing, and the court reviews the decision on the material the decision-maker had. Exceptions are limited, for example general background or evidence of a procedural defect. The appellate fresh evidence approach and qualified expert opinion on the merits do not open the door.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

Case CHB37

Extract from the (fictional) Mine Safety Act, s 12: 'The Chief Inspector may recommend to the Minister that a mine manager's certificate be cancelled for unsafe practices. The Minister may cancel a certificate on that recommendation.' Ms. Dalgleish manages a gypsum mine in Nova Scotia and is employed by a private company. After an inspection, the Chief Inspector sent the Minister a report alleging repeated ventilation lapses. Ms. Dalgleish was not shown the report. The Minister cancelled her certificate, which she needs to supervise underground work.

  1. The correct answer is A (The relevant relationship is with the Minister as regulator, not with her private employer): The relationship factor looks at how the decision-maker stands to the affected person. The Minister's power to cancel creates a direct regulatory relationship with Ms. Dalgleish, whatever her arrangements with her employer. Private employment would matter only if her real complaint were against the company.
  2. The correct answer is A (Cancellation removes the certificate she needs to work, a serious effect on her interests): Loss of a certificate required to supervise underground work seriously affects her livelihood, which is the core of the third Knight factor. Reputational harm is relevant but is not the strongest basis. The word may in the statute does not by itself create a hearing right, and not every final decision calls for an oral hearing.
  3. The correct answer is C (Though preliminary, the report shaped the cancellation, so she should have seen its findings and replied): The Chief Inspector's report is a preliminary step, but preliminary steps matter when the final decision-maker relies on them and the person is affected. The Minister cancelled on the strength of the report, so fairness required that Ms. Dalgleish know the case and respond. A later decision does not cure a failure to disclose what shaped it.
  4. The correct answer is A (Set the decision aside and return it to the officer for a fresh determination): Where the flaw is in the reasoning and more than one outcome is possible, the matter goes back to the decision maker. The legislature entrusted the decision to the officer, not the court. Supplying better reasons for the same result would let the decision be rescued by the court.
  5. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, because the presumption applies on judicial review and Housen standards belong to statutory appeals): Without a statutory appeal, the matter proceeds by judicial review and the presumption of reasonableness applies. The Housen standards are tied to appeals and do not transfer to review of a mixed conclusion. Correctness is reserved for the Vavilov categories, none of which is engaged by a settled test applied to medical evidence.
  6. The correct answer is D (It will ordinarily refuse to intervene before the process ends, absent exceptional circumstances): Courts discourage premature applications that fragment administrative proceedings, and a party should first raise the objection with the member and let the process finish. Sarcastic remarks, even if recorded, rarely amount to exceptional circumstances. The bias complaint is preserved and can be reviewed after the tribunal decides.
  7. The correct answer is A (It fails, because review asks whether the sanction is justified, not whether it is ideal): Reasonableness review looks at whether the panel's sanction is justified by the statutory purposes and the facts, and the court does not substitute its preferred penalty. The panel's reasons engage the relevant factors. Such decisions remain reviewable, but only for the kind of flaw Vavilov describes.
  8. The correct answer is C (He failed to exercise the statutory discretion himself, simply endorsing the staff recommendation): A decision-maker may take advice from staff, but must bring a genuine mind to the decision. Signing recommendations without reading the file is rubber-stamping, which abdicates the discretion. Staff drafting advice is not a delegation of the decision, and nothing in the facts shows prejudgment or a missing hearing right.
  9. The correct answer is D (Likely reasonable: regulatory inspection of business premises attracts a reduced expectation of privacy): Section 8 applies to business premises, but the expectation of privacy in a regulated commercial setting is lower than in a home. Warrantless inspections authorized by statute for compliance purposes are generally reasonable. The analysis would change if the inspection became a criminal investigation, which the facts rule out, and a warrant is not required for every search.
  10. The correct answer is D (The regulation is irrelevant to the Act's purposes or contrary to them, given its presumed validity): Regulations are presumed valid, and challengers must show the regulation is inconsistent with the objective of the enabling statute or irrelevant to it. It is not enough that the regulation is unwise or less effective than alternatives, because courts do not weigh the merits of policy. A fee cap respecting amounts payable falls within the stated power.
Case CHB38

Extract from the fictional Dental Technologists Act, s 31: "The Discipline Committee may reprimand a member whose public conduct undermines public confidence in the profession." Mr. Sethi, a registered dental technologist in Regina, publishes an opinion piece accusing the College registrar of mismanaging member fees. It names no patient and discloses no confidential information. His written submissions to the Committee rely on his freedom of expression. The Committee reprimands him, stating in two paragraphs that any criticism of the College is conduct undermining confidence, and does not mention his expression rights.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness review applying the Doré proportionality framework to the Committee's balancing of rights and statutory objectives): A discretionary decision that engages a Charter protection is reviewed for reasonableness through the Doré proportionality framework, as Vavilov confirmed. The Oakes test is the court's tool for testing legislation, not an individual exercise of discretion. Ignoring the Charter and reviewing only the logic would leave the protected interest unexamined.
  2. The correct answer is A (Identify the statutory objectives at stake, then weigh how the reprimand impairs expression against advancing them): Doré requires the decision-maker to consider the statutory objectives and then ask how the Charter protection is best preserved in light of them, which is a proportionate balancing. A short two-stage test borrowed from legislation review is not enough. Treating the protected interest as secondary to the regulator's own view defeats the point of balancing.
  3. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because the reasons show no proportionate balancing and equate criticism with misconduct): Mr. Sethi raised his expression rights, and the Committee's reasoning treats all criticism as misconduct, which leaves no room for weighing the protected interest. A mild sanction can matter when proportionality is assessed, but here nothing in the reasons shows that any balancing occurred. The defect is a failure of reasonableness, not a breach of procedural fairness.
  4. The correct answer is C (The board may admit it, but must weigh its reliability given the absence of cross-examination): Administrative bodies are not bound by strict court rules of evidence, and hearsay is generally receivable. The real question is the weight to give it, especially where the opposing party could not test the speaker. A rule requiring corroboration in every case would reimpose technical rules the statute relaxes.
  5. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, because the committee interprets its home statute and no correctness category is engaged): A professional body's interpretation of its home statute is presumptively reviewed for reasonableness, whatever the consequences for the individual. The court does not first decide the correct meaning, since that would restore correctness by another route. Self-regulation does not create a separate category.
Case CHB39

Extract (fictional federal statute), Harbour Port Safety Act, s 9: The Director of Port Security may refuse a restricted-area pass to an applicant the Director is satisfied poses a security risk. Facts: Delphine Ouellet, a Halifax crane operator, was refused a pass by the Director, a federal official acting under the Act. The refusal was emailed to her and she read it the same day, 3 March. She was never told what information the Director relied on.

  1. The correct answer is D (Yes, because the Director exercises a power conferred by an Act of Parliament): The statutory definition looks to the source of the power, a federal Act or a prerogative order, and not to the title, structure or procedure of the decision-maker. A single official exercising a statutory power qualifies. The adjudicative-function and hearing arguments describe features that are irrelevant to the definition.
  2. The correct answer is B (The Federal Court, which has exclusive original jurisdiction over relief against a federal board): Because the Director is a federal board, s 18 gives the Federal Court exclusive original jurisdiction, so the provincial superior court cannot hear the application even though the parties are local. The Federal Court of Appeal only has original jurisdiction over the bodies listed in s 28, and a port official is not among them.
  3. The correct answer is C (She is late and needs an extension, because time ran from the refusal itself): Under s 18.1(2) the thirty days run from when the decision was first communicated, not from the later arrival of reasons. Thirty-six days is therefore out of time, but the Court may fix or allow further time before or after the period ends. The belief that the limit is rigid and unextendable fails because the statute expressly grants that discretion.
  4. The correct answer is B (Review the Board in the Federal Court of Appeal and the Minister in the Federal Court): Each decision-maker is assigned to a court by its own status: the listed Board to the Federal Court of Appeal under s 28, and the unlisted Minister, as a federal board, to the Federal Court under s 18. Sharing a dispute does not allow one court to hear both. The provincial superior court has no jurisdiction over a federal Minister.
  5. The correct answer is B (Procedural discretion yields to fairness, and a serious credibility issue likely called for an oral hearing): A statutory power to control procedure does not oust the common law duty of fairness, and the Baker factors, including the importance of the benefit, set the content. Where credibility is central and the file contradicts the claimant, a written process alone will often fall short, as Singh illustrates. A rule that every benefits case needs an oral hearing overstates the law.
  6. The correct answer is D (A full appeal favours less procedure at first instance, since unfairness can be corrected later): The statutory scheme factor looks at the decision's place in the overall process. A genuine appeal with a full hearing allows defects at the first stage to be repaired, which lowers the procedure owed there. The first-stage duty still exists, so it is not removed, and it does not rise because an appeal exists.
  7. The correct answer is B (Delegated public power exercised by a statutory regulator, so the decision is open to judicial review): Professional colleges exercise public regulatory power delegated by statute to protect the public, so their decisions, including discipline, are subject to judicial review. Self-governance describes how the power is organized, not whether courts may supervise it. The College is a statutory regulator rather than the Crown, and an internal appeal does not oust the courts.
  8. The correct answer is A (Yes, the coalition meets the flexible factors of seriousness, genuine interest and an effective means): Public interest standing under Downtown Eastside is a flexible, purposive test: a serious justiciable issue, a genuine interest, and a reasonable and effective way to bring the issue to court. The coalition satisfies each factor on these facts. Direct personal effect is not required, a declaration is not limited to holders of private rights, and no co-applicant is needed.
  9. The correct answer is C (Review should be refused in discretion because the appeal is an adequate alternative remedy): Judicial review is discretionary, and courts ordinarily decline it where a statutory appeal can address the complaint, including a fairness complaint, unless exceptional circumstances are shown. An appeal on law and fact is adequate here. The court keeps its jurisdiction, so the claim that jurisdiction is removed is wrong, and the court is not obliged to rewrite the petition as an appeal.
  10. The correct answer is D (It may decline, as no live controversy remains and deciding would be an abstract exercise): Declarations are discretionary, and courts decline to decide moot or hypothetical issues unless the Borowski factors justify doing so. No live controversy, no adversarial stakes and no other proceeding remain, so a ruling would be abstract. A declaration needs no separate cause of action, but that does not oblige a hearing, and certiorari also needs something to quash.
  11. The correct answer is C (A palpable and overriding error, meaning an obvious error that affected the outcome of the decision): Vavilov confirms that where a statutory appeal exists, Housen governs and factual findings are reviewed for palpable and overriding error. That threshold is demanding, and disagreement about how evidence was weighed is not enough. Reasonableness and patent unreasonableness describe judicial review standards, which do not apply on a statutory appeal.
  12. The correct answer is C (Yes: waiver needs knowledge, so she may object promptly after discovery, within review time limits): The duty to object at the earliest opportunity presupposes that the party knew, or could reasonably have known, the facts. A concealed interest discovered after the decision can still ground an application, which must be brought promptly and within any applicable time limit. Proof that the shares changed the result is not required.
  13. The correct answer is A (The reasons fail to engage a central submission, so the decision lacks justification and intelligibility): Vavilov requires reasons to grapple with the key issues and central arguments raised, and silence on a central submission undermines justification. A court does not set rates itself, the older presumption that everything was considered cannot cure a gap in reasons, and an oral hearing is a procedural matter.
  14. The correct answer is A (A distant professional acquaintance does not displace the presumption of impartiality, so no reasonable apprehension arises): Adjudicators are presumed impartial, and the threshold for displacing that presumption is high; a remote professional connection of this kind does not meet it. Requiring recusal for every past association would make small professional communities unworkable. A ruling for counsel's client is not needed to show bias, but nothing here supports an apprehension to begin with.
  15. The correct answer is B (She likely has private interest standing, since a nearby owner's property interests are directly affected): A nearby owner whose use and enjoyment of property may change because of the variance has a private interest that courts usually treat as direct enough. Not attending the hearing may count against her on discretion, for example on delay or relief, but it does not take away standing. Because she has her own affected interest, she does not need public interest standing.
  16. The correct answer is B (Tribunals control their own process and may limit party status to those sufficiently directly affected): Absent statutory direction, a tribunal may decide who participates as a party, and a distant resident's general concern differs from the direct stake of adjacent owners. Public interest standing is a judicial doctrine for access to courts, not a right to party status before an agency. No separate hearing on status is required.
  17. The correct answer is C (Reviewable and unreasonable, because discretion must be exercised for purposes consistent with the statute): Roncarelli v Duplessis holds that no discretion is unlimited, and a decision-maker must act for purposes consistent with the statute. Refusing a licence to punish criticism is an extraneous purpose. Words conferring broad discretion do not remove the court's role, and the description of a licence as a privilege does not change this.
  18. The correct answer is A (May be reasonable, because the council explained the change by pointing to a legal development): A decision maker may change course if it justifies the change, and a new legal rule is a sound justification. The vice is unexplained departure, not departure itself. A bare statement of preference, however, would not discharge the burden of explanation.
  19. The correct answer is B (Remit the matter to the regulator to reconsider, as the legislature chose it to decide): Vavilov makes remittal the usual remedy because the legislature chose the administrative decision-maker, not the court, to decide the merits. A court decides the matter itself only in limited cases, such as where only one outcome is possible. Writing the rate order would take over the regulator's role, and so would dictating its formula.
  20. The correct answer is C (No, because clear statutory words can limit common law fairness where no constitutional guarantee applies): The legislature may displace common law procedural fairness by clear words, and 'no person is entitled to appear in person' is unmistakable. Quasi-constitutional status belongs to human rights legislation, not to the common law of fairness, and only a constitutional guarantee such as Charter s 7 could override the text. That a credibility finding was made does not create an oral hearing right against express contrary language.
  21. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, because a tribunal's reading of its own procedural rules attracts deference absent a fairness breach): She challenges the substantive reading of a procedural rule, not the fairness of the hearing, so the ordinary Vavilov analysis applies and the presumption of reasonableness holds. Whether a process was fair is assessed by the court under the Baker factors without a deference standard, but that is not the ground argued. Correctness is limited to the Vavilov categories.
  22. The correct answer is A (Correctness, because constitutional questions require a final and determinate answer consistent with the rule of law): Vavilov lists constitutional questions as a category where the presumption of reasonableness is rebutted, so correctness applies. The tribunal's statutory power over questions of law explains why it may rule on the Charter, not how that ruling is reviewed. Doré applies to discretionary decisions that limit Charter protections, not to a ruling on whether a procedure is constitutional.
  23. The correct answer is B (The delegated power covers licences, so an outright prohibition falls outside the statute's scope): A challenger must show that a regulation is inconsistent with the objective or scope of its enabling statute, and the power here concerns licences, not banning a lawful activity. Harshness is a policy matter, and cabinet owes no fairness hearing when making general legislation. Regulations are presumed valid, so the burden does not fall on cabinet.
  24. The correct answer is C (Councillors deciding policy may hold views; only a closed mind to the submissions disqualifies): A council acting in a legislative or policy capacity is expected to bring opinions to its decisions, so the standard is far less strict than for an adjudicator. The question is whether she approached the hearing with a closed mind, unwilling to be persuaded. A financial interest would be a separate ground of disqualification, not a precondition for this one.
  25. The correct answer is D (Pressure to adopt a position compromises the panel's decision-making independence, making the process unfair): Consolidated-Bathurst allows consultation on legal and policy questions but only where members remain free to reach their own conclusion. An explicit threat of a performance review removes that freedom. Courts rarely probe deliberations, yet here the pressure was stated openly, so the consistency rationale does not save the process.
Case CHB40

Extract from the Taxi Licensing Act, s 12: 'The Registrar may issue a taxi plate licence to an applicant the Registrar considers fit.' Registrar Okafor adopted a guideline stating that applicants with two or more traffic convictions in five years 'will be refused.' Sunil Dhillon has two convictions, both for minor speeding, and provided evidence of a clean record since. Okafor refused his application, writing that the guideline 'leaves me no room.' Before deciding, she also received a call from the Minister's chief of staff, who told her to refuse all applications this month.

  1. The correct answer is C (She fettered her discretion by treating a guideline as a binding rule excluding individual consideration): Section 12 gives the Registrar a discretion to be exercised case by case. A guideline may structure that discretion, but a decision-maker who treats it as a rule that leaves no room to consider the individual has fettered the discretion. Convictions are plainly relevant to fitness, and nothing in the extract requires guidelines to be made by regulation, so the other grounds fail.
  2. The correct answer is C (The refusal is vulnerable, since discretion vested in the Registrar must be exercised by her): A statutory discretion must be exercised by the person on whom Parliament or the legislature conferred it, and a decision dictated by someone else is not a genuine exercise of that discretion. Ministerial accountability does not authorize a minister's staff to direct the outcome of an individual licensing decision. Signing the refusal and giving reasons does not cure the problem, and no proof of a threat is needed.
  3. The correct answer is B (No fettering arises, since a guideline may structure discretion if she remained open to exceptions): Decision-makers may adopt policies and guidelines to promote consistency, provided they remain willing to hear arguments for departing from them. Here Okafor considered the guideline and the applicant's explanation, so the discretion was exercised rather than fettered. Guidelines need not be regulations, and Vavilov rejects the idea of jurisdictional questions attracting correctness review.
  4. The correct answer is C (It is reviewable for reasonableness, though policy breadth and the Cabinet context shape its intensity): Orders in council made under a statutory power are reviewable, and the political character of Cabinet does not make them immune. Reasonableness review applies, but a broad policy mandate and the fact that Cabinet decisions rarely come with detailed reasons shape how searching the inquiry is, since the record may supply the justification. Correctness is reserved for the Vavilov categories, and Vavilov does not make formal written reasons mandatory in all settings.
  5. The correct answer is A (Appellate standards apply, so correctness governs the pure legal question, despite the privative clause): Where the legislature has provided a statutory appeal to a court, Vavilov directs the court to apply appellate standards from Housen, so extricable legal questions are reviewed for correctness. The privative clause cannot displace an express appeal right, and expertise no longer drives the choice. The central importance test belongs to judicial review without an appeal.
  6. The correct answer is C (The duty to consult does not govern law-making, though later executive decisions may trigger it): Mikisew Cree (2018) held that the duty to consult does not apply to the legislative process, which is protected by parliamentary sovereignty and the separation of powers. Later executive decisions made under the new law, such as permits, can still trigger consultation. Fairness duties likewise do not attach to law-making, and the committee stage is part of that process.
  7. The correct answer is C (Allowing counsel in name only undermines fairness, because representation must permit real participation): A right to counsel that is limited to silent presence does not give the party the assistance that makes the right meaningful. A chair may regulate the conduct of counsel, for example by controlling repetition, but Ewa's limited English and the stakes make real advocacy important here. Access to the evidence beforehand is not the only way the right can be emptied of content.
  8. The correct answer is D (Look to the record and context to discern the rationale, then test it): Vavilov explains that where reasons are not required and none were given, the court examines the record and the surrounding context to understand the decision's rationale and assess whether the outcome is reasonable. Missing reasons do not change the standard to correctness. Nor do they by themselves make the decision unreasonable.
  9. The correct answer is B (No, because the reading departs from the defined text, and policy concerns cannot rewrite the provision): A reasonable interpretation must be justified by the text, context and purpose of the provision, and a statutory definition is a strong textual constraint. The tribunal added an aunt to a closed list of relatives on the strength of a policy preference. Purposive reasoning cannot override the words the legislature chose, and the problem is the result of the interpretation, not the length of the reasons.
  10. The correct answer is D (Not engaged: section 7 restricts state-imposed deprivations and does not generally guarantee a benefit entitlement): Section 7 has not been read as a general source of positive entitlements to state benefits, and an asset-limit scheme of general application does not itself inflict a deprivation on her. The Supreme Court has left room only for exceptional cases of positive obligation, which these facts do not show. The view that security of the person covers only criminal or quasi-criminal process is wrong for a different reason: security of the person is not limited to criminal contexts.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The decision-maker ignored central contradicting evidence, which undermines the rationality of the finding): A failure to meaningfully account for central evidence that runs counter to a conclusion can lead a court to lose confidence that the decision-maker was actually alive to the matter. That is different from requiring a list of every exhibit, and it does not invite the court to reweigh the record. Plausibility is a factual assessment, not a legal question.
  2. The correct answer is D (Written reasons were owed because the decision is significant to him, so their absence breaches fairness): Baker holds that in some circumstances, including where the decision has an important significance for the individual, fairness requires written reasons. A statute's silence does not displace the common law duty. The requirement is contextual rather than universal, which is why a sweeping rule for all decisions misstates the law.
  3. The correct answer is A (Council's decision targets one developer's existing rights, so a duty of fairness applies despite the vote): What matters is the nature of the decision, not the body or its voting procedure. A resolution revoking a single permit affects the specific rights of one named party, so it is an individualized decision to which the duty of fairness attaches, with content set by the Baker factors. Express statutory wording is not needed, and no precondition of requesting reasons exists.
  4. The correct answer is B (Decline to hear it, since the question will soon be decided in cases with full evidence): Even where the issue is important and the Crown contests the claim, the court may decline to hear a moot case when judicial economy and its proper role point elsewhere. Here the same question will be decided shortly on a proper record, so deciding it in a moot case adds little. Mootness leaves a discretion, so dismissal for lack of discretion is wrong.
  5. The correct answer is A (Set the decision aside, because the court cannot fill a central gap by supplying reasons): Vavilov directs the court to examine the reasons the decision maker actually gave, not to reach the same outcome by a different route. A plausible result does not save a decision that fails to grapple with a central submission. The focus on acceptable outcomes, and the idea that silence changes the standard, are both mistaken.
  6. The correct answer is B (Fairness required notice that the panel would use that knowledge, and a chance to respond): A specialized tribunal may bring expertise to its task, but where it relies on specific knowledge that goes to a contested point and the parties have not had a chance to address it, fairness calls for notice and a response. The distinction between law and fact does not drive the duty. Asking for reasons later does not repair the lost opportunity to be heard.
Case CHB41

Extract from the fictional Pension Review Act, s 12: "The Board shall reduce a pension supplement where the claimant has, in the Board's opinion, received income from employment during the claim period." Walter Dubois, 68, received a supplement. The Board cut it after finding he earned $9,000 in seasonal repair work. In writing, Walter argued that the $9,000 was reimbursement of tool costs, not earnings, and that the Board had left supplements unchanged for similar claimants. The Board's two-paragraph decision states only that he "received income from employment" and cites s 12.

  1. The correct answer is B (The reasons do not engage his central argument that the payment was reimbursement, not earnings): Walter's characterization of the payment as reimbursement was the central issue under the section, and the Board did not respond to it. Failing to account for a central argument undermines the justification of the decision. An oral hearing is not required by reasonableness review, quoting the statute or its history is not required, and the section's opinion wording does not mean that income has one correct judicial meaning.
  2. The correct answer is A (It confers discretion, but the decision must still be justified, transparent and intelligible): Discretionary language shapes the range of reasonable outcomes but does not oust reasonableness review. The older subjective-satisfaction and patent unreasonableness approaches no longer define review after Vavilov, and a court does not re-decide the question on correctness.
  3. The correct answer is A (Quash and remit to the Board, because the unresolved question is for the Board): The usual remedy after an unreasonable decision is remittal, because the legislature assigned the question to the Board. Substitution is reserved for cases where only one outcome is possible. Here the facts are disputed, so the court should not decide them in the Board's place.
  4. The correct answer is B (Since reasons were given, adequacy is assessed within reasonableness review of the decision's justification): Vavilov treats a failure to give required reasons as a fairness matter, but where reasons exist the question is whether they justify the outcome, which is part of reasonableness review. A correctness-like standard governs whether a duty to give reasons arose, not the quality of reasons actually delivered. Discretion does not remove the need for a justified, intelligible decision.
  5. The correct answer is C (The published policy and assurance can ground a legitimate expectation of the stated notice period): A published policy joined to an assurance is a typical basis for a legitimate expectation of procedure, which feeds into the content of fairness under Baker. The policy is not a regulation, so a departure does not automatically void the review; the court asks what fairness required in light of the expectation. Intent to mislead is not part of the test.
  6. The correct answer is A (As the minister's reasons, because signing beside the memo adopts its analysis): Vavilov recognizes that reasons can take many forms, including memoranda and briefing notes, and a decision maker may adopt staff analysis as the basis for the decision. The court reads the decision with that analysis in view. Receiving staff advice is not bias, and nothing requires the minister to author the reasons personally.
  7. The correct answer is D (It is a core policy decision grounded in fiscal and social factors, not reviewable in negligence): Core policy decisions, those based on public policy considerations such as economic, social and political factors, are immune from negligence liability so long as they are neither irrational nor made in bad faith. The immunity is a feature of the nature of the decision and not of the body making it. Governments can owe duties of care, and discretion alone does not confer immunity.
  8. The correct answer is A (No duty applies, because a broad policy choice of general application is legislative in nature): A policy applying to every college and every future applicant is general and polycentric, so the common law does not require a hearing for those affected. Baker factors are used to set the content of a duty that exists; they do not create one for legislative-type decisions. Individual hardship does not change the character of the decision.
  9. The correct answer is C (The court decides for itself whether the procedure was fair, without deferring to the tribunal): Fairness is a matter of law that the court assesses itself, looking at the Baker factors and the stakes for Mr Teale. The tribunal may choose its procedures, but whether they met the fairness standard is not reviewed on a deferential basis. Nothing confines fairness to judicial review when an appeal on law is already before the court.
  10. The correct answer is D (Mandamus, because a clear public legal duty exists, was triggered, and has not been performed): Mandamus compels performance of a public legal duty where there is a clear right to performance, the duty has been triggered, and no adequate alternative exists. The word 'shall' and the completed filing meet those requirements. Certiorari needs a decision to quash, prohibition restrains action rather than inaction, and the Federal Court can order a federal official to act.
Case CHB42

Extract from the fictional Wildlife Permits Act: 's 4(1) The Director may issue a hunting permit to an applicant who satisfies the Director that the applicant has completed an approved safety course. (2) The Director shall consider each application on its merits.' Marcus Oyelaran, who completed an approved course, applied. The Director refused, citing a ministry policy memo directing staff to refuse all out-of-province applicants. The memo was never published.

  1. The correct answer is C (A discretion limited by the statute's own conditions and requiring merits-based consideration of each application): Section 4(1) gives a discretion ('may'), limited by the condition that the applicant satisfy the Director on the safety course, and section 4(2) requires merits-based consideration. The Director is neither obliged to issue every permit nor free to add conditions of personal choosing. Treating the power as a bare duty ignores the word 'may' and the Director's role in assessing whether he is satisfied.
  2. The correct answer is D (No, a policy cannot override the statute's conditions or replace individual consideration of each application): A policy may guide a discretion, but a rigid rule refusing a whole class of applicants fetters the discretion and ignores the duty to consider each application on its merits. The out-of-province rule also adds a condition the Act does not contain. An unpublished memo is not law, so it cannot override the statute even if it serves a reasonable objective.
  3. The correct answer is B (An administrative guideline, lacking the statutory authority and formal enactment that give regulations binding force): A guideline or policy memo is delegated legislation only if it is made under a power to make binding rules and enacted in the required form. This memo is an internal direction to staff with no statutory basis as a regulation, by-law or order. Its general wording does not turn it into law.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness applies, because the clause cannot oust review and the presumption already supplies deference): After Vavilov, the presumption of reasonableness already reflects respect for the legislature's choice to assign the decision to an administrative body, so a privative clause does not raise the standard to patent unreasonableness. Such a clause also cannot remove the court's constitutional supervisory role. Nor does it create a correctness category for statutory interpretation.
  5. The correct answer is A (The guideline cannot override the statute, so the refusal is unlawful because it adds a condition): Soft law cannot amend or contradict a statute; a decision that applies a stricter guideline over the Act's clear criteria is unlawful, not just unreasonable. Deference to policy applies within the room the statute leaves open. Reliance is irrelevant when the guideline itself is outside the decision-maker's authority.
Case CHB43

Extract from the fictional Water Safety Regulation, s 5: the Inspector shall inspect each municipal water system annually and shall issue a compliance order where a system fails a prescribed standard. In 2024, Inspector Paul Nguyen found Riverbend's system failing a standard but issued no order for 14 months. A ministry budget decision had earlier reduced inspection staff. Residents, including Farah Idris, became ill from contaminated water and sue the Crown in negligence.

  1. The correct answer is B (The budget cut is immune core policy, while Nguyen's failure to enforce is operational): Allocation of funds among competing priorities is a core policy decision, but carrying out a mandatory inspection and order duty under a regulation is operational implementation that is assessed under ordinary negligence principles. The regulation's use of shall leaves Nguyen no policy discretion to defer the order.
  2. The correct answer is C (Breach of the Regulation is no tort itself but is evidence on the standard of care): Canadian law does not recognize a nominate tort of breach of statutory duty, and negligence per se is an American doctrine. A breach of a regulation can nonetheless be relevant evidence in deciding whether a reasonable inspector would have acted differently. The regulatory standard informs, but does not replace, the common law inquiry.
  3. The correct answer is A (The Crown can be liable, with fault apportioned, if its inaction materially contributed to the harm): More than one wrongdoer can contribute to a single injury, and the Crown is exposed if its negligence was a material contributing cause, with fault shared under apportionment legislation. A third party's concurrent fault does not erase the regulator's duty. The budget decision is immune, so liability rests on the inaction and not on the policy decision.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reasons need not be lengthy, but the decision must be justified and intelligible given the stakes): Vavilov says the reasons must show a justified, intelligible and transparent decision, and that the justification required rises with the impact on the individual. Courts must not fill gaps by crafting their own reasons for the decision-maker. Formal length is not required, but a central omission can make the decision unreasonable.
  5. The correct answer is B (Not a federal board, since its selection power flows from bylaws, not statutory authority): Federal incorporation gives corporate capacity but not statutory decision-making power over members, so a body acting under its own bylaws is not exercising jurisdiction or powers conferred by an Act of Parliament, which the Federal Courts Act definition of federal board requires. Public function and federal funding may matter to whether a body is amenable to judicial review at all, but they do not supply the statutory source of power, and no prerogative power is involved.
  6. The correct answer is C (A statutory appeal route exists, so parallel judicial review is unavailable to that extent): Where Parliament provides an appeal from a federal tribunal, review proceeds through that appeal route and a separate application for judicial review is not available to the extent the appeal exists. The Federal Court does have judicial review jurisdiction, so the choice of court is not the problem. Seeking leave first does not revive judicial review, and the decision's finality is not what prevents it.
  7. The correct answer is A (The court follows it, because the legislature may prescribe the applicable standard of review): Vavilov begins from legislative intent: where the legislature has prescribed the standard or created a statutory appeal, the court respects that choice and the presumption of reasonableness does not apply. The direction is not a factor to be weighed, and expertise is not a precondition. Courts depart from a legislative direction only where the rule of law demands it, as with constitutional questions.
  8. The correct answer is B (Section 11 is not engaged, as the penalty is regulatory in purpose and risks no jail): The inquiry asks whether the matter is criminal by nature or carries a true penal consequence, looking at the penalty's purpose and effect. A scaled penalty aimed at compliance, without imprisonment, is regulatory. Deterrence alone does not make it penal, since regulatory schemes also deter, and there is no dollar figure at which a penalty automatically becomes penal.
  9. The correct answer is D (Relying on undisclosed material breaches the right to know the case to be met): A party must be told the case against them and given a chance to answer material that influences the outcome. Staff authorship does not exempt a report that carries adverse allegations. A later appeal does not always cure a breach, and the duty to disclose does not depend on a prior request.
  10. The correct answer is C (The committee misused its discretion, denying him a meaningful chance to participate despite documented illness): Granting an adjournment is discretionary, but the discretion must be exercised consistently with the duty of fairness, and refusing one can deny the right to be heard. Genuine documented illness with little prejudice to anyone makes the refusal hard to defend. There is no automatic right to an adjournment, and patent unreasonableness is no longer a Canadian standard.
  11. The correct answer is B (Implemented international law is a legal constraint, so contradicting the convention text is likely unreasonable): Vavilov identifies international law as part of the legal context that bounds a reasonable interpretation, especially where the statute implements a treaty. A reading that contradicts the implemented text cannot be justified. Past practice does not cure a conflict with the instrument the Act was written to give effect to.
  12. The correct answer is B (Whether the board can decide questions of law, which presumptively includes applying the Charter): Conway holds that a tribunal with authority to decide questions of law presumptively has authority to apply the Charter, and a remedy follows if it is one the tribunal can grant under its statutory scheme. The legislature need not list Charter powers, because that power is implied from the power over law. Tribunals lack inherent jurisdiction, so the inquiry turns on statutory authority and not on equitable powers.
  13. The correct answer is A (Parliament may delegate broad powers to executive bodies, because it can amend or withdraw them later): In Canada the legislature may delegate wide powers to executive bodies because it stays sovereign and can recall or amend the delegation. The American requirement of an intelligible principle limiting the delegate is not a Canadian constitutional rule. Delegation to ministers is routine and does not depend on any Crown prerogative.
  14. The correct answer is C (It signals the intended process, so fairness is assessed within an inquisitorial model): A legislature's choice of an inquisitorial or adversarial model is part of the statutory context and a strong signal of the process intended. Fairness is then asked within that model, not by importing an adversarial template from elsewhere. Only clear statutory language can exclude the duty of fairness, and the fairness question is not turned into reasonableness review.
  15. The correct answer is C (Good faith, meaningful consultation with accommodation where warranted, but no veto and no duty to agree): Haida Nation holds that the duty applies to asserted but unproven rights and may require accommodation, depending on the strength of the claim and the seriousness of the potential harm. It does not give the Aboriginal group a veto, and the Crown is not bound to reach agreement. Here the Crown shared studies and changed the route, which is the kind of response the duty contemplates, so insisting on consent misstates the law.
  16. The correct answer is B (Apply appellate standards, so correctness governs the Board's answers to pure questions of law): Where the legislature provides a statutory appeal to a court, Vavilov directs the court to use appellate standards, so questions of law are reviewed for correctness. The central importance exception matters only on judicial review, where reasonableness is otherwise presumed. A choice of specialized decision maker does not reduce the force of the appeal right.
  17. The correct answer is B (Ask whether fairness required reasons, then judge their adequacy within reasonableness review of the decision): Whether a duty to give reasons arises is a procedural fairness question under Baker. Where reasons are given, Vavilov treats their justification, transparency and intelligibility as part of reasonableness review of the merits. The two questions are distinct and the court may need the record to assess a thin note.
  18. The correct answer is D (Circular reasoning, where the conclusion is assumed in the premise offered to support it): Vavilov lists circular reasoning among the failures of internal rationality: each finding depends on the other and nothing independent supports either. The other flaws describe different fallacies, such as an artificial two-way choice or a sweeping generalization, and none of them is what the committee actually did here.
  19. The correct answer is B (Extrinsic non-public material relied on adversely must be disclosed so the applicant can respond to it): Where an officer relies on extrinsic material that is not publicly available and that is significant to the outcome, the applicant must be told of it and given a chance to respond. Without disclosure Elena could not know the case to meet. Public general reports are treated differently because the applicant can find and answer them, which is why a duty to disclose every consulted document overstates the rule.
  20. The correct answer is D (The appeal exists only as the Act provides, so she must first obtain leave): A right of appeal is a creature of statute and carries whatever conditions the statute attaches. Judicial review would ordinarily be refused where a statutory appeal is available. The leave requirement in the wording is not limited to factual questions.
  21. The correct answer is B (Decline to hear the application, since an adequate appeal exists and no exceptional circumstances are shown): Courts ordinarily refuse judicial review when an adequate statutory appeal is available, absent exceptional circumstances, as C.B. Powell confirmed. An allegation of bias is not itself exceptional, and the independent tribunal can rehear the matter afresh. The supervisory power exists but is exercised with restraint where the legislature has provided a suitable route.
  22. The correct answer is D (Yes, because the Crown knows of a potential claim and plans conduct that could harm it): Under Haida Nation, the duty is triggered when the Crown has real or constructive knowledge of a potential Aboriginal claim and contemplates conduct that might adversely affect it. Proof of title is not a precondition, which is why waiting for a declaration would defeat the purpose of the duty. Nor must the claim be in litigation, or the harm be total extinguishment.
Case CHB44

Extract from the Dental Professions Act: s 21(1) The Inspector shall investigate any complaint and deliver a report to the Registrar. (2) The Registrar shall publish the report on the College website on receipt and may refer the dentist to the Discipline Committee. The Inspector cannot impose any sanction. Inspector Moreau investigated Dr Amrit Sandhu over billing complaints. Her report, finding probable overbilling, was published within a day, before Dr Sandhu saw it.

  1. The correct answer is C (A duty is owed, because publication gives the report serious practical effects on her reputation): Bodies that only investigate and recommend are usually outside the duty of fairness, but the exception gives way when the report itself seriously affects the person. Publication on receipt means the harm lands before any later hearing, so the recommendatory label does not decide the matter. Insisting on express statutory language confuses the common law source of fairness with legislated procedure.
  2. The correct answer is A (Notice of the allegations and a chance to respond before delivery, without an oral hearing): Fairness is flexible and is set by the Baker factors. An investigative stage that ends in a recommendation calls for notice and a chance to answer, not trial-type rights, because a full Discipline Committee hearing follows. Waiting until after publication fails because it leaves the reputational harm unaddressed when it matters.
  3. The correct answer is B (Publication on receipt already caused reputational harm that a later hearing could not reverse): A later process cures an earlier defect only where it fully replaces the flawed stage. Here the injury occurs at publication and the statute prevents withdrawal, so a hearing afterwards can vindicate her but cannot prevent the harm. Independence of the committee goes to bias, not to cure.
  4. The correct answer is A (Denying cross-examination breaches fairness, because the case depends on an untested account that she disputes): Where credibility is in issue and a person's livelihood and reputation are at stake, fairness generally gives the party an opportunity to test the key evidence. A summary offered by another witness does not let Beatrix probe the patient's own observations. Argument alone cannot replace testing a disputed account.
  5. The correct answer is D (Cabinet performs a broad policy function, so little or no procedural fairness is owed): A Cabinet decision to vary or rescind a regulator's order on petition is treated as a broad policy decision, and the Supreme Court has held that the procedural fairness owed to affected parties is minimal or absent unless the statute says otherwise. The adjudicative model and the Baker factors point toward more fairness, but they yield to the policy character of the function. Nothing in the statute adds procedural requirements.
  6. The correct answer is A (Certiorari, which quashes the decision and removes its legal effect from the outset): Certiorari quashes a decision already made, so it removes the cancellation and its effect.
