Prohibition looks forward and stops a body from acting, and mandamus orders performance of a public legal duty. Quo warranto concerns a person's right to hold a public office and has no bearing on the permit decision.
  7. The correct answer is B (Remit once more with guidance, because weighing the public interest is the regulator's task): Delay and repeated errors are relevant to whether to direct a result, but the court should not usurp a weighing the legislature gave to the regulator when more than one lawful outcome exists. Guidance in the reasons can narrow the next decision. Abuse of process is not engaged by successful review.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Harsher consequences demand a more rigorous justification, so ignoring central evidence points to unreasonableness): Vavilov explains that reasonableness is context sensitive: where a decision has a severe impact on an individual, the decision maker bears a heavier justificatory burden. Reasons that skip the core evidence on the feared harm fall short of that burden. The standard of review does not change, and the stakes shape the merits analysis, not merely the remedy.
  2. The correct answer is D (He was entitled to the substance of the adverse extrinsic material and a chance to respond): Fairness requires that an applicant know the case to meet, so adverse extrinsic evidence the officer relied on must be disclosed in substance, with a chance to respond. Protection of an informant's identity can limit the form of disclosure but not the duty to convey the substance. An oral hearing before an independent adjudicator is more than the written process in this setting requires, and the onus on the applicant does not displace fairness.
  3. The correct answer is C (Section 1(a) of the Bill of Rights protects enjoyment of property against deprivation without due process): Section 1(a) of the Canadian Bill of Rights expressly recognizes enjoyment of property and the right not to be deprived of it except by due process, unlike section 7 of the Charter. Section 2(e) concerns hearings generally and does not itself name property. Canada has no Australian-style compensation clause, and unwritten principles do not create a free-standing property guarantee.
  4. The correct answer is C (The court will likely wait for the appeal, since delay and cost are not exceptional circumstances): Courts ordinarily refuse judicial review while an adequate administrative remedy remains available, as C.B. Powell explains, and exceptions are rare. Cost and delay are common to all appeals, and the Review Division's power to hear evidence and reverse means it can address the alleged bias. An allegation of bias alone does not excuse skipping the appeal, because the appeal can cure it.
  5. The correct answer is D (Thirty days from when the decision was first communicated to the party directly affected): Section 18.1(2) sets thirty days from the time the decision or order was first communicated to the office of the Deputy Attorney General or to the party directly affected. On these facts forty-two days passed, so Pablo needs an extension. The sixty day period belongs to some provincial regimes and does not apply in the Federal Court.
  6. The correct answer is C (No, because the court retains discretion to decline a moot case after weighing the Borowski factors): Mootness is a discretionary doctrine, not a jurisdictional limit, so the court decides whether to hear a moot case after weighing the adversarial context, judicial economy and its proper role. Parties cannot waive it, and agreement alone does not bind the court. Treating mootness as a lack of jurisdiction is also wrong, because the court retains discretion to hear such cases.
  7. The correct answer is B (Ms. Stewart was owed notice and a hearing; Mr. Reyes ordinarily had no such entitlement): An office held during good behaviour or removable only for cause carries a right to notice and an opportunity to respond before removal, while a holder at pleasure ordinarily has no such entitlement. The distinction rests on the terms of the office rather than on reputation alone. A fixed term does not turn removal into a purely contractual matter.
  8. The correct answer is D (It may decline, because new issues should generally be raised first before the tribunal): A reviewing court has a discretion to refuse to consider an issue not raised before the tribunal, because the tribunal should first have the chance to apply its expertise and build a record. The rule is discretionary, so a bar on any new point overstates it, and a legal question is not automatically heard simply because it is legal.
  9. The correct answer is D (Uphold it, because the flaw is peripheral and does not undermine the reasoning overall): A reviewing court looks for a flaw sufficiently serious to go to the heart of the reasoning, not for minor errors. The decision here stands on sound analysis. A court does not perfect the reasons by replacing them.
  10. The correct answer is B (The court may still hear it, weighing an adversarial context, judicial economy and its proper role): Under Borowski, a moot case is not automatically dismissed: the court first asks whether a live controversy remains and, if not, exercises discretion guided by the adversarial context, judicial economy and the court's proper law-making role. Mootness is not jurisdictional, and the Crown cannot control the outcome by consenting. The possibility of repetition is only one consideration, so a mandatory hearing overstates the doctrine.
  11. The correct answer is D (The duty does not extend to the legislative process, so the consultation claim fails): In Mikisew Cree 2018 SCC 40 the Supreme Court held that the duty to consult is not triggered by the development of legislation, including a minister's steps to prepare a bill, because of parliamentary sovereignty and the separation of powers. The Nation's treaty rights remain protected because the resulting law must still respect section 35. Treating bill preparation as ordinary executive conduct, or placing it on the spectrum, misreads that holding.
  12. The correct answer is A (Notice was too short and sent by an unreliable method, so she could not respond): Fairness requires adequate notice and a real chance to be heard, which very short notice by an unverified channel does not provide, given that eviction affects housing. Speed can justify a streamlined process but not one that prevents participation. Statutory authority for expedited hearings does not displace the common law minimum.
Case CHB45

Extract, Pharmacy Licensing Act: s 14, the Registrar may revoke a licence on written reasons; s 15, a person aggrieved may appeal to the Appeals Committee within 30 days. The Act is silent on reconsideration. On 3 March, Registrar Hélène Tremblay signed and sent reasons revoking Samir Haddad's pharmacy licence. On 10 March she noticed that her reasons cite the wrong date for an inspection, and she received a new inspection report from the pharmacy.

  1. The correct answer is C (It applies flexibly: a final decision generally cannot be reopened without statutory authority): Chandler holds that once an administrative decision-maker has made a final decision, it is generally functus officio, but the principle is applied more flexibly than for courts. Narrow exceptions exist, such as slips or statutory authority to reopen. Treating it as inapplicable to tribunals is a common error, and it does not wait for the appeal period to lapse.
  2. The correct answer is A (She may correct it as a slip in expressing her intention, without reconsidering the merits): Chandler recognizes that a decision-maker may correct clerical errors or errors in expressing its manifest intention, because doing so does not reopen the merits. A blanket bar on any amendment overstates finality. The Appeals Committee's power to vary is separate, and a mere correction does not become a new decision.
  3. The correct answer is D (She cannot reopen to weigh new evidence; the remedy lies in the statutory appeal): A final decision on the merits is not reopened simply because new evidence emerges, and without statutory authority the proper route for the pharmacist is the appeal to the Appeals Committee. Having an appeal route does not make the Registrar's decision non-final. Consent of the affected party cannot confer a power the statute withholds.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, because the presumption covers administrative decisions and personal stakes do not displace it): Vavilov makes reasonableness the presumptive standard for administrative decisions, and the stakes for the individual do not create a correctness category. The older contextual factors such as expertise and importance no longer set the standard. Only a legislative direction or a rule of law category rebuts the presumption, and none appears here.
  5. The correct answer is B (The rule of necessity lets her decide, because no one else has authority, with disclosure): Where the statute names a single decision-maker and provides no alternate, the rule of necessity permits an interested person to decide so that the statutory scheme does not fail. She should disclose the interest and decide with care. Consent cannot be a precondition, and delegation needs statutory authority that the Act expressly withholds.
  6. The correct answer is A (It asks whether the regulation is consistent with the statute's purpose and scope, not its wisdom): Regulations are reviewed on a reasonableness standard that asks whether the regulation is consistent with the purpose and scope of the enabling statute, starting from a presumption of validity. Courts do not assess policy wisdom or rebalance economic harms. Importing the English formula or a correctness standard misstates the Canadian approach.
  7. The correct answer is B (The union, which must show the departure is serious enough to make the decision unreasonable): Vavilov places the burden on the party challenging the decision to show that it is unreasonable, and the shortcoming must be sufficiently serious. Mere disagreement with the outcome or a different result from earlier awards does not meet that threshold, and the onus does not shift to the decision maker.
  8. The correct answer is D (It is not binding law, and the court asks whether the interpretation is reasonable in context): Under Vavilov the reviewing court asks whether the decision is reasonable, which requires justification consistent with the statutory text, context and purpose. A published interpretation is relevant context but does not bind a court or remove its role. Authority to issue notices does not turn them into regulations.
Case CHB46

Extract, Taxi Licensing Act, s 12: 'The Licence Officer may refuse to renew a taxi licence where satisfied that the holder is no longer of good character.' Mr. Okafor has held a licence for twelve years without a complaint. Acting on an anonymous letter alleging fraud, the Officer refused renewal by a one-line email stating only 'renewal denied, character concerns', and never mentioned the letter to him.

  1. The correct answer is C (Notice of the fraud allegation and a fair opportunity to respond, in writing or in person): A twelve-year holder facing a fraud allegation stands to lose a livelihood, which calls for a participatory right to know the allegation and answer it. A trial-type hearing goes beyond what fairness requires, because the duty is flexible. The renewal and privilege arguments ignore that existing holders are owed more protection than first-time applicants.
  2. The correct answer is A (Written reasons are generally required for serious personal consequences, and a bare conclusion falls short): Where a decision significantly affects an individual, fairness generally calls for reasons, and a one-line conclusion does not allow Mr. Okafor to understand or challenge the outcome. The duty does not depend on a statutory requirement or on the body being adjudicative. Reasons first offered on judicial review come too late to meet the duty.
  3. The correct answer is B (Set aside the refusal and remit the matter for a fresh decision with a fair process): The merits of the character finding have not been decided fairly, so the proper course is to quash and return the matter to the decision-maker. Ordering renewal would take over a discretion the statute gives the Officer. A fresh application is not an equivalent remedy, because it does not restore his renewal status or cure the breach.
  4. The correct answer is D (Credibility is central and liberty is affected, so fairness requires an oral hearing): Following Singh, where a serious issue of credibility arises and a significant interest such as liberty is at stake, a decision on paper alone is unfair. Silence in the statute does not remove the common law duty, and administrative bodies are not limited to written process when the facts depend on whom to believe.
  5. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because the wording confines cancellation to unpaid fees and nothing justifies reading it wider): The text is a binding constraint, and a decision maker cannot expand a power beyond what its words and context permit without a justification that the reasons here do not offer. Purpose informs the reading but cannot override clear limiting language. The patent unreasonableness threshold is not part of current Canadian law.
  6. The correct answer is A (Whether an informed person would reasonably perceive the funding arrangement as compromising independence in this context): Because the statute is silent, the common law applies in a flexible way, and the question is how a reasonably informed observer would see the board's financial dependence given what it does. Proof of actual interference is not required, since the concern is institutional. The superior court standard is too demanding for a tribunal.
  7. The correct answer is C (Dubois's participation likely breaches the rule that those who decide must hear, absent statutory authority): Fairness requires that the people who decide a matter have heard the evidence, particularly when credibility turns on watching witnesses, unless the statute provides otherwise. A transcript is a poor substitute for seeing credibility assessed. A majority of hearing members does not cure the participation of a member who did not hear, but panels are not frozen if the statute allows a replacement.
  8. The correct answer is D (The time limit is likely directory, so lateness alone does not void the award absent prejudice): Where a statute sets a time limit but attaches no consequence to missing it, courts usually treat the limit as directory, so a late award is not automatically a nullity. The court then asks whether the lateness caused real prejudice or unfairness, and Marta shows none. Reading shall as mandatory would punish the parties for the arbitrator's lapse.
  9. The correct answer is A (It does not select the standard, but it informs how the court assesses reasonableness in context): Vavilov removed the contextual analysis, so expertise no longer chooses the standard; the presumption flows from the legislature's decision to delegate. Expertise still helps the court understand the institutional setting when it asks whether a decision is reasonable. Treating it as wholly irrelevant overstates the change, and treating it as a standard-setting factor is the Dunsmuir habit.
  10. The correct answer is C (Absence of a privative clause does not displace the presumption of reasonableness for an administrative decision-maker): Under Vavilov, reasonableness is the presumptive standard for review of administrative decisions, including interpretations of a home statute, and it does not depend on a privative clause being present. Correctness applies only through an appeal right, a constitutional question, or another recognized rule-of-law exception. Expertise is no longer the basis for the presumption.
  11. The correct answer is D (The applicant union must show the decision is unreasonable because of a serious flaw): The party challenging the decision must show it is unreasonable, and the flaw must be sufficiently central or significant, not merely superficial. The tribunal need not prove its own decision. The court does not decide the correct reading first, because reasonableness review is not a contest between competing interpretations.
  12. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because the content of a constitutional obligation is a legal question the courts finally settle): Under Haida Nation and Vavilov, whether a duty to consult exists and how far it extends are legal questions reviewed for correctness. Only the adequacy of the consultation actually carried out attracts reasonableness review. Doré is a framework for Charter values in discretionary decisions and does not govern section 35 questions.
  13. The correct answer is B (Not automatically: the statute sets a minimum, and fairness may add oral process): A statute that provides some procedure sets a floor, and silence on other steps leaves room for the common law to supplement it. Baker confirms that an oral hearing is not always required, but a serious credibility dispute with high stakes can make one necessary. The tempting view that written process ousts the common law needs clear statutory language, which is missing.
  14. The correct answer is D (The bulletin's hedged wording weakens the expectation, so the court asks whether video was fair): A legitimate expectation needs a clear, unambiguous and unqualified representation, and the word usually qualifies this one. The question then reverts to what fairness requires, with the weakened expectation as one Baker factor. The college's procedural power does not remove that factor, and a bulletin that is not a regulation can still found an expectation.
  15. The correct answer is B (It likely stands, since a real pollution rationale exists and bad faith is not established): Bad faith invalidates a by-law only where the challenger proves an improper purpose, and a genuine environmental rationale shows a proper one even if a personal complaint also surfaced. A by-law general in form is not discriminatory merely because one owner is caught by it. Councils make legislative decisions and need not give reasons for by-laws.
  16. The correct answer is C (Removal and its hardship do not alone engage section 7 without a threat to security): Deportation of a non-citizen, even a long-term resident, does not by itself engage life, liberty or security of the person, and the disruption of family and community life is not enough. Section 7 would be engaged by something more, such as a risk of torture or serious harm on return. The Charter does apply to permanent residents, so the last view is wrong, and removal is not punishment merely because a conviction triggered it.
Case CHB47

Extract, Hollis Bay By-law 24-11, s 6: 'No person shall operate a food truck within 100 metres of a school between 7 a.m. and 5 p.m.' The Municipal Act authorizes councils to pass by-laws 'respecting the health and safety of residents'. Council passed the by-law after receiving a public health report on student nutrition and a traffic safety concern near schools, following a short debate. Luc Bergeron, whose food truck serves lunch outside Hollis Bay Secondary, applies for judicial review, arguing the by-law is unreasonable because it destroys his business and the health evidence is thin.

  1. The correct answer is A (Whether the by-law fits the statutory purpose and is one a reasonable council could pass): By-laws are reviewed for reasonableness: the court asks whether the by-law is within the statutory purpose and whether a reasonable council could have passed it. The court does not rebalance the evidence, substitute its own interpretation or demand written reasons, because councils make legislative decisions and answer politically.
  2. The correct answer is B (Little, since councils may act on policy judgment and need not build a formal evidentiary record): Courts reviewing by-laws give elected councils wide latitude, because councils weigh competing local interests and are accountable to voters. A by-law can be upheld even where its policy rationale is debatable, and no formal evidentiary record is required. Thin evidence matters only as part of a showing that no reasonable council could have passed it.
  3. The correct answer is A (It treats similar vendors unequally without clear statutory authority, exceeding the power delegated to council): Municipalities have only the powers delegated to them, and a by-law that treats similar businesses differently without express or clearly implied authority exceeds that delegation. This challenge goes to scope of power, so deference to council's policy judgment does not rescue it. Thin evidence, missing reasons and a traffic concern are weaker grounds, since by-laws need no record or reasons and safety falls within the Act's purpose.
  4. The correct answer is C (Give weight to the board's latitude, then ask if the ratepayer could meaningfully respond): A statutory power to set procedure is relevant and earns respect, but it does not oust the common law duty of fairness. The court asks whether, given the stakes and the issues, the ratepayer had a full and fair chance to present her case and answer the other side. Cross-examination is not mandatory in every hearing, and Vavilov governs substantive review, not procedure.
Case CHB48

Pelican Bay Energy Act (extract). Section 10: The Commission may hear and decide questions of law arising in an application. Section 11: The Commission may approve an application, refuse it, or approve it subject to conditions it considers necessary in the public interest. Westcoast Gas Ltd applies for approval to build a compressor station. The Sinclair Lake Nation says the station will affect its treaty harvesting rights and that the Crown has not consulted it. The Crown has done no consultation and says the Commission process will suffice.

  1. The correct answer is B (Yes, because section 10 power over questions of law covers whether consultation was adequate): A tribunal can assess the adequacy of Crown consultation only if its statute gives it that authority, expressly or by necessary implication. A general power to decide questions of law is a strong indicator that it does, because whether the duty was met is a legal question. The statute need not mention Aboriginal rights, and being able to assess consultation does not mean the tribunal owes the duty itself.
  2. The correct answer is C (Refuse approval or attach conditions requiring adequate consultation first, using its section 11 powers): A tribunal with authority to assess consultation can use only the remedies its statute gives it. Section 11 allows refusal or conditional approval, which can hold the project until consultation has happened. The tribunal does not take over the Crown's duty and cannot award damages, since neither power appears in the extract.
  3. The correct answer is A (The Crown may rely on the hearing, and the duty is met if it was adequate): The Supreme Court has accepted that the Crown may rely on a regulatory body's process to fulfil the duty, provided it tells the Indigenous group it is doing so and the process gives the depth of consultation the circumstances require. Here the Crown said so at the outset and the Nation participated with effect, so the question becomes whether the process was adequate. Full participation alone does not end that inquiry, and the Nation's consent to the method is not required.
  4. The correct answer is D (Its reasoning has a fundamental gap, since it never grappled with central evidence in the record): Reviewing courts do not reweigh evidence, but a decision is unreasonable where the reasoning fails to account for central evidence in a way that undermines its justification. That is a reasonableness defect, not a palpable and overriding error, which is an appellate standard. Deference to scientific expertise does not excuse silence on contrary evidence, and bad faith is not required.
  5. The correct answer is D (Review remains available, and the clause does not alter reasonableness as the presumptive standard): A privative clause does not oust the superior court's constitutionally protected supervisory role. Under Vavilov, reasonableness is the presumptive standard whether or not a privative clause exists, so the clause neither closes the door nor raises the standard to correctness. Patent unreasonableness and the narrow jurisdiction category belong to the pre-Vavilov framework.
  6. The correct answer is A (No, because credibility was central and the stakes high, so testing the evidence was owed): Where credibility is central and a livelihood is at stake, fairness calls for a real opportunity to test the evidence, which a written statement alone does not allow. Deference to an agency's procedural choices is only one Baker consideration and does not outweigh the importance of the decision. Revocation does not, however, require a full civil trial with discovery, so the content is high but not unlimited.
  7. The correct answer is B (To understand the reasoning in context, but not to supply reasons the regulator did not adopt): Reasons are read holistically and in light of the record, so the transcript can show what the member was concerned about. A reviewing court may not, however, fashion its own justification for a result that the decision-maker did not give. Nor does reasonableness review permit a fresh assessment of credibility.
  8. The correct answer is C ('Final' bars a statutory appeal but does not oust the court's inherent supervisory review): A finality clause indicates there is no further appeal, but courts retain their inherent power to supervise legality through judicial review. Courts do not treat such language as eliminating review of an administrative decision. The word does not create a statutory appeal, and review is not limited to bad faith or fraud.
  9. The correct answer is A (Decline to reweigh the evidence, and ask only whether the approval is justified): A reviewing court does not reweigh evidence or substitute its view on how competing planning values should be balanced. Council considered both the heritage and housing material, and the question is whether the result is justified within the statute. No rule ranks heritage above housing.
  10. The correct answer is D (The statute governs, so appointment at pleasure does not itself breach procedural fairness): Absent constitutional or quasi-constitutional requirements, a tribunal's independence is set by its enabling statute, and the common law does not add security of tenure. A government party appearing before a government-appointed board is the ordinary administrative setting. Evidence of actual interference in his case would raise a different claim.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Negligent misrepresentation, if proximity and reasonable reliance are shown, since estoppel cannot override statutory time limits): A claim in negligent misrepresentation is open if a special relationship and reasonable reliance can be proved. Estoppel cannot prevent the operation of a statutory requirement, and Canadian law recognizes procedural but not substantive legitimate expectations. Misfeasance needs deliberate unlawful conduct, not a mistaken answer.
  2. The correct answer is A (Judicial review is unavailable because the club exercises private contractual authority, not statutory public power): A club expelling a member under its own bylaws acts through private, contractual authority, and no statute confers the power. Judicial review supervises the exercise of state-conferred public power, so Maya's route lies in contract or association law. The impact on her reputation, or the use of the word bylaws, does not turn private governance into public power.
  3. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness is presumed, because the legislature delegated the decision to an administrative body): Vavilov starts from a presumption that reasonableness governs the merits of administrative decisions, because the legislature's decision to delegate signals respect for the decision-maker's role. The contextual inquiry of the pre-Vavilov cases was replaced by this presumption and a short list of exceptions. Correctness is the exception, not the starting point.
  4. The correct answer is C (No, misfeasance needs deliberate unlawful conduct and awareness of likely harm, not careless inaction): Misfeasance in public office is an intentional tort that requires deliberate unlawful conduct by the officer, with knowledge or reckless indifference that it is unlawful and likely to harm. Inattention that leaves a file in a drawer, even if unreasonable, shows carelessness rather than that state of mind. The duty and standard of care analysis belongs to negligence, and the tort reaches all public officers.
  5. The correct answer is C (The strength of the claim and the seriousness of the potential harm to the right): Haida Nation fixes the place on the spectrum by two things together: how strong the case supporting the right is, and how serious the potential adverse impact would be. Here both are modest, so the duty sits at the low end, likely notice and disclosure. Earlier consultation, the identity of the proponent and the type of right may inform the facts but are not the governing factors.
  6. The correct answer is A (The label is not decisive: serious loss of liberty requires disclosure and a chance to respond): Fairness today depends on the Baker factors and the effect on the individual, not on labelling the function judicial or administrative. Segregation is a serious deprivation of residual liberty, so the inmate should know the substance of the case and be able to respond. That does not amount to a full trial-type hearing, and prison decisions remain open to judicial review.
  7. The correct answer is C (Decisions of general policy application attract little or no fairness, so no hearing was owed): Legislative and general policy decisions sit far from the judicial model, so the nature-of-the-decision factor points to little or no procedural fairness. A broad impact on a class of practitioners does not convert rule-making into adjudication of one person's rights. Binding force alone does not create a right to be heard.
  8. The correct answer is C (The general Act prevails, since it overrides an inconsistent tribunal statute unless that statute declares otherwise): Section 32 of the Statutory Powers Procedure Act makes the Act prevail over an inconsistent provision in a tribunal's own statute unless that statute expressly says it prevails. The home statute here has no such declaration, so the reasonable notice standard governs and seven days of website posting for a landlord with no internet access may not meet it. The Act does not require the longer of two periods, and it is not described as quasi-constitutional.
Case CHB49

Mr. Tunde Adeyemi, a Nigerian national, claimed refugee protection in Canada. The Refugee Protection Division rejected his claim, finding him not credible because he gave inconsistent dates for his arrest. His counsel had filed a psychiatrist's report explaining that trauma disrupted his memory of dates, but the member's reasons never mention it. Mr. Adeyemi has applied for judicial review in the Federal Court.

  1. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, since nothing in the facts rebuts the presumption in favour of that standard): The presumption of reasonableness applies to the Refugee Protection Division, and the high stakes do not create a correctness category. Palpable and overriding error belongs to statutory appeals, not judicial review. Credibility findings are reviewed for reasonableness with considerable deference to the finder of fact.
  2. The correct answer is C (The silence is a shortcoming, because the report bore directly on the credibility finding): Reasons must show the decision-maker grappled with evidence that contradicts its key finding or explains the very inconsistency relied on. Here the report went to the heart of the credibility conclusion. The presumption that all evidence was considered weakens when pivotal evidence is left unmentioned, yet reasons need not catalogue every document.
  3. The correct answer is B (Generally refuse it, because review is confined to the record before the decision-maker): Judicial review is a review of the decision made on the record that was before the decision-maker, not a rehearing. Fresh evidence is admitted only in limited circumstances, such as background on a procedural fairness issue. Credibility is not reviewed for correctness, so the court has no reason to retry it.
  4. The correct answer is C (Acceptable, as the comments are advisory and the hearing panel keeps authority over the decision): Consolidated-Bathurst permits collegial consultation that improves consistency, provided the members who heard the case retain the power to decide and are not pressured. Advisory comments that the panel is free to reject meet that condition. Disclosure of the draft is required only if something new, such as fresh evidence, enters the process.
  5. The correct answer is D (The registrar breached an express statutory notice requirement, so the suspension is invalid for procedural unfairness): Where the enabling statute spells out a procedural step, failing to take it is a breach of a legislated duty, and that breach alone invalidates the decision. The common law duty is a default that fills gaps; it is not the source here. Procedural fairness is assessed by asking whether the process was fair, not by applying the Vavilov reasonableness standard.
  6. The correct answer is B (The duty was satisfied by genuine opportunities to comment, and does not require adopting submissions): A statutory duty to consult is measured by its wording and context, and ordinarily requires a genuine chance to be heard and a real consideration of views, not agreement. Legislative-type rule-making carries no general duty to give reasons. Two sessions plus written submissions can be adequate for an industry-wide measure.
  7. The correct answer is D (With credibility central and her security at stake, fundamental justice required an oral hearing): Under Singh, section 7 protects everyone physically present in Canada, and a refugee claimant's security of the person is engaged. Where credibility is in issue, fundamental justice requires an oral hearing, whatever the statute provides. Common law fairness does not displace the Charter, and written submissions cannot test a credibility finding.
  8. The correct answer is C (Unlawful, because decision-making authority effectively passed to members who did not hear the matter): Consultation is lawful when the hearing panel stays free to decide. Here a committee that heard nothing can change the reasons without the panel's consent, so the real decision is made by others. Procedural rules adopted by a regulator cannot displace the requirement that those who hear must decide, and the limit is not confined to findings of fact.
  9. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable, because the central finding has no support in the record, so the reasoning fails): The flaw is not brevity, since short reasons can suffice. It is that the stated basis for revocation finds no footing in the evidence. A court should not supply new reasons to rescue the outcome, so the possibility of another justification does not save the decision.
  10. The correct answer is A (Employment founded on contract is ordinarily governed by private law, with damages as the remedy): Where the relationship rests on a contract and damages for wrongful dismissal are available, private law ordinarily governs and no public-law duty arises. A promise of a hearing in the contract is a matter of contract, not a precondition to the duty's existence. Public employment alone does not generate a hearing right.
  11. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness, because the presumption covers legal questions decided by the body administering its own enabling instrument): Vavilov applies the presumption of reasonableness to administrative decision-makers generally, including on pure questions of law about their own governing instrument. The legal character of the issue is not an exception. Nor does the presumption depend on specialized expertise, since it rests on the legislature's decision to delegate.
  12. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because she alleges the wrong legal test, which is an extricable question of law): On a statutory appeal, appellate standards apply: a legal error, including an extricable error in the legal test within a mixed question, is reviewed for correctness. Palpable and overriding error would govern the application of the test to the facts, which she does not challenge. Expertise-based deference and patent unreasonableness belong to older judicial review approaches.
  13. The correct answer is C (No, Vavilov abandoned the category and treats such questions through the presumption of reasonableness): Vavilov abandoned the category of true questions of jurisdiction, finding it difficult to apply and based on a narrow view of what tribunals may decide. Questions about a tribunal's authority under its statute are now reviewed through the presumption of reasonableness unless a recognised exception applies. The Court rejected a sliding scale of deference as well.
  14. The correct answer is D (It is one factor informing the content of fairness, and may require the promised conference): In Baker, the legitimate expectations of the person challenging the decision are one of several non-exhaustive factors shaping the content of the duty of fairness. A clear promise of a procedure can lead the court to require that procedure. The factor does not replace the others, and it is not limited to the trigger or the remedy.
  15. The correct answer is C (The court reads the record for context but cannot build the missing justification itself): Vavilov allows a court to consult the record to understand a decision, but it forbids the court from fashioning its own reasons to make up for a fundamental gap. Supplementing reasons by asking whether any basis could support the result was the older approach. Inadequate reasoning ordinarily leads to remittal, not to the court deciding the permit.
  16. The correct answer is D (A single judge may hear it if Divisional Court delay would cause a failure of justice): The Act lets a single judge of the Superior Court hear an application where the case is one of urgency and the delay required for an application to the Divisional Court is likely to involve a failure of justice, which fits a demolition scheduled within days. The Divisional Court is the ordinary forum. Nothing in the Act requires an internal appeal first, and the Court of Appeal does not hear review applications at first instance.
  17. The correct answer is B (They are not constitutionally required, and independence depends on the tribunal's function and statute): The Valente guarantees were developed for courts. Ocean Port rejected a general constitutional requirement of that kind for tribunals, which are creatures of statute and vary widely in function. A municipal licensing committee therefore owes only the independence that its statute and the common law, adapted to its role, require.
  18. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness, because interpreting its home statute on a rate question engages no correctness category): Vavilov makes reasonableness the presumptive standard and removes the old idea that a true question of law is reviewed for correctness. A rate question confined to one utility does not raise the broad legal-system concern that defines the central importance category. Privative clauses no longer drive the standard, so that distractor reflects the pre-Vavilov framework.
Case CHB50

Maple Ridge Mining Ltd. holds a provincial permit to operate an open pit mine. On 3 March the provincial Environmental Appeals Commissioner suspended the permit for 90 days after finding unreported discharges. Maple Ridge has applied for judicial review in the Superior Court and asks for a stay of the suspension. It employs 140 workers in a small town and says it would lose a $4 million contract. The Commissioner's evidence shows runoff reached a nearby creek.

  1. The correct answer is D (A serious issue to be tried, a low threshold unless the claim is frivolous): RJR-MacDonald sets a low first-stage threshold: the judge asks only whether the application is neither frivolous nor vexatious, without a detailed merits review. The strong prima facie case standard is reserved for mandatory orders or stays that would effectively end the dispute. Predicting success under Vavilov is the work of the hearing judge, not the stay motion.
  2. The correct answer is C (Give it real weight, since enforcing a statute is presumed to serve the public interest): A public authority enforcing its own statute is presumed to act in the public interest, so the court gives that interest real weight when balancing convenience. The presumption can be rebutted, and the applicant may still win by showing a stronger public interest in suspending enforcement. The Commissioner carries no burden to prove harm to the creek before the interest is weighed, and the weight is not decisive by itself.
  3. The correct answer is A (Financial loss can be irreparable where the applicant cannot later recover it from the regulator): Irreparable harm looks to the nature of the harm, not its size. Purely financial loss is usually compensable, but it becomes irreparable where it cannot be recovered afterwards, and damages are generally unavailable against a regulator whose decision is later set aside. Requiring bankruptcy, or measuring harm by its size or by hardship to third parties, misstates the inquiry.
Case CHB51

Extract from the (fictional) Housing Support Regulation, s 4: 'A Regional Housing Corporation may, by resolution, adopt eligibility rules for rent-geared-to-income units. The General Manager may remove a tenant from a unit if the tenant provides false information.' In March, the Corporation's board adopted a rule capping household assets at $25,000 for all applicants and tenants. In April, the General Manager removed tenant Ms. Cormier from her unit, finding she had given false asset information, without first telling her of the allegation.

  1. The correct answer is A (It settles only that some duty exists; the Baker factors then fix how much procedure): Meeting the Knight threshold establishes only that a duty is owed. How much procedure the duty demands then depends on context, assessed through the Baker factors such as the nature of the decision, the statutory scheme, the importance of the interest and the person's expectations. A full oral hearing is therefore not automatic.
  2. The correct answer is D (The general cap attracts little duty, while the individual removal attracts a real one): The board's asset cap applies to all applicants and tenants and is general in character, so it attracts little duty. The Manager's removal of Ms. Cormier rests on a finding about one person's conduct and is individual in nature. Labelling both as policy overlooks that distinction.
  3. The correct answer is C (Losing her subsidized home seriously affects her interests, so a duty arises despite its discretionary nature): Knight extends the duty to privileges and interests, not only legal rights, so the discretionary nature of a subsidy does not exclude it. Losing a home is a serious effect on a person's interests. The statute's condition on removal is relevant to content but is not the only reason a duty arises, and being able to reapply later does not remove the harm.
  4. The correct answer is A (A high level: disclosure of the case against her, a chance to respond, and written reasons): Where life, liberty or security may be at stake, as in Singh and Suresh, the content of fairness is high: the individual must know the case, answer it and receive reasons. That does not necessarily mean a full oral trial. Permanent residents in Canada are protected, so a lower level cannot be justified on status.
  5. The correct answer is B (The Divisional Court of Ontario, applying the province's Judicial Review Procedure Act to a provincial tribunal): The Board is a provincial decision-maker, so the Federal Court has no role. In Ontario, judicial review of provincial tribunals is brought in the Divisional Court under the provincial Judicial Review Procedure Act. The Federal Court option fails because the Board exercises no power conferred by an Act of Parliament.
  6. The correct answer is D (Broad discretion must serve the statute's purposes, and punishing political criticism is not one): Broad statutory wording does not free a decision-maker from the statute's purposes: discretion is always bounded by the Act, as Roncarelli v Duplessis held. Cancelling a licence to punish political criticism serves no purpose of a public-interest licensing power. The tempting view that ministerial accountability to the legislature replaces judicial supervision fails, because courts police the legal limits of delegated power.
  7. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because the scope of parliamentary privilege is a general legal question of systemic importance): Vavilov names the scope of parliamentary privilege as an example of a general question of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole. Such questions need a single authoritative answer, so the usual presumption gives way. The tribunal's expertise in discrimination law and the lack of an appeal do not change that.
  8. The correct answer is A (The member acted by reason of fraud or perjured evidence placed before the Board): Section 18.1(4) lists acting by reason of fraud or perjured evidence as its own ground, and it matches a decision resting on an invented account. The error-of-law ground needs a misstated legal test, which the facts do not show. No jurisdiction problem arises because the Board was entitled to hear evidence from informants.
  9. The correct answer is D (Describing her as a witness misled her about her status, so using her statements is unfair): Investigators must deal honestly about the person's position when the statements will be used against her, and the misdescription deprived her of an informed choice about how to answer. No police-style caution is required of a regulator. A later hearing cannot cure this when the panel has no power to exclude the statements, and statutory admissibility does not oust fairness.
  10. The correct answer is B (The legislature chose the arbitrator, and respect for that choice grounds the presumption): Vavilov roots the presumption in legislative choice to confer decision-making authority on an administrative body, not in expertise or a privative clause. Expertise remains relevant inside reasonableness review, but it no longer sets the standard. A privative clause is not needed for the presumption to apply.
  11. The correct answer is B (Yes, evidence of procedural defects is received because the record would not show them): Courts admit evidence that goes to a breach of natural justice or fairness, since the record usually cannot reveal that kind of defect. The affidavit is not merits evidence. Consent of the decision maker plays no role in admissibility.
  12. The correct answer is D (Reasonable only if the society proportionately balanced his expression interest against its statutory objectives): Under Doré and Loyola, a tribunal that limits Charter protection must proportionately balance the protection against its statutory mandate, and the court reviews that balance for reasonableness. Treating the Charter as irrelevant fails that duty. A formal section 1 analysis is for legislation, not discretionary decisions.
  13. The correct answer is D (It applies reasonableness and does not reweigh the evidence or substitute its own factual conclusions): On judicial review the standard is reasonableness, and Vavilov says reviewing courts do not reweigh or reassess evidence absent exceptional circumstances. Palpable and overriding error is the appellate standard for facts and applies where a statute gives an appeal. Inferences from evidence are factual in nature, so correctness is the wrong standard.
  14. The correct answer is C (No, because a clerk's email contradicting the directive is no clear representation by the panel): The doctrine needs a clear, unambiguous representation from the decision-maker, and a clerk's message in conflict with the Board's own published directive is neither. Reliance cannot supply what the representation lacks. The directive is a practice document, not law, so the claim that the directive binds like law reaches the right result by a wrong rule.
  15. The correct answer is D (The decision is tainted and set aside, with no inquiry into whether the outcome would differ): Bias taints the whole collective decision when a member with a reasonable apprehension of bias took part in the deliberations, because the influence on the others cannot be measured. The court therefore does not ask whether the result would have been the same. That is why the argument that the other two would have agreed fails, and the matter goes back to a properly constituted panel.
  16. The correct answer is B (Mandamus, if she satisfies the conditions for compelling performance of a public legal duty): Mandamus compels a decision-maker to perform a public legal duty, and applicants must show a clear right, a demand and refusal or unreasonable delay, and no better remedy. Certiorari quashes an existing decision and there is none here. Prohibition restrains excess of power, and quo warranto challenges the right to hold office, neither of which is in issue.
  17. The correct answer is C (They are non-exhaustive considerations weighed together to fix the content of the duty, not separate conditions): The Baker factors help determine how much procedure is owed once a duty of fairness exists, and the list is open. They are weighed together rather than ticked off one by one. Treating them as thresholds or as an exhaustive list confuses whether a duty arises with what it requires.
Case CHB52

Elena Petrova, a Ukrainian citizen living abroad, applied for a work permit to take a two-year contract with a Halifax software firm. An immigration officer refused the permit by letter, stating only that the officer was not satisfied she would leave Canada at the end of her authorized stay. The department's electronic case notes record concerns about her travel history and savings but never mention the signed employment contract she filed. Elena has applied to the Federal Court for judicial review and wants the permit granted.

  1. The correct answer is D (The letter together with the officer's case notes, which a court treats as the reasons): In routine immigration decisions, courts treat the officer's contemporaneous notes in the case-management system as the reasons, following Baker. The letter and notes are read together to see the real basis for the refusal. A later affidavit cannot supply reasons, and the absence of formal reasons does not mean none are needed.
  2. The correct answer is A (The reasons fail to grapple with central evidence, the signed contract, bearing on her likely departure): Under Vavilov, a decision is unreasonable if its reasoning does not meaningfully account for the central evidence and arguments before the decision-maker. The notes never mention the contract that bears directly on the conclusion about her intention to leave. Brevity alone is not a defect, and the court does not apply correctness or substitute its own assessment of her intentions.
  3. The correct answer is B (Set the decision aside and return the application to a different officer for redetermination): Under Vavilov, the usual remedy for an unreasonable decision is to remit the matter to the decision-maker, because the legislature entrusted the decision to that body. A court directs a particular result only where remitting would serve no purpose because the outcome is inevitable. The remaining doubts about her savings and travel history show the outcome is not inevitable either way, so neither granting nor dismissing is right.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness review is constrained by the enabling words, and banning ownership exceeds regulating hours of use): A by-law is reviewed for reasonableness, and the enabling provision sets the outer boundary of what council may do. Regulating hours of operation does not extend to prohibiting possession of the equipment. Legislative acts like by-laws do not carry a fairness duty of hearing or reasons.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (No general duty of fairness applies to legislative acts of broad application, absent a statutory requirement): Fairness duties attach to administrative decisions affecting individuals, not to legislative decisions of general application such as making or repealing a regulation. A statute may add consultation requirements, but this Act is silent. The effect on trappers' livelihoods does not turn a general rule into an individual decision.
  2. The correct answer is D (The revocation is quashed, and the board reconsiders the matter using a fair process): Certiorari removes the legal effect of a decision made in breach of procedural fairness, so the revocation falls and the board must decide again properly. The court does not ordinarily substitute its own certification result, because the statute gives that task to the board. A declaration that the statute is invalid is not needed, since the flaw lay in the process, not the law.
  3. The correct answer is C (The provincial superior court, because council's power comes from provincial legislation, not federal law): The Federal Court's review jurisdiction depends on the source of the decision-maker's power, namely an Act of Parliament or a prerogative order, and not on the subject matter of the dispute. Council exercises a power conferred by provincial legislation, so the Court of King's Bench of Saskatchewan hears the application. Interprovincial grain movement does not convert a municipal body into a federal board.
  4. The correct answer is B (The Federal Court of Appeal, which has exclusive first instance jurisdiction over the listed bodies): Section 28 of the Federal Courts Act lists federal bodies whose decisions are reviewed first by the Federal Court of Appeal, and for those bodies the Federal Court has no jurisdiction. The applicant therefore cannot choose its forum. Provincial superior courts do not review the federal tribunal's work, and the Federal Court's general power under section 18 covers bodies not listed in section 28.
  5. The correct answer is D (Inspection needs no advance notice, but the repair order requires notice and a chance to respond): Routine inspection is an investigative step, and advance warning could defeat its purpose. The order, however, affects her property and carries a deadline with no appeal, so she is owed notice and a chance to respond before it is made. Labelling both steps as enforcement ignores that the order determines her obligations.
  6. The correct answer is C (No, because the reading ignores the definition's wording and purpose and rests only on location): A reasonable interpretation must be justified by the text, context and purpose of the provision. The definition asks what the worker is primarily employed to do, and the director's reasons never engage that language or the purpose of the exemption. The result is unreasonable on that basis, not because the court reviews the definition for correctness.
  7. The correct answer is B (It is invalid, since a regulation cannot exceed the express limit set by its parent Act): A regulation must stay within the limits its enabling statute sets, and an express cap leaves no room for a larger fee. Deference to cabinet's policy judgment operates inside the statutory boundary, not beyond it. Whether a fee is a tax goes to constitutional authority to levy, which is not the issue here.
  8. The correct answer is B (It generally fails, since an unfair process invalidates the decision even if the result was likely): A denial of procedural fairness ordinarily invalidates the decision, because the right to be heard has its own value and courts do not speculate about what a fair hearing would have produced. The same-result argument touches only the court's narrow remedial discretion, which is not a mandatory bar. Reasonableness of the outcome is a separate inquiry that does not repair an unfair process.
  9. The correct answer is A (Admit them, since the record cannot show a procedural defect and the court must decide fairness): An exception to the record rule allows affidavit evidence to establish a procedural defect that the record itself does not disclose. Because the court decides fairness itself, it needs evidence of what occurred. The observers speak to facts, not opinion, and the tribunal has no veto over what the court receives.
  10. The correct answer is A (It is transparent, intelligible and justified in relation to the relevant facts and law): Vavilov defines a reasonable decision by its hallmarks of justification, transparency and intelligibility, assessed against the facts and law that constrain the decision maker. The patent unreasonableness test and the outcome-focused range of acceptable results come from the pre-Vavilov framework. Asking what the court would have decided itself describes correctness, which is a different standard.
  11. The correct answer is C (The by-law is general and attracts little duty; the audit reduction is individual and attracts more): A by-law changing a formula for all employees is general in character and attracts little duty, while an audit-based reduction of one person's pension is an individual decision with a direct effect on her interests. Counting how many people are affected does not reverse that logic. Contractual origin of pension terms does not by itself exclude the public-law duty.
  12. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness for the interpretation, while the court itself decides whether the procedure was fair): One decision can raise issues governed by different frameworks. The officer's reading of the Regulations is reviewed on the presumptive reasonableness standard, whereas the missing interview and notice are fairness complaints that the court assesses directly. Expertise is not what decides the standard, so a front-line officer does not attract correctness.
Case CHB53

Hamid Raza applied to the Registrar of Mines under a provincial Mining Claims Act for a placer claim licence. Section 12 of the extract reads: 'On receipt of a complete application and the prescribed fee, the Registrar shall issue a licence within 30 days unless the applicant is disqualified under section 14.' Mr. Raza filed a complete application and paid the fee. Six months later the Registrar has neither issued nor refused the licence and has given no explanation. No disqualifying fact exists on the record.

  1. The correct answer is B (A clear public legal duty to act, owed to him, whose conditions are satisfied): Section 12 uses mandatory language and its condition is met, so it creates a clear public legal duty to act that is owed to the applicant. The other items are separate requirements of the Apotex test, each proved by different facts such as delay, the lack of another remedy or the balance of convenience, not by the wording of the section.
  2. The correct answer is D (The application can serve as the demand, and six months of silence implies a refusal): A prior demand and refusal are needed, but they may be inferred. A complete application can itself be the demand, and prolonged unexplained silence can amount to an implied refusal. Insisting on a formal letter or an express written refusal would let an official defeat mandamus simply by not answering.
  3. The correct answer is A (An order that the licence issue, because no lawful outcome other than issuing it remains): Where the statute leaves only one lawful outcome, mandamus may direct that result, because no discretion remains to be respected. An order merely to decide would let the Registrar delay again and would treat a closed question as open. Certiorari and a bare declaration do not compel the licence the statute requires.
  4. The correct answer is C (The officer relied on an irrelevant consideration outside the scope of the statutory purpose): Under Vavilov a discretionary decision must be justified in light of the legal constraints that bear on it, including the purposes of the statute. Resting a refusal mainly on a factor the scheme does not contemplate makes the decision unreasonable. Guidelines inform an officer's discretion but are not law, and nothing here suggests an oral hearing was owed.
  5. The correct answer is A (It cannot lower the constitutional standard, so review must be prompt and regular): Clear statutory language can displace the common law duty of fairness, but it cannot override the Charter. Where detention engages section 7, fundamental justice requires reviews that are prompt and regular, so a discretionary schedule cannot stand on its own. The detainee need not first have the Act declared invalid for the Charter standard to inform what the officer must do.
  6. The correct answer is C (In the Federal Court of Appeal, which has exclusive jurisdiction over bodies listed in section 28): Section 18 gives the Federal Court exclusive original jurisdiction over federal boards, but section 28 moves review of the listed bodies, including the CRTC, to the Federal Court of Appeal. The two courts do not share jurisdiction over the same decision, and a provincial superior court has no supervisory power over a federal board. An application filed in the wrong court is dismissed for want of jurisdiction.
  7. The correct answer is D (Disclosure of the report's substance and a chance to make submissions before the commission decides): Investigation is fact-gathering and the decision belongs to the commission, so fairness is judged across the whole process and centres on informed submissions before that decision. Cross-examination belongs to the later tribunal stage. Saying no protection applies overlooks that referral exposes the company to proceedings.
  8. The correct answer is D (Evidence is generally limited to the record before the decision-maker, subject to narrow exceptions): Judicial review is a review of the decision actually made, not a rehearing, so the court generally looks only at the material that was before the decision-maker. Narrow exceptions cover background information, procedural defects and absence of evidence for a required finding. The record includes more than the reasons, and the rule binds both sides.
  9. The correct answer is A (Cabinet deliberations are generally protected from compelled disclosure, and fairness alone does not override that protection): Cabinet confidences are protected by statute and by the constitutional need for candid deliberation, so an applicant cannot compel production just by invoking fairness. Her interest in her licence may call for notice of the case against her, but not for access to Cabinet deliberations. The fact that an individual is affected does not itself defeat the protection.
  10. The correct answer is A (The Tribunal, which adjudicates the merits of referred complaints after the Commission's screening and investigation): The Commission screens and investigates, while the Tribunal holds hearings and decides the merits of referred complaints, so adjudication is deliberately separated from investigation. Treating the Commission's findings as the decision would blur those roles. The Minister has no adjudicative authority under this scheme.
  11. The correct answer is B (The Federal Court of Appeal hears a judicial review application using the s 18.1 grounds): Section 28 sends the matter directly to the Federal Court of Appeal, which then applies the same grounds and powers found in s 18.1 as a reviewing court. It is a judicial review, not a rehearing with new evidence. There is no first stage in the Federal Court and no certification gate.
  12. The correct answer is C (The reasons fail to grapple with a central argument, so the decision lacks justification): Under Vavilov, reasonableness review asks whether the decision is justified, transparent and intelligible, and reasons must show the decision maker meaningfully accounted for the central issues raised. Silence on the hospitalization undermines justification. Inadequate reasons are assessed inside reasonableness rather than as a separate fairness breach, and the court does not substitute the relief it would have granted.
  13. The correct answer is B (Yes, because its commercial interests are directly and substantially affected and it took part below): A regulatory approval that diverts a third of Frontier's customers affects its commercial interests directly, and participation as an intervener supports its position. Economic harm can ground standing where it flows directly from the decision, so a legal right in the narrow sense is not required. Public interest standing is a different route for those without a personal stake and is not needed.
Case CHB54

Lucas Moreau, a machinist, complained to the provincial Human Rights Tribunal that Harbourview Manufacturing discriminated against him on the basis of disability. The Tribunal's statute (fictional extract) provides: 's 20. The Tribunal may dismiss a complaint at any stage, on written submissions, if it is satisfied there is no reasonable prospect the complaint will succeed.' Both sides filed written submissions. Harbourview then filed a reply attaching a new medical report, which the adjudicator received but did not send to Lucas. The adjudicator dismissed the complaint with two paragraphs of reasons and no oral hearing. Lucas applied for judicial review.

  1. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, the presumptive standard where an expert tribunal applies its own home statute): Under Vavilov, reasonableness is presumed when a court reviews the merits of a tribunal's decision, including its application of a threshold in its own statute. None of the correctness exceptions applies, since the issue is not a general question of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole. Patent unreasonableness no longer exists as a standard.
  2. The correct answer is C (No breach: a statutorily authorized paper process can satisfy fairness on a summary dismissal): The statute expressly allows dismissal on written submissions, and fairness does not demand an oral hearing where a paper process gives the parties a meaningful chance to put their case. The importance of a human rights complaint raises the level of protection but not to a trial-type hearing. A preliminary stage still attracts a duty of fairness, so the problem is elsewhere in the process.
  3. The correct answer is A (Harbourview's new medical report was considered without giving Lucas a chance to respond to it): Fairness requires a party to know and answer the evidence that will be used against them, and the new report went to the heart of whether the complaint had a reasonable prospect. Short reasons can meet the Vavilov standard if they respond to the issues. The lack of an oral hearing is permitted by the statute, and the threshold issue is reviewed for reasonableness, not correctness.
  4. The correct answer is B (Mandamus can require the Minister to consider the request lawfully but cannot dictate the result): Mandamus enforces public legal duties but cannot dictate how a true discretion is exercised. It can require the Minister to consider Ms. Dube's request according to law. Because the statute asks whether the Minister is satisfied that funding is warranted, ordering approval would substitute the court's judgment for the Minister's. Mandamus does lie against a Minister acting in an official capacity.
  5. The correct answer is B (It is late, since thirty days from communication have passed and an extension is needed): Section 18.1(2) of the Federal Courts Act sets thirty days from when the decision was first communicated to the party. Forty seven days had passed, so she needs an extension, which the court has power to grant. The sixty day figure is not the federal period, and time does not run from the date of reasons.
  6. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness, because the tribunal exercised a statutory discretion and no body contests the other's authority): The boundary category is aimed at real disputes where two bodies each claim, or each disclaim, authority over the same matter. Here the tribunal used a statutory discretion to dismiss a complaint, which is reviewed for reasonableness. Calling the situation overlapping mandates does not convert a discretionary decision into a jurisdictional boundary ruling.
  7. The correct answer is C (Notify both parties of the new issue and allow submissions before deciding on that basis): A decision-maker who identifies a new issue that could determine the outcome must tell the parties and let them respond before relying on it. The duty to apply the correct law is real, but it is met through a fair process rather than by deciding without notice. Explaining the point afterwards in the reasons gives the losing party no chance to be heard.
  8. The correct answer is B (Decline to hear it, since a statutory appeal is an adequate alternative remedy here): Courts ordinarily refuse judicial review where an adequate alternative remedy such as a statutory appeal exists, and only exceptional circumstances justify proceeding. Ms Okafor points to none, so the Vavilov framework for choosing a standard is never reached. Treating her application as an appeal would let her bypass the legislature's chosen route and its time limits.
  9. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because the appeal clause directs appellate review of a pure question of interpretation): The ruling is a general holding about the Act's meaning, so it is a pure question of law reviewed for correctness because the legislature chose an appeal. The word jurisdiction in an appeal clause does not revive the abandoned jurisdictional error category. It is the appeal itself, not any label, that supplies correctness here, and expertise cannot override that legislative direction.
  10. The correct answer is C (Reasonable limits on repetitive or irrelevant questioning are valid if the evidence was fairly tested): A tribunal may manage its hearing and curtail repetition or irrelevance, provided the party has had a fair opportunity to test the evidence that matters. The panel's power is not unlimited, which is why the bare claim of control over process is too broad. No consent or written ruling is a precondition to a reasonable limit.
Case CHB55

Anh Tran, trained in dental technology abroad, applies to a provincial College of Dental Technologists for registration. The Act says: 'The Registrar may refuse registration if the applicant's qualifications are not equivalent to the College's standard.' The Registrar's letter says only: 'Your application does not meet requirements.' The file contains a six-page analyst memorandum recommending refusal, which the Registrar signed as approved and enclosed with the letter. Refusal prevents Anh from working in the province.

  1. The correct answer is C (Reasons are owed, since the refusal seriously affects her livelihood and professional future): Where a decision has a significant effect on a person's ability to practise a profession, fairness generally requires reasons even if the statute is silent. The old distinction between judicial and administrative decisions does not decide whether reasons are due. Exhausting appeals is not a condition of receiving reasons at first instance.
  2. The correct answer is D (The adopted memorandum can serve as the reasons, so the duty may be met): In Baker, notes prepared by a subordinate and adopted by the decision-maker were accepted as the reasons. Fairness looks to substance and not to a particular form, so a memorandum the Registrar approved and provided to Anh can discharge the duty. The bare letter on its own states a conclusion, not the reasoning.
  3. The correct answer is A (Review for reasonableness, asking whether the reasoning is transparent, intelligible and justified given the record): Once reasons exist, their adequacy is tested within reasonableness review under Vavilov, which asks whether the decision is justified, transparent and intelligible in light of the record. The court does not substitute its own view of equivalence. Mere existence of reasons does not end the inquiry, and thin reasoning is not a free-standing fairness ground.
  4. The correct answer is D (Legislatures may combine these functions in one agency, subject to a contextual bias analysis): Canadian law lets legislatures assign investigative and adjudicative functions to one body, and an overlap is not by itself a breach. The question remains whether, in context, a reasonable and informed person would apprehend bias. Counsel's rule reflects a stricter English-style separation that Canadian courts have not adopted.
Case CHB56

Dimitri Volkov, a permanent resident, sought ministerial relief from inadmissibility under this fictional extract: 'The Minister may grant relief where satisfied that admission is not contrary to the national interest.' The Minister refused, with brief reasons that quote the test and mention his 2009 conviction, but never address his evidence of rehabilitation. A Federal Court judge reviewed the refusal for reasonableness and upheld it. Volkov now appeals to the Federal Court of Appeal.

  1. The correct answer is A (It reviews the Minister's decision itself for reasonableness, effectively stepping into the judge's shoes): Agraira directs the appellate court to focus on the administrative decision, stepping into the reviewing judge's shoes and asking whether that decision is reasonable. The standard of palpable and overriding error is for facts, not for a judge's reasonableness analysis. Substituting its own view on relief would ignore the deference reasonableness still demands toward the Minister.
  2. The correct answer is D (The refusal is unreasonable because the reasons do not grapple with a central issue raised): Vavilov says reasons need not address every argument or item of evidence, but the decision maker must meaningfully account for the central issues raised. Rehabilitation is the heart of the test here, so silence on it leaves the refusal unjustified. Quoting the statutory test shows awareness of the law, not an intelligible application of it, and the flaw is one of substance rather than procedure.
  3. The correct answer is B (Allow the appeal, quash the refusal and remit to the Minister for redetermination): Vavilov makes remittal the usual remedy, and a court should decide the matter itself only if a single outcome is inevitable. Because several responses remain open, the discretion stays with the Minister. The appeal court examines the administrative decision itself rather than deferring to the first judge, and a new hearing before the Federal Court would repeat a review that is no longer needed.
  4. The correct answer is A (Ask whether the decision and reasons are transparent, intelligible and justified, not what the judge prefers): Reasonableness review under Vavilov is centred on the decision-maker's reasons and asks whether the reasoning is justified, transparent and intelligible. The court does not conduct its own de novo analysis or decide the best reading. A mere disagreement with the board's preferred interpretation is not a ground for quashing.
  5. The correct answer is A (General rules of broad application ordinarily attract no common-law duty of fairness before they are made): Rules of broad application, such as regulations made by cabinet, are legislative in nature and ordinarily attract no common-law duty unless the statute says otherwise. Economic impact on an industry does not convert them into individual decisions. A formal administrative label does not matter, since the breadth of the rule decides.
  6. The correct answer is D (Look to the record and context to decide whether the outcome is reasonable): Where no reasons are required, the court examines the record and the surrounding context to see whether the outcome is reasonable in light of the legal and factual constraints. A missing explanation does not by itself make a decision unreasonable, and the court should not invite new reasons after the fact.
  7. The correct answer is B (Fundamental justice requires a fair opportunity to meet the evidence on which the reduction rests): Section 2(e) promises a fair hearing in accordance with the principles of fundamental justice when rights and obligations are determined, and that includes a fair chance to meet the case relied on. The content is flexible, so it does not import full trial rights or guarantee cross-examination of every witness. It applies to administrative determinations as well as criminal ones, and it is a separate statutory guarantee in addition to common law fairness.
  8. The correct answer is D (The statute governs, since a practice direction cannot override a time limit set by the legislature): A practice direction is subordinate procedural guidance and cannot contradict a time limit fixed by the enabling statute. Specificity helps only when instruments of equal rank conflict, so it does not let a board direction displace the statute. Mr. Almeida filed within the statutory thirty days, so his appeal is in time.
  9. The correct answer is B (They are valid if clearly worded, though read narrowly because they let regulations amend statutes): Clauses that let executive regulations amend a statute are known as Henry VIII clauses. Courts accept them when Parliament uses clear words, but because they cut across legislative supremacy they are read narrowly and are not extended by implication. Confirmation by both Houses is not a general condition of validity.
  10. The correct answer is D (The Crown knows of a potential right and contemplates conduct that may adversely affect it): Under Haida Nation, knowledge of a credible but unproven claim, together with contemplated Crown conduct that may harm it, triggers the duty. The strength of the claim affects how deep consultation must go, not whether the duty arises. The duty flows from the honour of the Crown and does not depend on a statutory notice requirement.
  11. The correct answer is B (Administrative bodies lack constitutional tenure guarantees, so pleasure appointments alone do not defeat independence): Judicial independence guarantees under the Constitution protect courts, not administrative tribunals, whose degree of independence is set by their enabling statute. Appointment at pleasure does not by itself show a lack of independence, though a specific institutional arrangement may still raise a concern. The adjudicative character of the board does not carry court-level tenure guarantees.

Practice Test 13 Answer Key

  1.
