The correct answer is B (Elevated content: an oral hearing, a chance to test the evidence, representation and reasons): Under Baker, a process close to judicial adjudication, the finality of the decision and the loss of a livelihood all push the content of fairness up. Mr. Noori is therefore likely owed an oral hearing, the chance to test the evidence, representation and reasons. The old distinction between privileges and rights no longer limits fairness, and a criminal trial standard overshoots.
  2. The correct answer is A (Privileges and interests suffice, so a licence or benefit can ground a duty without a right): Knight does not confine the duty to legal rights: privileges and interests suffice, and licences and benefits are classic examples. Renewal of a taxi licence therefore falls within the third factor. Treating it as a mere privilege confuses the label with the effect on the person.
Case CHB57

Extract, Safe Housing Act (fictional), s 6: 'The Director may appoint a hearing officer to conduct a hearing. The Director shall decide the matter after considering the hearing officer's report.' Landlord Haldane Properties faces an order to repair forty units. Hearing officer Nasrin Aziz conducts a three-day hearing and files a report. Director Luc Bergeron reads only a one-page summary prepared by a junior analyst and signs an order. Haldane learns this from the Director's reasons.

  1. The correct answer is B (The Director decides, and the hearing officer's report informs that decision without binding it): The extract says the Director shall decide after considering the report, so the decision-making power sits with the Director and the hearing officer is a fact-finder and adviser. The statute does not create joint decision-making or reduce the report to optional advice. Haldane's objection therefore fails on the question of who may decide, though other objections may remain.
  2. The correct answer is C (Staff may assist, but the Director must personally consider the report and evidence before deciding): Institutional decision-making allows officials to rely on staff support, but the statute requires the Director to consider the hearing officer's report, and a one-page digest of a three-day hearing does not show that happened. The Director's lack of direct contact with the witnesses does not remove the obligation to engage with the record. The report is not binding, but it cannot simply be ignored.
  3. The correct answer is D (It is valid if Moreau lawfully holds authority to exercise the Director's powers): A person other than the named decision-maker can act only if the law gives that authority, whether through legislation permitting substitutes or a valid delegation. Careful reading of the record does not create authority that is missing. The statute makes the Director, not the hearing officer, the decision-maker, so fairness does not require the hearing officer to decide.
  4. The correct answer is B (The statement was qualified, so it was not the clear, unambiguous and unqualified representation required): Canadian law requires a representation that is clear, unambiguous and unqualified. Words such as probably and if the documents are in order are qualified on their face. Reliance matters only once such a representation exists, and the medium of the statement is not what defeats the claim.
  5. The correct answer is A (Because rejection ends the objection, Ines was owed a meaningful chance to respond to the concerns): A step that looks preliminary can still be determinative: here a rejection ends her only route to a hearing, so the decision is effectively final for her. That raises the procedure owed, at least to a fair opportunity to know and answer the concerns. The availability of judicial review does not substitute for fairness at the decision itself.
  6. The correct answer is A (The clerk's affidavit may be received, but his own account of the documents is excluded): Evidence showing that nothing supported a finding can be received to demonstrate the gap in the record. Osei's version of his documents is an attempt to relitigate the facts and is excluded. The clerk is a proper source for what was tendered.
  7. The correct answer is D (The court may decline, since the registrar should have had the first chance to address it): Courts have discretion to refuse to consider an issue raised for the first time on judicial review when it could have been put to the decision-maker, since the legislature chose that body to decide in the first instance. The discretion is not a rigid bar, which is why a mandatory refusal is wrong, and a legal label does not compel the court to hear it or change the standard.
  8. The correct answer is A (Hear it: segregation can deprive residual liberty, and an internal grievance cannot order release): Habeas corpus can address segregation because it can amount to a deprivation of residual liberty. A court should not decline just because another process exists unless that process is as broad and advantageous. An internal grievance is slow and cannot order release, so it does not displace habeas. The sentence does not authorize every form of confinement.
  9. The correct answer is D (Her remedy lies in contract and employment law, since the statute gives her no special protection): A public body's employee whose relationship rests on a contract of employment generally has remedies in contract and employment law, as Dunsmuir explained, unless a statute gives special protection to the office. The Libraries Act is silent on her removal, so nothing converts her dismissal into a public law decision. That the employer is public, or that dismissal was for cause, is not enough to trigger fairness duties.
  10. The correct answer is B (She is functus officio and cannot reopen for new evidence absent statutory or contractual authority): Chandler applies functus officio to administrative adjudicators, and an arbitrator who has issued a final award cannot reopen it for new evidence unless the governing statute or agreement permits. Likelihood of a different outcome is the test for admitting fresh evidence on appeal, not for reopening here. The thirty day period belongs to judicial review, not reconsideration.
  11. The correct answer is D (No fairness duty arises, since holding office during pleasure carries no right to be heard): Under Knight, the common law requires procedural fairness for the removal of an office holder only where the office is held for cause or carries security of tenure. An office held during pleasure can be ended without reasons or a hearing, so the order in council needed neither. Her livelihood interest and the Baker factors do not alter that classification, and the stem shows no allegation of misconduct that might change the analysis.
  12. The correct answer is D (The claim is struck, since the dispute arises under the collective agreement and arbitration is exclusive): Where the essential character of a dispute arises from the collective agreement, the labour relations statute gives the arbitrator exclusive jurisdiction. Framing the claim as wrongful suspension or as a tort does not change its essential character. There is no concurrent choice of forum, and a stay pending a board ruling is unnecessary when the answer is plain.
  13. The correct answer is B (Extension refused, because the court also weighs merit and the proposed ground is not arguable): The extension decision is discretionary and considers the whole picture, including whether the application has some merit. Granting time to pursue a ground that cannot succeed serves no purpose, even with a good explanation and short delay. A lawyer's error can be a reasonable explanation, so that is not why the motion fails.
  14. The correct answer is B (Relevant, but courts are cautious because the board has its own means to resolve divergence): Vavilov accepts that internal inconsistency can bear on reasonableness, while recognizing that a body with many members may have its own mechanisms to resolve disagreement. Consolidated-Bathurst-style collective processes illustrate this. Inconsistency does not alter the standard of review or automatically defeat the later decision.
  15. The correct answer is D (The Federal Court, since the officer exercised a federal statutory power as a federal board): A federal officer exercising a statutory power is a federal board under the Federal Courts Act, so judicial review of the discretionary refusal lies in the Federal Court. The Tax Court hears appeals of assessments rather than review of fairness-relief decisions, and the Court of Appeal route applies only to bodies listed in the Act.
  16. The correct answer is B (Only if the question attracts correctness, where the court finds the single right interpretation): Under reasonableness the question is whether the interpretation is justified, not whether it is the best, so a judge's contrary preference does not matter. Only where a correctness category applies must the court identify the one right answer. The absence of a statutory appeal actually reinforces the presumption of reasonableness.
  17. The correct answer is C (Palpable and overriding error governs the factual finding, while correctness governs the questions of law): A statutory appeal brings in appellate standards as described in Housen v Nikolaisen. Legal questions are reviewed for correctness, and factual findings, including inferences from a traffic study, only for palpable and overriding error. The Vavilov presumption of reasonableness does not apply here.
  18. The correct answer is A (A lower level of fairness applies, because rights are determined only later at the hearing): A preliminary investigative step that recommends but does not decide attracts a lower level of fairness, particularly where full protections follow at the hearing. Some fairness may still apply, so the claim that none is owed overshoots. Reputational harm from a report does not raise the content to final-hearing level.
  19. The correct answer is A (Decline to hear the application until the tribunal finishes, absent exceptional circumstances justifying early intervention): Courts generally decline to review interlocutory rulings while the administrative process is ongoing, absent exceptional circumstances. The landlord can raise the hearsay ruling after the tribunal decides, through the appeal right he has. Treating an evidentiary ruling as jurisdictional misreads the doctrine, and no rule gives the tribunal's chair exclusive power to review it.
  20. The correct answer is B (It screens whether the appeal should be heard, with the merits decided later under appellate standards): Leave is a gatekeeping mechanism that decides whether the question merits the appeal court's attention. The correctness of the regulator's interpretation is determined at the hearing of the appeal itself. A leave judge neither applies a deferential standard nor splits issues into jurisdictional and other errors.
  21. The correct answer is B (Thirty days from first communication of the decision, which a judge may extend): Section 18.1(2) sets thirty days from the time the decision was first communicated, and a judge may fix or allow further time, so Tomas must seek an extension for his 45-day filing. The sixty-day, written-reasons and six-month versions misstate the length, the starting point or who can extend.
  22. The correct answer is A (A failure of internal rationality, because the chain of analysis contains an unexplained leap in logic): Vavilov describes two types of fundamental flaw: a lack of internal coherence, including a chain of analysis that is not rational or contains an unexplained leap, and a decision that is untenable in light of the legal and factual constraints. The stem gives only an unexplained jump from lateness to dishonesty, which is a rationality problem. Nothing in it points to a misread statute, an unfair process or an ignored record.
Case CHB58

Extract from an invented Taxi Licensing Bylaw, s 14: The licensing committee may revoke a licence where the holder has repeatedly breached this Bylaw. Gurpreet Dhillon, a driver for twelve years, received notice of a revocation hearing but not the three passenger complaints relied on. After a ten minute hearing the committee revoked his licence and gave two sentences of reasons. On judicial review the court finds that withholding the complaints breached procedural fairness.

  1. The correct answer is B (Quash the revocation and send the matter back for a fresh hearing by the committee): Breach of procedural fairness ordinarily leads the court to quash the decision and return it to the decision-maker for a fresh, fair hearing. Substituting the court's own view or ordering the outcome the applicant wants is exceptional, because the bylaw entrusts the decision to the committee. Upholding the revocation on the merits would ignore that fairness is assessed independently of the outcome.
  2. The correct answer is B (Reject it, because the penalty and the third incident remained open to real contest): The futility exception is narrow: relief is refused only where the result is a foregone conclusion, which is not shown when the sanction and a disputed incident remain open to argument. Admitting two incidents does not waive the right to be heard on the third or on penalty. A prediction that the committee would probably reach the same result is not enough.
  3. The correct answer is A (The bylaw gives the revocation decision to the committee, so courts ordinarily return it): Judicial review remedies are discretionary, and where a statute assigns a decision to a tribunal the usual order quashes the decision and returns it. Courts depart from that only in rare cases, such as where a single lawful outcome is possible. Treating reinstatement as automatic confuses the invalidity of the decision with a power to make the licensing decision itself.
  4. The correct answer is D (Safety justifies withholding details if the summary still conveys the case he must meet): Disclosure duties yield to legitimate concerns such as informant safety, but the person must still be told enough of the case against him to respond, usually by a fair summary. A bare claim of confidentiality is not enough. Informer privilege is not limited to criminal trials, and no general rule requires prior court approval for administrative withholding.
Case CHB59

Northern Reach Communications, a small Yukon internet provider, asked the Canadian Radio-television and Telecommunications Commission (CRTC) to order a larger carrier, Arctic Telecom Ltd., to give it access to its fibre network. After a written hearing the CRTC dismissed the application. Northern Reach says the CRTC ignored its pricing evidence and wants the decision set aside. Its lawyer, Marguerite Tanaka, is unsure which court to approach and what relief to seek.

  1. The correct answer is A (The Federal Court of Appeal, because s 28(1) gives it jurisdiction over the listed bodies): Section 28(1) lists particular federal bodies whose decisions go directly to the Federal Court of Appeal for judicial review. The Federal Court's general jurisdiction under s 18 is the default only for federal boards outside that list. No preliminary screening step exists in the Federal Court.
  2. The correct answer is D (Section 28 removes the Federal Court's jurisdiction and gives it to the Federal Court of Appeal): Where s 28 applies, the Federal Court of Appeal alone has jurisdiction, and the Federal Court has none over the same decision. The section deals with judicial review applications themselves, not only appeals. Framing the relief as a declaration does not avoid it, since the section covers all the s 18 relief.
  3. The correct answer is A (Setting the decision aside and referring the matter back to the CRTC for redetermination): Section 18.1(3) lets the court set aside a decision and refer it back for determination in accordance with its directions. The merits of network access belong to the CRTC, so the court does not usually substitute its own order. Damages are not a judicial review remedy and require a separate action.
Case CHB60

Extract from a fictional Act, s 25: 'The Minister may exempt a foreign national from a requirement of this Act if justified by humanitarian and compassionate considerations, taking into account the best interests of a child directly affected.' Joaquim Pereira, a Portuguese national living in Hamilton for nine years, applied for an exemption. His six-year-old Canadian-born daughter has a chronic medical condition. The officer refused, writing that she 'would adapt' and that Joaquim 'has not shown exceptional hardship.'

  1. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, constrained by the statutory text, which requires attention to the child's best interests): Discretionary decisions are reviewed on reasonableness under Vavilov, and the extract contains no statutory appeal. The review is constrained by the governing scheme, here the express direction to consider the child's best interests, as Baker and Kanthasamy illustrate. Correctness and palpable and overriding error belong to other frameworks, and patent unreasonableness no longer exists as a standard.
  2. The correct answer is D (No, because the text requires holistic weighing of humanitarian considerations, not a threshold of hardship): The extract directs the officer to weigh humanitarian and compassionate considerations as a whole. Adding a stricter hardship threshold narrows that discretion in a way the text does not support, as the Supreme Court recognized in Kanthasamy. The officer's latitude is bounded by the statutory purpose, and brevity of reasons is not itself the defect.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quash the refusal and remit it, since the statute entrusts the discretion to the delegate): Where a discretionary decision is unreasonable, the usual remedy is to set it aside and return the matter to the decision-maker, because the legislature entrusted the decision to that body. Substitution is reserved for rare cases where only one outcome is possible, and an unclear medical record points away from that. Discretionary decisions are reviewed on the merits for reasonableness, not only for fairness.
  4. The correct answer is A (Express statutory authority displaces the structural bias argument, leaving constitutional challenges or proof of actual bias): A legislature can authorize overlapping functions, and clear statutory language ousts a common law bias rule based on structure. Mr. Ng could still raise a constitutional argument or show actual bias in how the Registrar acted. Procedural fairness is not assessed on a reasonableness standard, so the deference option fails as well.
  5. The correct answer is D (Only to the extent the tribunal's enabling Act incorporates its provisions by express reference): The British Columbia statute is a menu of provisions that apply to a particular tribunal only when that tribunal's own enabling Act says so. It is not a code that binds every agency by default, unlike the way many assume the Ontario Act operates. Silence in the enabling Act therefore leaves the common law to govern.
  6. The correct answer is D (The court will likely decline review, because the Minister's merits appeal is an adequate alternative remedy): Courts have a discretion to refuse judicial review where an adequate alternative remedy exists, weighing convenience, expeditiousness, the nature of the error and the burden on the applicant (Harelkin). A speedy merits appeal can cure the alleged misweighing, and the complaint raises no bias or jurisdictional issue that the Minister could not handle. Superior court supervision is not displaced, but its exercise is discretionary and ordinarily takes the form of declining the application rather than staying it.
  7. The correct answer is B (Notice must cover the case to be met, so a new allegation needs fresh notice): The owner prepared for an overcharging complaint and was then judged on a different allegation. Adequate notice means notice of the case to be met, so a new ground calls for fresh notice and time to respond. Bias, reasons and disclosure may be live in other facts, but the defect here is the surprise change of case.
  8. The correct answer is B (The decision is individual and ends her licence, so every factor points toward a strong duty): Each factor points the same way: the decision is individual in character, it is made by a public regulator, and it ends the pharmacist's licence. A decision that looks adjudicative is only one consideration and does not leave the others neutral. The licence loss is not the sole factor, because the nature of the decision and the relationship also count.
  9. The correct answer is D (He should not sit, since an investigator who urged prosecution would appear to have prejudged it): An individual who gathered the evidence and recommended charges has taken a position on the case, and an informed observer would doubt that he could judge it afresh. Where the statute does not clearly authorize the overlap, the common law disqualifies him personally. The report's disclosure is a separate fairness point and does not cure this one.
  10. The correct answer is D (A right to the customary oral hearing, but no entitlement to renewal itself): In Canada the doctrine is procedural: a regular practice can create an expectation that the usual procedure will be followed, and fairness then requires it. It does not guarantee the result, which is the English-style substantive approach Canadian courts have not adopted. Nothing requires the practice to be formalized in a regulation.
  11. The correct answer is C (Correctness for the tariff interpretation and palpable and overriding error for the leak volume finding): A statutory appeal signals that the legislature wants appellate standards, as applied in Housen v Nikolaisen. Questions of law attract correctness, and findings of fact (and mixed questions with no extricable legal error) attract palpable and overriding error. The expertise of the regulator does not turn an appeal into reasonableness review.
  12. The correct answer is C (It signals appellate review, so correctness applies to law and palpable and overriding error to fact): Vavilov treats a statutory appeal to a court as the legislature's direction that appellate standards apply, as set out in Housen. Expertise no longer justifies a separate deferential standard once such an appeal exists. The jurisdictional-error category and the sliding scale are pre-Vavilov ideas that the Court abandoned.
  13. The correct answer is B (Dismiss it as premature, since the legislation expressly requires exhaustion of the appeal first): Where the legislature itself says judicial review may not be brought until the appeal is exhausted, the court gives effect to that direction and dismisses a premature application. The Harelkin factors guide discretion only where the statute is silent. An exhaustion clause is not an ouster of review, so it does not offend the court's supervisory role.
  14. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because it is an extricable error in the legal test, a question of law): Housen holds that an extricable error in the characterization or statement of the legal standard is an error of law and is reviewed for correctness, even within a mixed question. Not every mixed challenge is shielded. Reasonableness does not apply on a court appeal.
  15. The correct answer is C (A flaw must be central to the outcome, and a peripheral slip does not suffice): Vavilov cautions that reasons are read holistically and are not subject to a line-by-line treasure hunt for error. A flaw must be sufficiently central or significant to the decision to make it unreasonable. The reasons still matter, so the slip is not irrelevant, but a peripheral miscalculation that leaves the zoning conclusion untouched does not undermine justification.
  16. The correct answer is D (Security of tenure, protecting members from executive removal and so supporting independent decision-making): Removal only for cause and by a legislative vote is a security of tenure measure. Financial security and administrative control are separate structural elements, and impartiality concerns a member's personal connection to a case, which tenure rules do not address.
  17. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness applies, since the question is important to employers but not to the legal system generally): The exception for general questions of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole is narrow. It covers matters such as the scope of privilege or the limits of state power, not questions of broad economic or sectoral significance that arise under a single regulatory statute. Quasi-constitutional status of a Code does not alter the standard, and Vavilov recognizes no sliding scale of scrutiny.
  18. The correct answer is D (Not fatal, because reasons must meaningfully address the central issues, not every argument made): Vavilov expects reasons to grapple with the key issues raised, but it does not require a decision maker to address every submission or write a judgment-style essay. A board still must consider what the parties argue, so the claim that it owes no duty to consider submissions overstates the rule.
  19. The correct answer is A (Statutory discretion is limited by the rule of law and must serve the statute's purposes): Roncarelli v Duplessis established that wide statutory discretion is still bounded by the purposes of the enabling law, and punishing a person for an unrelated act is an improper purpose. A missing hearing might be a further complaint, but the core defect here is the purpose behind the decision. The idea that a permit is a mere privilege was the very argument rejected in that case.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (A step that only investigates or recommends attracts a limited duty, assessed with the final decision): A purely investigative or recommendatory step that decides no rights attracts at most a limited duty, because fairness is assessed across the whole process and the person can respond before the final decision is made. The duty centres on the final decision, not only a formal hearing. Requiring a full hearing at each stage would collapse preliminary and final steps into one.
  2. The correct answer is B (A court may still review whether the Board acted within its legal authority, despite the clause): The rule of law requires that a superior court be able to review whether a statutory decision-maker stayed within its legal authority. A privative clause may signal that the legislature wants deference, but it cannot remove that supervisory role. Confining review to the Charter or to factual errors has no support in Canadian authority.
Case CHB61

Priya Raman, a real estate agent, had her licence revoked by the Provincial Real Estate Council. Section 12(1) of the invented Real Estate Licensing Act reads, in this extract: 'The Council may revoke a licence where the licensee has engaged in conduct unbecoming a registrant.' Raman argued that 'conduct unbecoming' requires proof of dishonest intent. The Council's reasons find her listing statements inaccurate but do not address her intent argument. The Act provides no appeal.

  1. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, the presumptive standard, since no legislative direction or category rebuts it): The Council interprets its home statute, so reasonableness is presumed. The lack of an appeal points toward judicial review on the presumptive standard, not toward correctness. Vavilov also removed any intermediate standard based on partial expertise.
  2. The correct answer is A (It undermines the decision, because reasons must meaningfully grapple with the central issues raised): A decision-maker's failure to address a central argument about the meaning of its governing provision suggests it did not actually consider the question. The reasons then fail to show justification, transparency and intelligibility. The court does not step in and answer the interpretive question itself.
  3. The correct answer is C (Set aside the decision and remit it, since the Council holds the merits mandate): The usual remedy for an unreasonable decision is to remit the matter so the decision-maker can reconsider with correct guidance. A court decides the merits only where remittal would serve no useful purpose because only one outcome is possible. Here the intent question is open, so remittal is proper.
  4. The correct answer is D (Dalia is owed more procedure, because greater impact on a life calls for stronger protections): Baker says the more important the decision is to the lives of those affected and the greater its impact, the more stringent the procedural protections. Loss of a professional livelihood weighs far more than losing a parking permit. The factor is not confined to liberty and security interests, and it belongs to procedural fairness rather than substantive review.
  5. The correct answer is B (The committee fettered its discretion by applying the policy rigidly without weighing individual circumstances): A decision-maker may adopt policies to guide discretion, but must remain open to the particular case and cannot treat the policy as binding. Refusing to hear Mr. Leclerc's explanation is the clearest sign of fettering. Policies need not be regulations, and publication matters to fairness without being the main defect.
  6. The correct answer is D (A panel that departs from consistent earlier decisions must explain why, and this one did not): Vavilov treats consistent internal decisions as part of the context that constrains a decision maker. Panels are not strictly bound, but a marked departure from earlier practice must be justified in the reasons. Treating earlier decisions as binding precedent overstates the rule, and the burden of explanation does not depend on the applicant raising them.
  7. The correct answer is C (Liberty is engaged, but fundamental justice is contextual, so a prompt review after suspension may suffice): Returning a releasee to custody takes away liberty, so section 7 is engaged. What fundamental justice demands depends on context, and where the officer must be able to act quickly on public risk, a prompt independent review can be enough. A rule requiring a full prior hearing ignores that context, and a privilege label does not defeat the liberty interest.
  8. The correct answer is C (Urgency justified acting without notice but has faded, so fairness now requires a chance to respond): Urgency can justify an order made without notice, but the justification lasts only as long as the emergency does. After three months the regulator can no longer rely on the rupture to avoid hearing the company, so the ordinary duty of fairness revives. The suggestion that the order was invalid from the start fails because the statute expressly permits emergency action.
  9. The correct answer is D (It is premature, because the tribunal can rule on its jurisdiction first and review remains available): A jurisdictional challenge does not in itself justify early review under C.B. Powell, because tribunals can decide their own jurisdiction in the first instance and the court can review that ruling with the final decision. The short remaining hearing and the availability of later review confirm that the application is premature. Tribunals are not barred from deciding such questions.
  10. The correct answer is A (Where the statute is silent, common law applies flexibly, and a reasonable apprehension of interference arises): Ocean Port leaves independence mainly to the enabling statute, but when the statute is silent the common law applies, adjusted to the function of the tribunal. A direct signal that reappointment depends on outcomes would lead an informed person to doubt the board's independence. Requiring proof of actual bias confuses independence with individual bias.
  11. The correct answer is B (Where the damage was the inevitable result of exercising the authority, not avoidable by reasonable care): The defence of statutory authority protects only damage that is the inevitable consequence of exercising the statutory power, which the defendant must prove could not be avoided by reasonable care. Mere authorization to operate does not immunize careless operation. Good faith and lack of intent are irrelevant to negligence liability.
  12. The correct answer is C (A routine screening that decides no rights does not attract a right to counsel): Procedural fairness is calibrated to what is being decided, and a preliminary or screening stage that determines no rights calls for minimal process. The possibility of later consequences does not convert routine questioning into an adjudication. A right to counsel is more likely at later, determinative stages.
  13. The correct answer is C (It renders the decision unreasonable only if the flaw is sufficiently central to the outcome): Under Vavilov a reviewing court looks for a fundamental gap or an unsound line of analysis, and a flaw must be sufficiently central or significant to the decision to undermine it. A mistake about one study that the findings do not depend on is peripheral. The court may not reweigh the evidence, so reassessing the studies for itself is the wrong response.
  14. The correct answer is B (The format prevented effective cross-examination on the central issue, so refusing to adapt was unfair): Online hearings are acceptable, but here the technical failures impaired his ability to test the only evidence on the key issue, and the panel refused any adjustment. No rule requires in-person hearings whenever credibility is in issue. Authority to hold virtual hearings does not answer whether this hearing was fair, and the fairness question is not decided by reasonableness review.
  15. The correct answer is C (The practice cannot override the statutory exclusion of notice and hearing, so the claim fails): Legitimate expectations supplement the common law of fairness; they do not displace clear statutory language. Because the legislature expressly excluded notice and hearing, neither the practice nor the importance of the grant restores them. The compromise option invents a middle path that the text of the Act rules out.
  16. The correct answer is B (A duty of fairness attaches to administrative decisions that affect individual interests, including this one): Nicholson held that a general duty of fairness applies to administrative decisions affecting a person's rights, privileges or interests, and that probationary status does not remove it, although it lowers the content. The earlier approach tying natural justice to a judicial or quasi-judicial label was set aside. Classification is not a precondition to fairness.
  17. The correct answer is B (Misfeasance in public office: deliberate unlawful conduct, knowing it was unlawful and likely to harm her): Odhavji Estate v Woodhouse describes misfeasance in public office as deliberate unlawful conduct by a public officer who knows the conduct is unlawful and likely to injure the plaintiff. The chief's private admission supplies both elements. Negligence does not fit deliberate conduct, no proceedings were commenced, and breach of a statute is not itself a nominate tort.
Case CHB62

Extract from an invented Housing Authority Act, s 9: The Registrar holds office for a term of five years and may be removed only for cause. Sandra Okafor was appointed Registrar. After eighteen months the Minister dismissed her by letter, alleging poor judgment in handling a tenant file. She received no notice of the allegation and no chance to respond. The Minister says the office is political and no hearing is owed.

  1. The correct answer is D (Removal only for cause, which means she must know the grounds and respond): Knight distinguishes offices held at pleasure, which attract no duty on removal, from offices held for cause, which do. The statute here allows removal only for cause, so she must be told the grounds and given a chance to respond. A ministerial appointment, a fixed term or a public duty, taken alone, does not create that right.
  2. The correct answer is A (Notice of the grounds for removal and a fair chance to answer them, perhaps in writing): Under the Baker factors, removal for cause calls for notice of the grounds and a fair chance to answer them, which can be given in writing. A full adversarial hearing with counsel is more than Knight required for an office of this kind. Reasons given only after dismissal would not let her respond before the decision was made.
  3. The correct answer is A (Her tenure is statutory and protected, so public law fairness applies despite any employment label): A person who holds a statutory office with protected tenure is not simply an employee under a contract, so public law fairness applies, as Knight and Dunsmuir explain. Here the statute itself limits removal to cause, which brings the public law duty. The employee label and the availability of a civil claim do not remove it, and fairness is not limited to judicial offices or elected officials.
  4. The correct answer is D (The panel fettered its discretion and failed to consider the best-interests criterion the statute requires): A decision-maker may be guided by policy but cannot let it replace the statutory test, and reasons that ignore the central issue raised show the criterion was never weighed. Vavilov asks whether the reasons meaningfully grapple with the key submissions, which these do not. The court does not decide for itself that the exemption must be granted, and policy reliance does not turn the review into correctness.
  5. The correct answer is B (The court should likely decline to hear it, because he let an adequate statutory appeal lapse): Judicial review is discretionary, and courts ordinarily refuse it where an adequate statutory appeal was available and not pursued. Vavilov addresses the standard of review, not whether review is available. The supervisory jurisdiction is not a way around the legislature's chosen route.
  6. The correct answer is B (No, prosecuting and then privately advising the panel on one file raises a reasonable apprehension): A lawyer who argued for one side and then sits with the decision-makers in private gives the appearance of a second voice for the prosecution. Separate counsel should present the case and advise the panel. The panel's formal power to decide does not remove the appearance created by that private access.
  7. The correct answer is D (Uphold, because text, context and purpose reasonably support the board's reading despite the judge's view): Where more than one interpretation is open and the decision maker has justified its choice by reference to text, context and purpose, reasonableness review does not let the court substitute its preferred reading. Deference is not unlimited, which is why the reading must still be supported by the statute rather than by expertise alone. Here the board did that work and the words allow either meaning.
  8. The correct answer is A (Unreasonable, because departing from settled internal decisions carries a burden to explain the change): Vavilov accepts that tribunals are not strictly bound by precedent, but a decision-maker that departs from longstanding internal authority bears the justificatory burden of explaining why. Silence about a fifteen-year line of decisions leaves the departure unexplained. The panel's freedom to depart is therefore real but conditional, and the problem is the missing explanation, not a rule of binding precedent.
  9. The correct answer is D (It is unfair: Lakeview had no notice of a new ground and no chance to answer): A party is entitled to notice of the case to be met, including any ground on which the tribunal may decide against it. Deciding on an unraised ground deprives the party of its chance to lead evidence and argue. That the ground drew on the same documents does not show Lakeview knew it was in issue, and prejudice in the sense of a probable win need not be proved.
  10. The correct answer is A (Refuse relief in discretion, since delay and third party reliance outweigh the strong claim): Judicial review remedies are discretionary, and unreasonable delay combined with prejudice to third parties who relied on the decision can justify refusing relief even where the claim is strong. The absence of a statutory limit does not remove this discretion. The federal thirty day period does not govern a municipal decision.
  11. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because leave simply opens a statutory appeal and appellate standards then govern questions of law): A leave requirement is a gatekeeping step in a statutory appeal, not a signal that review is deferential. Once leave is granted, Vavilov and Housen apply appellate standards, so legal questions are reviewed for correctness. The importance of the question may matter to leave but does not alter the standard.
Case CHB63

Extract from the fictional Mine Safety Act. Section 12(1): The Director may suspend a mine operator's permit if satisfied, on reasonable grounds, that an unsafe condition exists. (2) Before suspending, the Director shall give the operator written notice of the grounds. (3) An operator may appeal a suspension to the Mining Appeals Tribunal within 20 days. (4) A decision of the Tribunal is final. Facts: Director Alma Fortin suspended Kestrel Ridge Mining's permit after an inspector reported cracked support beams. Kestrel Ridge says it received no written grounds beforehand, learned of the suspension within days, and did not appeal.

  1. The correct answer is A (That the Director be satisfied, on reasonable grounds, that an unsafe condition exists): The power only arises if the Director is satisfied on reasonable grounds that an unsafe condition exists; that is the limit on when the power may be used. The notice requirement is a procedural step, the appeal period is a remedy provision, and the finality language concerns the Tribunal's decisions.
  2. The correct answer is C (Breach of the written-notice step in section 12(2), which the word shall made mandatory): Section 12(2) imposes an express precondition with the word "shall", and the facts say no written grounds were given beforehand. An inspector's report can ground a reasonable belief, the finality language applies to the Tribunal's own decisions, and nothing in the extract requires the Director to explain the appeal period as a condition of validity.
  3. The correct answer is D (Decline to hear it in its discretion, since an adequate appeal went unused): Courts ordinarily refuse judicial review where an adequate alternative remedy, here the Tribunal appeal, was available and not used, though the discretion is not absolute. Section 12(4) protects only Tribunal decisions and could not oust review in any event, and a party cannot create the absence of a remedy by missing the deadline itself.
  4. The correct answer is B (Grant an extension, because intention, explanation, merit and lack of prejudice all favour her): Section 18.1(2) lets a judge allow further time, and courts weigh a continuing intention to proceed, a reasonable explanation for the delay, an arguable case and prejudice to the other side. All four favour Samantha. The extension is not automatic, since the court must still be satisfied on those factors, and no internal reconsideration is a precondition.
  5. The correct answer is B (The category was abandoned, so a question about a tribunal's powers is presumptively reviewed for reasonableness): Vavilov concluded that true questions of jurisdiction could not be reliably distinguished from other questions and eliminated the category. A tribunal's decision about the limits of its authority is therefore reviewed for reasonableness unless a different exception applies. The English habit of treating jurisdictional error as a special ground does not carry over.
  6. The correct answer is C (It fails, because the duty binds the Crown alone, and the Crown keeps ultimate responsibility): The duty to consult flows from the honour of the Crown and binds the Crown, not private proponents. The Crown may delegate procedural aspects of consultation to a proponent, but the duty and legal responsibility stay with the Crown, so an action against the company alone fails. The Nation need not prove its claim first, which is why the proof-in-court argument is also wrong.
  7. The correct answer is B (The Bill of Rights binds only federal law and bodies, so it does not apply here): The Canadian Bill of Rights is a federal statute that governs federal legislation and federal decision-makers only. A provincial tribunal exercising provincial powers is outside it, though the Charter and common law fairness still apply. Its quasi-constitutional status gives it weight in interpretation at the federal level but does not extend its reach to the provinces.
  8. The correct answer is A (It does not reweigh the evidence, and intervenes on factual findings only in exceptional circumstances): Vavilov says reviewing courts must refrain from reweighing and reassessing evidence absent exceptional circumstances. The palpable and overriding error test belongs to appeals under Housen, not judicial review. Uncontradicted expert evidence still has to be weighed by the decision maker, not the court.
  9. The correct answer is A (The importance of the decision to the individual, whose long-standing employment is cut off): Baker treats the importance of the decision to the person affected as a factor that raises the level of fairness owed, and a refusal that ends her ability to work is a severe consequence. The nature of the decision and the officer's own procedural views add little or point the other way. A legitimate expectation needs a promise or established practice, which the facts do not show.
  10. The correct answer is B (Correctness, as a general question of central importance to the legal system as a whole): Vavilov identifies general questions of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole as a category requiring correctness, and the scope of solicitor-client privilege is a standard example. Expertise in privacy law does not displace that need for a uniform answer. Adjusting the margin of deference is not how Vavilov handles the stakes.
Case CHB64

College of Dental Hygienists Act (fictional extract), s 40: A member may appeal a final order of the Discipline Committee to the Divisional Court on a question of law. Rina Kapoor, a hygienist, faces a discipline hearing. The Committee chair had publicly described Rina's alleged conduct as 'unacceptable'. Before the hearing began, the Committee refused her request to remove the chair, and Rina applied for judicial review of that refusal.

  1. The correct answer is B (Courts generally decline to review interim rulings until the process ends, absent exceptional circumstances): The general rule, described in C.B. Powell, is that courts do not review an administrative process piecemeal and wait until it concludes, leaving exceptional circumstances for the rare case. This avoids delay, cost and fragmentation, and the applicant can still raise the bias point on the later appeal. The mere allegation of bias does not by itself amount to exceptional circumstances.
  2. The correct answer is D (The court may refuse review in its discretion, since the appeal can adequately address bias): A court keeps its discretion to refuse judicial review where an adequate alternative remedy exists, and a statutory appeal that lets the Divisional Court consider bias as a question of law will usually qualify. The discretion does not run mechanically in either direction: an appeal does not oust judicial review, and a privative clause is not a prerequisite. The court weighs factors such as convenience, expense and the nature of the error.
  3. The correct answer is C (The decision is invalid and should be quashed, whatever the likely outcome of a fair hearing): A breach of the right to an impartial decision maker invalidates the decision, because, as Cardinal explains, a denial of a fair hearing renders a decision invalid whether or not a fair process would have produced the same result. Courts keep a narrow discretion to refuse relief, but predicting the outcome does not cure a defect of this kind. Remittal to a differently constituted panel, not substitution, is the usual response.
  4. The correct answer is A (Costs are discretionary but usually follow the event, so the successful applicant ordinarily recovers costs): Costs are in the court's discretion, and outside special regimes the starting point is that they follow the event. The good faith of the decision-maker does not displace that starting point. The idea that each side bears its own costs is the exception for particular situations, not a default rule for honest public bodies.
  5. The correct answer is D (Intervene, because a finding with no evidentiary foundation fails the factual constraints on the decision): Declining to reweigh evidence does not protect a finding with no evidence behind it. The factual record is one of the constraints that bear on a decision, so a conclusion unsupported by any evidence is unreasonable. This is different from disagreeing about weight. Bad faith is not required, and patent unreasonableness is the superseded standard.
  6. The correct answer is B (Section 7 is not engaged, because loss of income or a licence is an economic interest): Section 7 protects life, liberty and security of the person, and purely economic interests such as income or the right to practise a trade fall outside it. The Charter does bind provincial regulators, so the view that it reaches only federal bodies fails. The college still owed common law fairness, but that is a separate source of procedural rights.
  7. The correct answer is B (The Act governs, because it prevails over inconsistent enabling provisions unless those provisions expressly declare otherwise): The Ontario Act contains a paramountcy rule: its provisions prevail over conflicting enabling-statute provisions unless the other statute expressly says it prevails. That rule displaces the usual specific-over-general and later-in-time presumptions. The common law is not needed because a statutory standard of notice exists.
  8. The correct answer is D (The award is vulnerable as unreasonable, because it departs from comparable awards without explanation): Earlier decisions do not bind a tribunal, but they form part of the legal and factual context, and a marked departure calls for justification. Without any explanation, the outcome is not shown to be justified. The proper response is usually to set the award aside and remit it, not to cap it.
  9. The correct answer is B (Overbroad, since the tribunal may refuse to apply the regulation here but cannot invalidate it generally): A tribunal with power over questions of law can decline to apply a provision that it finds unconstitutional in the case before it. A general declaration of invalidity is a remedy reserved to the courts. The tribunal's ruling is not binding precedent for other decision-makers, and it was not required to refer the Charter question elsewhere.
  10. The correct answer is B (Yes, because a denunciatory fine enforced by possible imprisonment is a true penal consequence): Section 11 turns on the nature of the proceeding and its consequences, not on the label of the forum. A fine imposed to punish and denounce, backed by imprisonment on default, is a true penal consequence, so the full range of section 11 rights is available. The regulatory character of the scheme is not decisive, and nothing limits the guarantees to a single paragraph.

Practice Test 15 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The clear statute prevails over the common law independence requirement, since no constitutional claim is raised): Ocean Port holds that, absent constitutional constraints, the legislature may specify a tribunal's degree of independence, and clear statutory language overrides the common law. The presumption of respect for natural justice operates only where wording is ambiguous. Here the text is explicit, so reading down or weighing Baker factors would rewrite it.
  2. The correct answer is B (The employer's right to know the case against it and to respond): The core of the duty of fairness is notice of the case to be met and a meaningful chance to answer it, and a decision resting on an argument the employer never saw denies both. Nothing in the facts suggests a lack of independence or bias, and reasons are a separate matter. The service rule exists precisely to protect this right.
  3. The correct answer is C (The Federal Court for the immigration officer, the Divisional Court for the municipal board): Federal boards, commissions and officers fall under s 18.1 of the Federal Courts Act, so review of the immigration officer goes to the Federal Court. A provincial superior court supervises provincial and municipal bodies, in Ontario through the Divisional Court. Neither court reviews the other's decision-makers, and the Courts of Appeal hear appeals rather than first-instance review applications.
  4. The correct answer is C (No: the statute permits overlapping roles, not individual prejudgment, so her remark can ground bias): Legislation can validate a structure in which one body investigates and decides, but it is not read as licensing a member to close her mind on the merits. A flat declaration of guilt goes beyond a provisional view formed at the investigation stage. Her participation on the panel after saying it would be enough to raise the apprehension.
  5. The correct answer is B (It does not set the standard but may inform what a reasonable decision looks like): Vavilov grounds the presumption of reasonableness in legislative intent, not in expertise as such, so expertise no longer selects the standard. It still matters inside the analysis, because a decision maker's experience can shape what counts as a reasonable outcome in context. It cannot, however, widen deference to questions reserved for correctness.
  6. The correct answer is D (Ask whether the judge chose and applied the right standard, focusing on the officer's decision): Under Agraira, the appellate court asks whether the reviewing judge selected the correct standard and applied it properly, which in practice means stepping into the judge's shoes and focusing on the administrative decision itself. Palpable and overriding error applies to facts, not to the choice of standard. The appeal is not a fresh correctness review of the officer.
  7. The correct answer is B (Appellate standards: correctness for questions of law, and palpable and overriding error for facts): Vavilov says that where the legislature provides an appeal, appellate standards from Housen apply. Treating the appeal as a rehearing ignores the deference owed to findings of fact. The last pairing reverses the correct allocation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Breach of the statutory notice period, a binding procedural precondition to a valid by-law): Where a statute prescribes a specific notice period before a by-law may be passed, compliance is a precondition to validity, and a shortfall is a ground for quashing even though the by-law is legislative in nature. Common law fairness does not displace or supplement a legislative scheme this explicit. The bias and unreasonableness arguments have no support in the facts given.
  9. The correct answer is D (The order targets her individually, so its policy rationale does not remove the duty of fairness): A policy rationale does not shield an order that picks out one person and changes her legal position. Knight looks at the character of the decision, and one aimed at a named landlord is individual in nature. Internal consultation cannot replace hearing from the affected person, and the quasi-judicial label is no longer required.
  10. The correct answer is C (Whether the Minister proportionately balanced the school's religious freedom against the curriculum's statutory objectives): Loyola applied the Doré approach to a discretionary decision affecting religious freedom, asking whether the decision-maker gave the protection proper weight when pursuing statutory aims. Having authority to set a curriculum does not end the inquiry, because the exercise of that authority must still respect the protection. The Oakes framing belongs to challenges against legislation.
  11. The correct answer is B (A declaration that the council breached its duty of fairness, as reinstatement is not possible): Quashing achieves little after the demolition, but a declaration can still record that the council acted unfairly and help support a damages claim. Certiorari does not itself decide entitlement to damages. Mandamus cannot require reconstruction as a remedy for a process failure, and an injunction cannot undo what is finished.
  12. The correct answer is A (Section 2(e) of the Bill of Rights, which does not require a liberty or security interest): Section 7 of the Charter requires a deprivation of life, liberty or security of the person, and purely economic loss does not qualify. Section 2(e) of the Bill of Rights is triggered by a federal determination of rights and obligations, so it needs no such interest. Section 11 applies to persons charged with offences, and section 15 concerns discrimination rather than the entitlement to a hearing.
  13. The correct answer is B (Serious credibility issues touching life or security require an oral hearing despite a statutory paper process): In Singh, the Supreme Court held that where credibility is central and life, liberty or security of the person is at stake, fundamental justice under s 7 requires an oral hearing. Baker concerned a humanitarian application where credibility was not the central issue, so no oral hearing was needed. A statute authorizing paper determinations cannot be read to displace constitutional fairness.
  14. The correct answer is B (Timely notice of the hearing and the substance of the allegations, enough to respond): Fairness requires that a person know the case to be met, which means timely notice of the hearing and the substance of the allegations, in enough detail to prepare a response. Court-style pleadings are not required in an administrative setting. Telling Dr. Hamel only of a practice review gave no warning of a fraud allegation, so notice was inadequate.
Case CHB65

The Nechako Ridge Band holds treaty trapping and hunting rights. The federal Northern Land Board, which has final authority under its statute, must decide whether to permit a 90 km winter road across Crown land. The road would cut through the Band's main trapping territory and open it to outside hunters. The Board gave the Band ten days' notice and held one open house. It ignored the Band's written objections and approved the permit.

  1. The correct answer is A (Yes, because the Crown knew of the treaty rights and the road might adversely affect them): The duty is triggered by Crown knowledge of a right, whether asserted or established, together with contemplated conduct that may adversely affect it. Treaty rights are the stronger case for consultation, not an exception to it. A statutory regulator exercising the Crown's authority can be the vehicle through which the duty is met, and actual loss need not be proven in advance.
  2. The correct answer is C (Deeper consultation, because the treaty right is established and the road threatens serious harm): The content of the duty varies with the strength of the right and the seriousness of the adverse effect. A proven treaty right and a road that removes the Band's main trapping area both point to the deeper end of the spectrum. Even then, the duty means meaningful dialogue and possible accommodation, not a requirement of consent.
  3. The correct answer is B (Quash the permit and return the matter so the Crown can consult the Band properly): A decision made in breach of the duty to consult is set aside and sent back so the consultation can be done properly. A finality clause does not oust review for a constitutional shortfall. The court should not take over the permit decision or foreclose the road, since the Crown may still approve it after adequate consultation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Early prohibition may be granted, since a reasonable apprehension of bias would infect the entire hearing): Early intervention is exceptional under C.B. Powell, but an adjudicator who investigated this very file and publicly declared Ms. Ivanova guilty has prejudged the matter in a way that makes the hearing futile, and no later decision on the same file can cure it. These facts go beyond a bare allegation of bias, so the court may grant prohibition now. Prohibition is available for bias, and the rule that interim rulings wait for the final outcome yields in such an exceptional case.
  5. The correct answer is C (No common law hearing is owed, because rule-making of general application is legislative in character): Delegated rule-making that binds all members equally is legislative, so the common law of fairness does not require a hearing. Heavier personal impact does not change that, because the rule is not aimed at him. Any consultation duty would have to come from the statute.
  6. The correct answer is C (The application fails, because judicial review of a provincial board lies in the provincial superior court): The Federal Court reviews only federal boards, meaning bodies exercising powers under federal law. What matters is the source of the decision-maker's authority, not the applicant's incorporation or the reach of its business. A Manitoba board's decision is reviewed in the Court of King's Bench of Manitoba.
  7. The correct answer is B (He fails, because an office held at pleasure carries no duty of fairness on removal): Knight confirms that the holder of an office at pleasure is not owed a duty of fairness on removal, because the office carries no security of tenure to protect. Mr. Leclerc therefore has no right to a hearing or to reasons. The Governor in Council is not immune from judicial review, and reasons are not a freestanding minimum for every removal, so the other reasoning fails.
  8. The correct answer is B (Conflicting answers across decision-makers would undermine the rule of law, so courts settle the question): Vavilov grounds this category in the rule of law: some legal questions need a single, determinate answer for the whole legal system, and consistency cannot be left to many decision-makers. The Court moved away from relative expertise or an implied appeal right as the basis. Counsel's own concession that significance is limited also means the category would not apply on these facts.
  9. The correct answer is D (Ms Sandhu bears the burden of showing the decision is unreasonable, as the party challenging it): Under Vavilov the party challenging the decision must satisfy the court that it is unreasonable. The impact on the applicant affects how much justification the court expects, not who carries the burden. No shifting of the burden occurs merely because some error is shown.
  10. The correct answer is D (The Superior Court supervises tribunals under the Code of Civil Procedure, not through common law writs): Quebec is a civil law province and does not use the common law writs. Its Superior Court holds a supervisory power over tribunals, now set out in the Code of Civil Procedure, and the court can quash or correct decisions through that power. The Ontario colleague describes common law practice, and neither internal appeals nor the Court of Appeal displace the Superior Court's role.
  11. The correct answer is C (Test the regulation against the statute's purpose and any available record, presuming it valid): Regulations are legislative instruments, so no duty to give reasons arises and none is needed for the court to assess consistency with the enabling statute. The court looks at the regulation's text, context, statutory purpose and any available record of its rationale, starting from a presumption of validity. Setting the right wage is policy, not a legal question for the court.
  12. The correct answer is B (Whether its enforcement power derives from municipal legislation and serves a public regulatory purpose): The question is whether the decision-maker exercises a power with a public character, and the source of the power and its regulatory purpose are central. A bylaw-based power to ticket and tow regulates the public on city streets. Corporate form, share ownership and a payment relationship may inform the analysis but do not decide it.
  13. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because Vavilov treats constitutional questions about the validity of legislation as requiring one final answer): Vavilov lists constitutional questions as a category requiring correctness, because the rule of law demands a single definitive ruling on whether legislation complies with the Constitution. Doré reasonableness governs discretionary decisions that limit Charter protections, not a challenge to the validity of the statutory provision itself. The tribunal's expertise does not displace that rule.
  14. The correct answer is C (The label is not the test; closeness to the judicial model sets the procedure owed): Since Nicholson and Baker, the duty of fairness applies to administrative decisions affecting rights, interests or privileges, and the older labels no longer gate it. The question is how closely the process resembles judicial decision-making, which affects how much procedure is owed. Standard of review is a separate inquiry and does not drive procedural content.
  15. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because the approval contradicts a statutory conformity requirement that the reasons do not address): The governing statute is a legal constraint on the decision, and a mandatory conformity requirement limits the committee's discretion. Approving a tower far above the cap without any explanation fails the justification requirement. Bad faith is not needed, and the plan binds because the statute says so.
  16. The correct answer is B (A duty applies because reputation and livelihood are affected, though protective mandates may narrow its content): A public announcement naming a professional as under investigation affects reputation and livelihood, so a duty applies even though the regulator's protective mandate may narrow what is owed. Treating publication as a mere preliminary step ignores its harm. A power to disclose does not displace the common-law duty without clear words.
Case CHB66

Extract from the fictional Correctional Segregation Act: s 12 provides that the warden may place an inmate in segregation for up to 15 days where necessary for safety, after notice and a hearing. Inmate Colm Brodeur was placed in segregation by Warden Ruth Easton for 40 days without notice or hearing. A judge later declared this a breach of Charter s 7 and ended segregation. Brodeur claims damages under s 24(1). The Crown relies on Ward's countervailing factors and says Easton acted on a mistaken but honest reading of the Act.

  1. The correct answer is D (A Charter breach, and that damages would serve compensation, vindication or deterrence on these facts): Ward requires a proven breach and then a functional justification: damages must serve compensation, vindication of the right, or deterrence of future breaches. Only then do countervailing factors and quantum arise. Bad faith, invalidity of the statute and the absence of other remedies are not threshold elements for an award against an official who misapplies a valid law.
  2. The correct answer is B (Good faith is no bar here, but it bears on deterrence, vindication and quantum): Charter damages do not require bad faith, so an honest mistake is not a defence. The warden ignored the Act's own safeguards of notice, a hearing and a 15 day limit. The degree of fault remains relevant to how much deterrence and vindication are needed and so to the size of the award.
  3. The correct answer is B (Quantum is a fair and appropriate sum guided by the objectives served, not a tort calculation): Ward directs a fair and appropriate sum for the particular breach, shaped by compensation, vindication and deterrence and moderated by proportionality, rather than a mechanical import of tort rules. Deterrence is one objective, not the sole one, and damages are not confined to a separate action, since a court granting relief under s 24(1) may fix the amount.
  4. The correct answer is D (Section 11 is inapplicable to this internal discipline, but section 7 can still be engaged): Internal prison discipline exists to maintain order in the institution rather than to punish crimes against society, so it does not engage section 11 even though the conduct resembles an offence and the sanction is harsh. Segregation can nonetheless affect residual liberty and security, so section 7 and fairness obligations remain available. Inmates do not lose Charter protection on entering prison.
Case CHB67

The Residential Tenancy Tribunal Act lets the Tribunal 'make rules governing its practice and procedure, consistent with this Act.' Rule 12 requires any adjournment request to be filed ten days before the hearing. Two days before her hearing, landlord Marguerite Osei learned her only witness had been hospitalized. She filed a written request with a medical note. The registrar refused it, citing Rule 12 alone, and the member heard the matter without her witness and ruled against her.

  1. The correct answer is B (The express power to make procedural rules covers adjournment timing, so Rule 12 is validly authorized): The Act expressly authorizes rules on practice and procedure, and the timing of adjournment requests is plainly procedural. Authority to make a rule is a separate question from whether the rule is applied fairly in a given case. The argument that every procedural matter must be listed confuses a specific enumeration with a general enabling power, which is what the Act gives.
  2. The correct answer is D (They fettered their discretion by applying Rule 12 mechanically, ignoring whether fairness required an adjournment): A tribunal rule is a guide to how discretion will ordinarily be exercised, not a command that displaces fairness. Treating the ten-day cut-off as absolute meant no one asked whether losing the only witness to an emergency justified a short delay. Review for bad faith is too narrow, because procedural fairness is assessed by the court itself without deference to the member's view.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quash the decision and remit the matter for a new hearing before the Tribunal): A breach of procedural fairness ordinarily results in the decision being set aside and the matter returned for a new hearing, because the court cannot know what the missing witness would have said. Courts decline a remedy on futility grounds only where the result was inevitable, which cannot be said when the central witness was unavailable. Substituting the court's own findings is reserved for rare cases where only one outcome is possible.
  4. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because the dispute concerns the jurisdictional boundary between two or more administrative bodies): Where two or more administrative bodies claim authority over the same matter, the rule of law requires a single, definitive answer, so correctness applies. This is the one place where the jurisdictional idea survives after Vavilov. Each body's reading of its own statute would otherwise attract reasonableness, which is the tempting but wrong answer here.
Case CHB68

Extract from the fictional Correctional Facilities Act, s 44: "The Warden may transfer an inmate to another institution where necessary for the safe and orderly operation of the institution." Ms. Rebecca Anderson, 34, serving a sentence at a Manitoba women's facility, receives daily opioid-substitution treatment prescribed by her physician. The Warden orders her moved 600 km north to a facility without a clinic, citing overcrowding. Treatment would be interrupted for at least three weeks. Her lawyer wrote that the move threatens her security of the person under s 7. The Warden's letter does not mention treatment.

  1. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, applying the Doré proportionality framework because the discretion affects a protected interest): A discretionary decision that affects a Charter-protected interest is reviewed for reasonableness, with the Doré proportionality framework supplying the analysis. Correctness is reserved for categories such as constitutional division of powers questions. Patent unreasonableness disappeared after Dunsmuir, and an operational statute cannot exempt the Warden from the Charter.
  2. The correct answer is A (That the safety objective was weighed against the treatment interruption and the balance was proportionate): Doré asks the decision-maker to identify the statutory objective and weigh it against the impact on the protected interest, reaching a proportionate result. It is a flexible balancing, not the rigid stage-by-stage justification used for legislation. Consent and a formal evidentiary hearing are not preconditions to a proportionate limit.
  3. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because it ignores the raised treatment interruption and shows no weighing of the protected interest): Overcrowding is a legitimate objective, but the letter never engages the interruption of treatment that counsel expressly raised, so the protected interest was not weighed at all. Evidence offered afterwards in court cannot repair that gap, because the review concerns the decision actually made and its reasons. The defect lies in the proportionality analysis, not in the absence of an oral hearing.
  4. The correct answer is A (No, he needs an action, or an order converting the application into an action): Judicial review relief in the Federal Court is public law relief such as quashing, declarations and mandamus, and damages are not among those remedies. A claimant must sue in an action, or ask the court to direct that the application be treated and proceeded with as an action. The Federal Court can hear damages claims against the federal Crown, so the jurisdictional objection fails.
  5. The correct answer is C (A presumption of regular decision-making applies, and speculation alone does not justify probing deliberations): Courts presume that tribunals decide properly and protect the secrecy of deliberations, as the Supreme Court did in Ellis-Don. A claimant needs something more than suspicion before the process is examined. A full-board meeting on policy is lawful in itself, and the burden does not shift to the tribunal to prove its own regularity.
  6. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because the dispute concerns the boundary between the jurisdictions of two administrative bodies): Vavilov names the determination of jurisdictional boundaries between two or more administrative bodies as a rule of law category requiring correctness review, because the legislative scheme cannot give both bodies the final say. Deference to the Tribunal's view of its own mandate would let one body decide the limits of the other. Heightened scrutiny is not a recognized standard.
  7. The correct answer is B (Follow the statute: correctness for the interpretation of the regulation, reasonableness for the factual findings): Vavilov requires courts to respect a standard the legislature has prescribed, and the direction here separates law from fact. Expertise cannot override it. A prescribed standard on judicial review is not an appeal, so Housen is not the governing framework.
  8. The correct answer is D (Palpable and overriding error, because appellate standards apply to findings of fact on a statutory appeal): Where the legislature creates an appeal to a court, Vavilov directs the court to use appellate standards, and the Housen standard for facts is palpable and overriding error. The width of the appeal clause does not turn factual findings into questions of law. The board's specialization is irrelevant to the standard once an appeal exists.
  9. The correct answer is A (The audit stage carried a minimal duty, and the later objection process offers a full response): At the audit stage the Agency is gathering information and no rights are finally decided, so the duty is minimal. The company's real protection is the later assessment with its right to object and appeal. Fairness is not confined to judicial or quasi-judicial decisions since Nicholson, so denying any duty is wrong, but demanding a hearing or caution at audit overshoots.
  10. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because it is a general question of law of central importance to the legal system): Vavilov keeps correctness for general questions of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole, and privilege is a standard example. The category did survive, unlike true jurisdiction. The reason is the need for uniform answers, not any comparison of expertise.
  11. The correct answer is A (The holding is too remote to be a direct interest or to suggest bias): Automatic disqualification is reserved for a direct and material interest in the outcome, and a trivial indirect stake in a pooled fund is neither. An informed person who thought the matter through would not apprehend bias from it. Non-disclosure may be unwise, but it does not turn a remote holding into a disqualifying one.
  12. The correct answer is D (By weighing the Baker factors in context, so the content of fairness fits this decision): Fairness is flexible and contextual, and its content is set by weighing the Baker factors against the particular decision. Neither a fixed minimum nor the old right and privilege or judicial and administrative labels settles the matter. A short suspension decided on a complaint may call for notice and a chance to respond rather than a full trial.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Appellate standards, so correctness for questions of law, since a statutory appeal exists): Where the legislature provides a statutory appeal, Vavilov directs the court to apply appellate standards, which means correctness for extricable questions of law. Reasonableness and its exceptions govern judicial review, not statutory appeals, and palpable and overriding error is reserved for fact and mixed questions.
  2. The correct answer is C (It is a general question of law of central importance to the whole legal system): Vavilov keeps correctness for general questions of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole, and the scope of solicitor-client privilege is a classic example. Lack of expertise is not the test, and Vavilov abandoned the true question of jurisdiction category. Heightened scrutiny is not a recognized standard.
  3. The correct answer is C (Section 2(e) applies only to determinations of rights and obligations, and a policy recommendation determines none): Section 2(e) is triggered when a federal body determines a person's rights and obligations, and an advisory board that only recommends a course of action decides nothing about any operator's legal position. Being affected is not enough on its own. The claim that commercial operators fall outside the Bill is not the reason, since the failure lies in the absence of a determination.
  4.
