The correct answer is B (She probably waived the objection by participating with knowledge and not raising it promptly): A party who knows of a ground for reasonable apprehension of bias and takes part without objecting at the first opportunity is generally taken to have waived it. The test for bias is reasonable apprehension, not proof of actual partiality, so demanding proof of actual bias states the wrong standard. Treating the defect as one that no conduct can waive overstates the rule.
  5. The correct answer is B (A meaningful chance to present evidence and have it fully considered, not necessarily an oral hearing): Baker found that the importance of the decision required a fair opportunity to present evidence and have it considered, but that an oral hearing was not necessarily required, as written submissions could suffice. The discretionary nature of the relief does not remove the duty. An independent tribunal is not demanded by the importance factor.
  6. The correct answer is A (Detention engages liberty, and fundamental justice requires meaningful review that weighs the length of detention): Immigration detention is a direct restriction of physical liberty, so section 7 applies. Prolonged detention without a meaningful review that can consider its length and alternatives falls short of fundamental justice. The administrative label does not reduce the protection, and a review limited to whether removal remains possible would ignore the factors that make long detention unjustified.
  7. The correct answer is B (Significant weight, especially where the statute leaves procedure to the agency, though fairness stays reviewable): Baker lists the agency's own choices of procedure as a factor, and respect is owed especially where the statute gives the agency latitude over its process. The court still decides whether the procedure was fair; it does not apply Vavilov reasonableness review to that question. Published rules inform the analysis but do not bind the court.
  8. The correct answer is D (It reviews the record before the minister, and the later study generally falls outside it): Judicial review examines the decision actually made on the material before the decision maker, subject to narrow exceptions for fresh evidence. A later study cannot show that the minister acted unreasonably on what was known. Whether reasons mention groundwater affects the justification analysis, not the content of the record.
  9. The correct answer is B (Ms. Park's income interest warrants a higher level of fairness than Mr. Ruiz's modest one-off payment): The importance of the interest affected informs how much fairness is owed. A benefit that supplies a person's income matters more than a modest one-off payment, so the content differs. The trigger is not a binary label, and a statutory classification of the claim as a right does not control.
Case CHB69

Extract, Harmon Human Rights Code, s 14: 'An employer shall accommodate an employee's religious practice to the point of undue hardship.' Sana Qureshi, a warehouse worker, asked to leave early on Fridays for prayer. The Human Rights Tribunal dismissed her complaint, finding the employer's shift schedule neutral and not asking whether accommodation was possible. The employer says staffing costs amount to undue hardship, but the Tribunal made no findings on that. Sana applied for judicial review, and the court found the Tribunal's reasons unreasonable for ignoring s 14. The court must now choose a remedy.

  1. The correct answer is B (Remit the complaint, since undue hardship is unassessed and the Tribunal is the proper fact finder): Remittal is the usual remedy because the legislature gave the complaint to the Tribunal, and the key factual question of undue hardship has not been decided. A reviewing court does not find facts or receive new evidence on the merits. Directing a finding or deciding the complaint would take over the Tribunal's statutory role.
  2. The correct answer is A (Direct the Tribunal to apply s 14, while leaving the findings on undue hardship to it): A court may clarify the governing legal framework when it remits, for instance that s 14 must be applied, which guides the redetermination without taking it over. It should not dictate findings, the weight of evidence or the accommodation itself, which belong to the Tribunal. Withholding all legal guidance is not required and would invite a repeat of the error.
  3. The correct answer is C (Delay, cost to the parties and efficient use of public resources, where remitting would be pointless): Vavilov names delay, cost, urgency and the efficient use of public resources as factors that may justify a court deciding the matter, mainly where remitting would serve no purpose. A court's own view of the better statutory reading, or the gravity of the error alone, does not displace the legislature's selection of decision-maker. The need for a remedy is also not enough by itself.
  4. The correct answer is C (No, relief is discretionary, and unexplained delay can justify refusing it despite the breach): Remedies on judicial review are discretionary, and long unexplained delay is a recognised reason to refuse relief even after a breach is proved. The thirty day federal period does not govern a provincial college, so that reasoning fails even though it points to the same outcome. Treating relief as automatic ignores the court's discretion.
  5. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness review remains, although the broad policy discretion informs what the statute permits): Subjective wording such as 'if satisfied' widens the discretion but does not remove review: the decision must still fall within the statute's limits and be justified in light of them. The breadth of the policy discretion informs what is reasonable. A bad faith only test reflects older deference, and cabinet's lack of expertise does not trigger correctness.
  6. The correct answer is C (Prohibition, which restrains a tribunal from proceeding with a matter outside its authority): Prohibition restrains a decision-maker from acting beyond its legal authority and is the forward-looking remedy, used before the hearing takes place. Certiorari looks backward at a decision already made, and mandamus compels action rather than stopping it. Habeas corpus concerns detention, which is not at stake for Mr. Chow.
  7. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness presumptively applies, because an appeal to another tribunal is not an appeal to a court): Vavilov ties the appellate standards to a statutory right of appeal to a court. An internal administrative appeal does not create one, and the application is a judicial review, so the presumption of reasonableness stands. The court does not adopt the Tribunal's own standard.
Case CHB70

Mr Haddad's application for judicial review of a negative pre-removal risk assessment was dismissed by a Federal Court judge on reasonableness review. The judge certified a question: whether an officer must consider the cumulative effect of discrimination in assessing risk. Mr Haddad also believes the officer breached procedural fairness, a ground the judge rejected but did not certify. He appeals, and the Federal Court of Appeal later dismisses his appeal.

  1. The correct answer is C (Within 30 days after the Federal Court's judgment is pronounced, as provided for final judgments): The Federal Courts Act sets 30 days for an appeal from a final judgment, counted from its pronouncement. The 15 day period belongs to the initial leave application at the Federal Court. Certification does not restart the clock.
  2. The correct answer is D (Yes, because the appeal is from the judgment, so other issues may be considered): Certification is the gateway that opens the appeal, but the appeal lies from the judgment itself. The Federal Court of Appeal may therefore address other issues that arise, as it sees fit. No separate leave or amendment is required.
  3. The correct answer is A (He must apply for leave under the Supreme Court Act within 60 days, showing importance): Appeals to the Supreme Court in this setting require leave, which turns on whether the case raises an issue of public importance. Certification in the Federal Court does not create a right of appeal to the Supreme Court. The 60 day period comes from the Supreme Court Act, not the Federal Courts Act.
  4. The correct answer is B (Invite both parties to make submissions on the new point before she decides the grievance): A decision-maker who wishes to rest the decision on a point the parties have not addressed should give them notice and a chance to speak to it. Expertise lets her apply specialized knowledge to the issues argued but does not replace the parties' right to be heard on a new issue. Citing authorities in her reasons would only reveal the problem after the decision is made.
  5. The correct answer is B (Dismiss it as premature because an adequate review route within the college remains unused): Courts generally decline to intervene while an adequate alternative remedy or internal review is available and no exceptional circumstances are shown. The court does not lack jurisdiction, so transfer is wrong, and nothing in the facts suggests a natural justice exception. The effect of the unused panel review is that his application is premature.
  6. The correct answer is A (Drafting help is allowed, but delegating the credibility assessment and the actual decision is not): A decision-maker may use staff for research and drafting, but the person the statute charges with deciding must make the decision, including credibility findings, herself. Here the staff lawyer made the credibility assessment, so the decision was in substance delegated. Adopting the document by signature does not cure that, while a blanket ban on staff assistance goes too far.
  7. The correct answer is A (The duty rests with the Crown alone, so the company cannot be liable for breaching it): The duty to consult flows from the honour of the Crown, so it is owed by the Crown, which may delegate procedural aspects to a proponent but keeps ultimate responsibility for the adequacy of consultation. Haida Nation says a third party cannot be held liable for failing to discharge the Crown's duty, although the Crown may rely on the company's work. The Nation's remedy therefore lies against the Crown's decision, not in a damages claim against the company.
  8. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness review applies, taking account of the council's democratic accountability and the enabling statute): Delegated legislation is presumptively reviewed for reasonableness, measured against the enabling statute and the context, including the municipal council's democratic character. Municipal by-laws are not immune from review, and they are not treated like statutes of a sovereign legislature. A bare rational-basis test is a foreign import.
Case CHB71

Extract from the fictional Municipal Streets Act: 's 12(1) A council may by by-law delegate to the municipal clerk the power to issue street-closure permits. (2) The clerk may not further delegate that power.' The Council of Millbrook passed such a by-law. Clerk Anita Voss, overwhelmed before a festival, signed a letter authorizing Festival Services Inc., a private firm, to issue permits. The firm issued a permit to Dev Mistry for a block party on Elm Street.

  1. The correct answer is B (The clerk was forbidden to delegate further, so the firm had no authority to issue permits): The Act allows delegation from council to the clerk and expressly stops the clerk delegating further, so the clerk's letter was outside the clerk's power. Supervision or later approval does not change the fact that the body issuing the permit lacked authority. Council's by-law delegated only to the clerk, so the suggestion that it authorized the firm directly fails.
  2. The correct answer is B (Delegated legislation whose validity depends on being authorized by the Act, not on Council's own sovereignty): A by-law made under an enabling Act is delegated legislation: a municipal council has no sovereign legislative power, so its by-law is valid only if the Act authorizes it. It is general in nature rather than an individual decision, so no hearing right arises. Ministerial approval is not a condition of validity unless the Act says so.
  3. The correct answer is D (Its powers come from statute, read generously and purposively, but cannot extend beyond the Act's limits): A municipality has only the powers the legislature gave it, but the modern approach reads municipal powers broadly and purposively within the Act's limits. Reading them narrowly with doubt resolved against the council reflects an older approach, and inherent local powers do not exist. The narrower statement captures the source of power but misses the interpretive approach.
  4. The correct answer is C (The court may withhold costs from an unsuccessful litigant who raised genuine public interest issues): Costs are discretionary, and courts may depart from the loser-pays rule where the losing party brought a genuine public interest case on issues that transcend its own interests. Public interest character is therefore relevant to costs and not only to standing. An advance costs order is a separate remedy and not a precondition to a favourable exercise of discretion.
  5. The correct answer is D (Decline to reweigh the reports, and ask whether the tribunal's preference was justified and intelligible): Absent exceptional circumstances a reviewing court does not reweigh or reassess the evidence considered by the decision maker. It does, however, ask whether the way the tribunal handled the evidence was justified, so refusing to look at the weighing goes too far. Treating the question as mixed fact and law imports appellate thinking into judicial review.
  6. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because a division of powers question is settled by the courts despite the privative clause): Vavilov lists constitutional questions, including the division of powers and paramountcy, as an exception to the presumption of reasonableness. A privative clause or expertise cannot move the final word on the constitutional boundary away from the courts. The patent unreasonableness standard was abolished as a common law category and the home statute does not change the analysis.
  7. The correct answer is C (His own deliberate avoidance of the process may disentitle him, since remedies depend on applicant conduct): Judicial review remedies are discretionary, and an applicant who deliberately avoided the process he now criticizes may be refused relief. Licensing decisions affecting livelihood do attract a duty of fairness, so denying any duty is wrong. The suggestion that relief follows automatically from proven unfairness ignores the court's discretion over remedy.
  8. The correct answer is D (The order binds until set aside on appeal; she cannot challenge it in the contempt hearing): Wilson holds that a court order is valid and must be obeyed until set aside through appeal or other proper proceedings, so it cannot be attacked indirectly in contempt proceedings. The nullity argument is the attractive near miss, but it is rejected for orders of a court of competent jurisdiction, and a Charter argument does not alter the route.
  9. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, because applying settled privilege law to specific documents is not a general legal question): The category covers general questions of law whose answer matters to the whole legal system, such as the scope of the privilege. Applying an agreed test to particular documents is a fact-sensitive application of settled law, so the presumption of reasonableness stays in place. Correctness would apply only if the board had redefined or narrowed the privilege itself.
  10. The correct answer is D (No hearing was owed, because general by-laws are legislative and attract no common law fairness): Procedural fairness at common law attaches to administrative decisions affecting individual rights, privileges or interests, not to legislative acts of general application. A by-law applying across the town is legislative, so no hearing or reasons were owed unless the enabling statute requires notice or consultation. Baker applies to individualized decisions, not to this kind of enactment.
  11. The correct answer is C (As part of the reasons, read with the letter, as Baker recognized for officers' notes): In Baker the Supreme Court accepted that an immigration officer's notes could stand as the reasons for the decision. A court therefore reads the form letter and the notes together to see the officer's actual analysis. Requiring a formal document or a later affidavit would exalt form over the real record of reasoning.
  12. The correct answer is C (It is not binding, but she may still seek mandamus if its legal test is met): Ombudsman recommendations are persuasive and non-binding; their force comes from publicity and political accountability, not legal compulsion. The Ombudsman route does not replace judicial review, so a court could still order a decision made if the applicant meets the test for mandamus, including a public legal duty to act and a demand and refusal. The finding of maladministration neither bars review nor creates a damages entitlement.
  13. The correct answer is B (The application is premature, since the letter is only a step in an unfinished process): A request for more information is an interim step in a process that has not produced a final decision, and under C.B. Powell the court does not review such steps absent exceptional circumstances. Any complaint about the request can be raised if the final decision is adverse. Asking for withdrawal is not a precondition, and a mere burden does not make the step reviewable.
  14. The correct answer is B (Yes, a hearing de novo before the review board can cure the earlier defect): Under Harelkin, a defect at first instance can be cured by an appeal that is a full rehearing and itself meets the standard of fairness. Here the review board hears the whole matter with notice, disclosure and a chance to respond. The view that a first instance breach can never be repaired is the old nullity approach, which Canadian law no longer applies so rigidly.
  15. The correct answer is C (It must fit text, context and purpose, though no formal interpretive exercise is required): Vavilov confirms the modern principle: words are read in their entire context, in their grammatical and ordinary sense, harmoniously with the scheme and object of the Act. The decision-maker need not perform a formal interpretation, but its reading must be consistent with those constraints. Accepting any plausible meaning resembles the American deference approach, which Canadian law does not adopt.
  16. The correct answer is A (The regulation is invalid to the extent it conflicts with the Act that authorizes it): A regulation is subordinate to its parent Act and is invalid to the extent it conflicts with it, so it cannot permit a larger deposit than the Act caps. The idea that later or more specific rules prevail applies between enactments of equal rank, not between a statute and a regulation made under it. A policy rationale or consultation cannot rescue the conflict.
  17. The correct answer is D (The flaws are serious enough to go to the root of the decision's justification): Under reasonableness review the burden rests on the party challenging the decision, and a court intervenes only where a shortcoming is sufficiently central or significant. Clerical or stylistic slips that leave the officer's justification intact do not meet that threshold. Shifting the burden to the Minister, or asking what the judge would have decided, misdescribes the exercise.
  18. The correct answer is B (Clear statutory wording can displace common law independence guarantees, so the enabling statute sets the baseline): Ocean Port held that a legislature may, by clear language, decide how independent a tribunal must be, so the at pleasure wording governs unless the statute itself is constitutionally infirm. The Valente guarantees were designed for courts and are not automatically demanded of tribunals. Mr. Tessier therefore cannot use an unwritten principle to override an express legislative choice.
Case CHB72

Town of Millbrook Act extract: s 40(1) Council may by by-law regulate or prohibit short-term rentals. (2) Council shall publish notice of a proposed by-law in a local newspaper at least ten days before enactment. In January, council told the owners' association it would meet owners before any rental by-law. Council published the required notice in March and enacted a by-law banning rentals townwide. Priya Nair, who runs three rentals, received no personal notice. In June, a second by-law revoked only the permit for her property at 14 Mill Street, citing tenant complaints.

  1. The correct answer is A (No common law duty applies to legislation of general application, so the statutory notice suffices): Procedural fairness does not extend to legislative decisions of general application, and a municipal by-law applying townwide is one. The statute sets the notice that was owed, and council gave it. Owners' financial stake does not turn a general rule into an individual decision.
  2. The correct answer is A (Despite its form, it targets one owner on allegations about her conduct, so fairness applies): Courts look at the substance of a decision, not its legislative form. A measure aimed at one identified owner because of alleged conduct is in effect an individual decision, and fairness applies. The statute's silence on further procedure does not oust the common law.
  3. The correct answer is C (Legitimate expectations do not apply to legislative decisions, so the January commitment cannot invalidate the by-law): The doctrine is part of procedural fairness and so stops where fairness stops: at the enactment of general legislation. Council may have acted poorly, but the commitment gives the court no basis to quash the by-law. Reliance and mandamus both presuppose an enforceable expectation that does not exist here.
  4. The correct answer is B (No fairness duty attaches to general legislative acts, so only procedures in the statute are required): Decisions of a legislative nature that set general rules for a broad class do not attract a common law duty of fairness, and any required procedure comes from the statute or the Constitution. The economic impact on Lars does not turn a general regulation into an individual decision. Legitimate expectations arise only from clear representations or practice, and none appears here.
  5. The correct answer is B (As admissible context, weighed for reliability and subordinate to the clear meaning of the text): Canadian interpretation permits legislative history and debates as relevant context, though the weight depends on how reliable and probative the material is, and the text still governs. The old exclusionary rule has been abandoned, and nothing makes the material binding or dependent on the parties' consent.
  6. The correct answer is C (Whether an informed observer, thinking it through, would conclude the chair likely could not decide fairly): Canada applies the test from Committee for Justice and Liberty v NEB: the apprehension must be reasonable and held by an informed person viewing the matter realistically and practically. Proof of actual bias is not required, and a party's subjective feelings do not settle the question. The English real danger test is not the Canadian standard, and past professional work alone rarely meets the high threshold.
  7. The correct answer is D (Counsel is not required, because the stakes are modest and the issues are simple): Whether fairness requires counsel turns on the seriousness of the charge, the complexity of the case and the person's capacity to present it. A minor charge with a small fine and no loss of residual liberty engages none of these strongly. A general constitutional right to publicly funded counsel in administrative settings has not been recognized.
  8. The correct answer is C (Ask whether the decision reflects a proportionate balance, accepting outcomes within a reasonable range): Doré keeps the reviewing court in a deferential role: it asks whether the decision-maker's balance of the protection and the statutory objectives is proportionate, and it accepts outcomes within a range. It does not rerun the balance itself. The Oakes framework is for testing legislation, and a bare mention of the Charter is not enough.

Practice Test 17 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Quash the approval and remit to the Crown for consultation that responds to the fishery concerns): Where the approval is the legal trigger for construction and was issued without meaningful consultation, the usual remedy is to quash it and send it back so the Crown can consult properly before any new decision. A bare declaration would leave an unlawful permission operating, which is why it is not the best choice when construction has not started. Prohibiting the project outright goes beyond the remedy for a process defect, because the duty to consult gives no veto.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness, because the Minister is an administrative decision maker and no appeal lies to a court): Under Vavilov, reasonableness is the presumptive standard for administrative decision makers, and appellate standards apply only where the legislature has provided a statutory appeal to a court. An appeal to a minister is an administrative route, not a court appeal, so the Minister's decision is reviewed for reasonableness. Applying correctness because the Minister sat on an appeal confuses the label with the kind of appeal Vavilov recognises.
  3. The correct answer is C (No appealable question of law arises when a settled test is simply applied to facts): Applying an uncontested legal test to the facts is a question of mixed fact and law, and where the legislature limited the appeal to questions of law, an error in application without an extricable legal error falls outside the appeal. Correctness applies only to legal questions, not to every part of a decision. Palpable and overriding error would be relevant only if the appeal reached mixed questions, and expertise-based reasonableness is not the appellate standard.
  4. The correct answer is C (By asking what consequence the legislature intended for lateness, in light of scheme and purpose): The mandatory and directory labels do not settle the question; the consequence of breach is a matter of legislative intent found through text, context and purpose. The imperative word "must" is relevant but not decisive, and the English prejudice presumption is not a Canadian rule of interpretation.
  5. The correct answer is A (Section 1(a) of the Canadian Bill of Rights, protecting enjoyment of property and due process): Section 1(a) of the Bill of Rights expressly protects the enjoyment of property and the right not to be deprived of it except by due process, and it binds federal actors. Charter section 7 does not protect property, and the facts disclose no liberty or security interest. Section 8 addresses privacy in searches and section 11 requires a charge of an offence, neither of which is the due process complaint here.
  6. The correct answer is A (It cannot oust superior court review for legality, so the court reviews the decision for reasonableness): Under Vavilov a privative clause does not displace the presumption of reasonableness review, and the rule of law requires that superior courts keep their supervisory role over the legality of administrative action. A one-line explanation that ignores the central argument is exactly what reasonableness review examines. Reading the clause down to jurisdictional error would revive a pre-Vavilov category that the Court abandoned.
  7. The correct answer is B (No, because reasons need only meaningfully grapple with the central issues, not every minor submission): Vavilov does not demand that a decision maker respond to every argument or set out every finding. What matters is whether the reasons meaningfully deal with the key issues and central arguments. Silence on minor points is not a flaw sufficiently serious to undermine the decision, and a court should not invent reasoning to cover a real gap.
  8. The correct answer is C (No breach, because the published notice withdrew the practice before she applied for renewal): An expectation from practice is defeated when the agency clearly announces a change before the claimant acts. Renata applied after the practice was publicly withdrawn, so no continuing expectation existed for her. Agencies may change their procedures prospectively; the common law minimum of fairness still applies to the new process.
  9. The correct answer is D (No, a limited written appeal cannot replace the hearing he was entitled to at first instance): Cure depends on whether the later proceeding gives the person the hearing that was missing. An appeal limited to law on a written record, with no new evidence, cannot give Mr. Osei the chance to present his case that fairness required at first instance. The bare existence of an appeal right does not repair the defect, and later reasons cannot stand in for a hearing.
  10. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because disputes about the boundary between two administrative bodies require one determinate answer): Vavilov recognizes jurisdictional boundary disputes between administrative bodies as a rule of law exception requiring correctness, so that overlapping mandates are resolved consistently. It does not depend on the level of government, so the option limiting correctness to regulators from different governments is wrong. The broader Dunsmuir true question of jurisdiction category was abandoned, so the option reviving it misstates the source of correctness, and deference on the ground of expertise does not apply to this category.
  11. The correct answer is C (Judicial review does not apply; the court can examine only his employment contract, not the expulsion): A voluntary religious association exercises no statutory power, so public law review is unavailable and courts do not supervise membership decisions as such. Daniel's contract with the Fellowship is a legal right, so a private law claim about its termination may proceed. The financial consequence of expulsion does not make the membership decision itself reviewable, and the Charter does not apply to private bodies directly.
  12. The correct answer is B ('May' confers discretion, but it must be exercised reasonably in light of the Act's purposes): A grant of power by 'may' confers discretion, but discretion must be exercised reasonably and within the statute's purposes. The Director cannot treat the decision as unreviewable, and refusing a recipient who proved need, without reasons, invites scrutiny. Reading 'may' as 'must' goes too far, since the statute still leaves a real decision to make about the allowance.
  13. The correct answer is B (Disclosure of the relevant material, so she knows the case and can respond properly): Fairness in a discipline hearing requires disclosure of the relevant material in the regulator's hands so the member knows the case and can answer it. This is not the full criminal disclosure model, but it goes well beyond a one-line summary. Cross-examining at the hearing is no substitute for preparing in advance.
  14. The correct answer is A (Yes, because the decision seriously affects her livelihood, and fairness requires written reasons despite statutory silence): Baker held that reasons are required where a decision has a significant effect on an individual, and Vavilov reinforces the expectation of reasons in that setting. The loss of a long-established business meets that standard even though the statute says nothing about reasons. Tying reasons to a statutory appeal right, or to a judicial label, reflects older thinking that Canadian law has abandoned.
  15. The correct answer is C (No automatic right to counsel arises; need depends on gravity, complexity and his ability to self-represent): Whether counsel is needed in an administrative proceeding depends on context: the gravity of the possible penalty, the complexity of the issues and the person's ability to present his own case. A two-day loss of a minor privilege on undisputed facts sits at the low end. The administrative label does not by itself bar counsel in more serious matters.
  16. The correct answer is B (Fairness applies: a public caution seriously affects reputation, so notice and a reply are owed): Screening is ordinarily preliminary, but this committee can finish the matter itself with a lasting public sanction. That serious effect brings the duty back, though in a moderate form: notice and a chance to answer rather than a full hearing. Waiting for a later hearing is no answer because a caution means there may never be one.
  17. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness review under Vavilov, with the enabling statute's scope and purposes as the key constraint): Vavilov's framework governs review of the validity of delegated legislation, with reasonableness as the standard. The regulation must stay within the limits set by the enabling statute's wording, context and purpose, which is where the charity's argument is tested. A separate, narrower test is no longer used.
  18. The correct answer is D (It is barred to the extent of the appeal, because a statutory appeal channels review): Where Parliament expressly provides an appeal from a federal board's decision, the Federal Courts Act excludes judicial review of that decision to the extent it can be appealed. Bias can be raised in the appeal itself. The Federal Court cannot convert the application, and the two courts do not share first-instance jurisdiction here.
  19. The correct answer is C (A specialized appeal procedure with expertise suited to the issue, showing exclusive legislative intent): Maybrun directs courts to look at the wording and purpose of the statute, the availability of an appeal, the expertise and accessibility of the specialized body, the nature of the attack and the penalty. A specialized, accessible appeal procedure points strongly to exclusivity. Penalty severity is only one factor and does not decide the question alone.
  20. The correct answer is C (Unreasonable if the reading cannot be reconciled with the provision's text, context and purpose): A decision maker need not perform a formal interpretive exercise, but the outcome must be justifiable in light of text, context and purpose. A purely lexical reading that ignores the scheme's evident purpose is vulnerable. The absence of a formal analysis is not itself the flaw, which is why the claim about a missing formal analysis fails.
  21. The correct answer is A (No breach arises, because members keep discretion and an informed observer would see no compromise): Guidance that promotes consistency is compatible with independence when each member remains free to depart from it and to decide on the evidence. Binding or coercive directions would raise a real concern, but these guidelines are advisory. The argument that any guidance breaches independence overstates the rule.
  22. The correct answer is B (The board departed from settled practice without explaining why, a departure that must be justified): Vavilov treats the decision maker's past practice as part of the legal and factual context that constrains a reasonable decision. A board may change course, but a departure from its settled approach must be explained. Administrative bodies are not bound by strict precedent, Canadian law does not give substantive effect to expectations in this way, and the absence of an oral hearing is a fairness point, not a reasonableness one.
  23. The correct answer is D (Section 7 is not engaged, since ordinary stress and stigma fall short of profound psychological harm): Under Blencoe, security of the person includes psychological integrity only where state action causes serious and profound harm, and the stress and stigma that ordinarily accompany a proceeding do not reach that level. Reputation and the freedom to practise are not themselves protected interests. Section 7 is not limited to criminal matters, so the civil label is not the reason the claim fails.
  24. The correct answer is B (Ask whether the transfer deprives him of residual liberty, then whether fundamental justice was respected): Section 7 analysis has two stages: first a deprivation of life, liberty or security of the person, then a check against the principles of fundamental justice. A significant tightening of a prisoner's confinement can deprive residual liberty. The Baker factors and Vavilov address common law fairness and substantive review, which are different inquiries and do not replace the trigger question.
Case CHB73

Ayesha Mahmood appeals a taxi licence refusal before the City Licensing Appeal Panel on March 3. At the outset she recognizes panel member Len Pritchard as her former landlord, who sued her in 2021. She says nothing, hoping he might favour her. Midway, the panel rules against her on a preliminary point. She then objects to his sitting. The panel refuses and later upholds the refusal.

  1. The correct answer is A (No: knowing the ground at the outset, she had to object then, so waiver is likely): A party who knows of a ground of bias must raise it at the earliest opportunity, and silence while hoping for a favourable result is treated as waiver. Waiting for the panel's rulings turns the objection into a tactical decision made after seeing how things went. No rule requires a written objection before the hearing date.
  2. The correct answer is D (A party may not await the result, and early objection lets the tribunal cure the problem): Allowing a party to hold a known objection in reserve would let it gamble on a favourable result and attack the process only after losing. Raising the point early also gives the tribunal a chance to recuse the member or reconstitute the panel. The tribunal does not lose jurisdiction, and the duty to object rests on the party as well.
  3. The correct answer is A (She did not recognize Pritchard until midway, and waiver requires knowledge of the facts): Waiver must be informed, so a party who did not know the facts cannot be taken to have given up the objection by staying quiet. Self-representation, the panel's failure to invite objections and the seriousness of the stakes may colour how clearly waiver is found, but none of them removes a party's own knowledge. Only lack of knowledge goes to the root of the waiver.
  4. The correct answer is B (Fairness required disclosure of the allegation and a chance to respond, given the livelihood at stake): Under Baker, the importance of the decision to the individual weighs heavily, and the right to practise a profession is a significant interest. Statutory silence leaves the common law to supply the procedure, and the old distinction between rights and privileges no longer limits fairness. Fairness here calls for disclosure and a chance to respond, not a trial-type hearing with cross-examination.
  5. The correct answer is C (Peer adjudication is a common statutory design and does not alone raise reasonable apprehension): Professional self-regulation depends on peers who understand the standards of practice, and legislatures routinely design discipline panels this way. A shared membership in the profession does not by itself lead an informed person to apprehend bias. Nomination rights and casting votes are not required by the common law.
  6. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because Vavilov requires a single answer when two bodies claim the same matter): Vavilov treats questions about the jurisdictional boundaries between two or more administrative bodies as a correctness category, because the rule of law needs one determinate answer about who decides. This is not the old true question of jurisdiction idea, which Vavilov abandoned. Neither body's expertise or privative clause changes that.
  7. The correct answer is C (Expertise is not a separate step; deference flows from the delegation, and expertise informs the review): Vavilov grounds the presumption of reasonableness in the legislature's decision to delegate, not in a case-by-case showing of expertise. Expertise still informs how a court reads the reasons and the context, but it is no longer a factor that selects the standard. The pharmacist's proposed proof requirement is the abandoned contextual analysis.
  8. The correct answer is A (Counsel may advise on law but cannot decide facts, and new issues require notice to parties): Tribunals may seek legal advice on questions of law without hearing from the parties. The advice must stay on law: counsel cannot influence findings of fact or credibility, and if counsel raises a new issue that could affect the outcome, the parties must be told and given a chance to respond.
  9. The correct answer is D (Unreasonable, because it does not engage with the central accommodation argument the complaint depended on): The accommodation issue was the core of the complaint, so a failure to respond to it is a failure of justification. Confirming that the rule existed does not answer whether it had to be waived. Vavilov treats adequacy of reasons within reasonableness and bars courts from inventing the missing analysis.
  10. The correct answer is D (The court may withhold relief in its discretion because a hearing could not change the outcome): Remedies on judicial review are discretionary, and a court may refuse relief where a fair hearing could not change a legally mandated result. Here the bylaw compelled suspension on conviction and the conviction was undisputed. A breach of fairness is serious, but it does not oblige the court to quash where the outcome is dictated by law. The old distinction between privileges and rights was abandoned long ago.
  11. The correct answer is A (Set aside, because the reasons ignore the key textual argument, which the judge's view cannot cure): A reasonable interpretation must grapple with the text and with a party's serious textual argument, and the definitions in the bylaw are exactly that constraint. The reviewing judge's agreement with the result cannot replace reasoning the tribunal never gave. Flawed reasons lead to remittal, not to a change of standard.
  12. The correct answer is C (The award is unreasonable, because it ignores a binding statutory cap without justification): A decision must be justified in light of the legal constraints bearing on it, including the governing statute. An award exceeding an express cap without explanation fails that test even under deferential review. Remedial discretion is real but operates inside the limits the legislature set, and the standard stays reasonableness.
  13. The correct answer is A (Reasonable apprehension of bias in a substantial number of cases, not merely his own): Institutional bias is a challenge to the structure itself, so the informed person must apprehend bias in a substantial number of cases rather than only the one before the court. Combining investigation and adjudication in one agency is common and is not invalid in itself. A claim that one individual played both roles on his file is a different, individual-level argument.
  14. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness applies: the issue concerns a home statute, not law central to the whole legal system): Vavilov confines the central importance category to questions significant to the legal system as a whole, such as privilege, not matters important to many parties or recurring in one regime. A definition inside the tribunal's own statute stays presumptively reasonable. A sliding scale of deference was rejected in Vavilov.
  15. The correct answer is B (Procedural fairness at common law, since the college's decision affects her rights and privileges): Revocation of a professional licence plainly triggers the common law duty of fairness, because the decision is final and affects an individual's rights, privileges and interests. Section 7 does not protect the pursuit of a particular livelihood or ordinary reputational harm, and discipline for maintaining professional standards is not the determination of an offence under section 11.
  16. The correct answer is C (No, cost, delay and an adverse interim ruling are ordinary and do not justify early intervention): C.B. Powell holds that courts should not interfere with an ongoing process absent exceptional circumstances, and the bar is high because expense, delay and unfavourable rulings are features of every proceeding. The rejected view that only jurisdictional challenges qualify is wrong because exceptional circumstances are not limited to a fixed category, but nothing in these facts meets the bar.
  17. The correct answer is A (Fairness applies because the transfer gravely reduces his liberty, so he should be told why beforehand): A transfer to maximum security significantly affects an inmate's residual liberty, and Cardinal confirms that the duty of fairness extends to decisions of this kind in prisons. The content is moderate: reasons for the transfer and an opportunity to respond, not a trial-type hearing. Security concerns may justify limited disclosure, but they do not remove the duty or allow it to be met only afterward.
  18. The correct answer is D (The court declines to reweigh the evidence since the appeal right covers only legal issues): Where the legislature limits an appeal to questions of law, the court has no mandate to revisit the tribunal's assessment of the weight of evidence. Palpable and overriding error governs facts only when the appeal right extends to them, and reasonableness is the standard on judicial review rather than on a statutory appeal. Correctness is not a general standard for factual findings.
  19. The correct answer is D (The statute allows delegation only to members or officers, so delegating to a firm exceeds it): A power to delegate is itself statutory, and the maxim means delegation is limited to what the statute allows. This statute permits delegation only to members or officers of the Board, so a private firm falls outside it. Remaining formally responsible or invoking agency law cannot enlarge the class of permitted delegates.
  20. The correct answer is C (Mandamus should be refused, since an adequate alternative remedy exists that she did not use): Mandamus is a discretionary remedy of last resort, and the Apotex test asks whether another adequate remedy exists. The statutory application to the Chief Adjudicator is fast and binding, so Ms. Lee should use it first. A breached time limit shows a duty but does not excuse skipping an available, effective route.
  21. The correct answer is D (The board has only the powers its statute confers expressly or by necessary implication): Administrative bodies are creatures of statute and have no inherent jurisdiction, so any remedy must come from the enabling statute, expressly or by necessary implication. A general public interest mandate does not supply a power to compensate private parties that the legislature did not confer. An order outside that grant would fail reasonableness review under Vavilov because it lacks statutory authority.
Case CHB74

Mr. Anil Deshpande teaches Grade 8 in Moose Jaw. He wrote blog posts on a private account criticizing his school division's curriculum. Section 12 of the fictional Teachers' Professions Act (extract) reads: 'The Discipline Committee may suspend a teacher whose conduct, in the Committee's opinion, undermines public confidence in the profession.' After a short hearing, the Committee suspended him for 60 days, stating that his posts were 'disrespectful.' It did not discuss his freedom of expression. Mr. Deshpande applies for judicial review, arguing that the suspension unjustifiably limits his freedom of expression under section 2(b) of the Charter. He does not challenge the validity of section 12.

  1. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness review, asking whether the Committee proportionately balanced the Charter protection against its statutory objectives): Where a discretionary decision engages a Charter protection, the Doré and Loyola framework applies within reasonableness review: the decision maker must proportionately balance the protection against the statutory objectives. Correctness is the tempting answer, but it is reserved for challenges to the validity of the law itself, which Mr. Deshpande has not brought. A bare mention of the Charter is not enough, and the Oakes test is aimed at legislation, not individual exercises of discretion.
  2. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because the validity of the provision is a constitutional question for the courts to settle): Vavilov confirms that constitutional questions, including the validity of a statutory provision, are reviewed for correctness. Expertise and a home statute do not change that, and Doré governs the Charter compatibility of a discretionary decision, not the constitutionality of the enabling law. Palpable and overriding error is an appellate standard for fact finding and has no place in this analysis.
  3. The correct answer is D (Set aside the suspension and return the matter to the Committee to reconsider with proportionality): The statute gives the penalty decision to the Committee, and several proportionate outcomes remain open, so the usual course is to quash and remit. Substituting a penalty is reserved for cases where only one outcome is possible. A section 52 declaration is unavailable because the validity of section 12 is not in issue, and damages do not cure a flawed decision.
  4. The correct answer is A (Late, since the period ran from the first communication of the decision, not the written reasons): The thirty days run from when the decision was first communicated, which was the email of 2 June, so 18 July is 46 days later and out of time. A judge can extend the period, so the court is not powerless, but an extension depends on the usual factors and is not owed because reasons came later.
  5. The correct answer is D (Decline to remit, because the statute allows one lawful outcome and remittal serves no purpose): Where the law dictates a single answer, sending the file back would waste time and resources, and Vavilov allows the court to decline to remit. The concession of the applicant is not what matters. Adopting flawed reasons would not be needed.

Practice Test 18 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The Federal Court, which reviews federal boards under section 18.1 of the Federal Courts Act): Judicial review of most federal boards lies in the Federal Court under section 18.1. The Federal Court of Appeal's first-instance review is limited to the specific bodies listed in section 28, which does not include the Parole Board. The Supreme Court is an appellate court, not a first-instance reviewer.
Case CHB75

Extract from a fictional Act, s 9: 'A party may appeal a decision of the Residential Tenancy Board to the Supreme Court on a question of law only.' Marguerite Dube, a landlord in Nelson, was ordered to refund a rent increase after the Board found her notice of increase was not validly served. The Board read 'served' in the Act to exclude delivery to a tenant's mailbox, and found as fact that Dube left the notice in the mailbox on 3 March. Dube appeals.

  1. The correct answer is D (Correctness, since appellate standards apply to a statutory appeal on a pure question of law): Where the legislature provides a statutory appeal, Vavilov directs courts to apply appellate standards, so a pure question of law is reviewed for correctness. The meaning of 'served' is an extricable legal question. Deference grounded in the Board's expertise, the pre-Vavilov reasoning, no longer displaces the appellate framework.
  2. The correct answer is C (Not reviewable on this appeal unless it discloses an extricable legal error, such as no evidence): A statutory appeal confined to questions of law does not open findings of fact. Only an extricable legal error, such as a finding made on no evidence, can be reached. Palpable and overriding error governs where facts are appealable, which is not the case here, and reasonableness is not the framework for statutory appeals.
  3. The correct answer is B (Correctness applies, because the personal delivery rule is an extricable legal question inside the mixed conclusion): Under Housen, as applied through Vavilov, a mixed question attracts palpable and overriding error review only where no legal principle can be extricated. Here the Board adopted a legal rule that personal delivery is required, and that rule is reviewed for correctness. Labelling the conclusion as mixed does not shield the underlying legal error from the Court.
  4. The correct answer is D (Common law fairness is an independent source of procedure and applies, since her interests are affected): The common law duty of fairness is triggered by a decision of a public nature affecting the rights, privileges or interests of an individual, as in Knight, and does not depend on the Charter. Here the findings damage her professional standing and cost her clients, so her interests are clearly affected. The statute need not mention a hearing, because the duty is implied unless clearly excluded.
  5. The correct answer is C (No, because common law fairness generally does not govern legislative decisions of general application): Decisions of a legislative nature and of general application, such as a regulation setting quotas for a whole class, do not attract a common law duty of procedural fairness; protection comes from the statute or the political process. The argument treats severity of impact as decisive, but the nature of the decision, not its effect, removes the duty. Cabinet is not immune from review, and the regulation could still be challenged on vires grounds.
  6. The correct answer is B (As a statutory office-holder removable only for cause, he is owed notice and a hearing): Constable Wright holds a statutory office removable only after a finding of misconduct, which brings him within the line of cases requiring notice and a hearing before removal for cause. Describing him as an employee misses that the statute fixes the grounds for removal. The hearing is owed at common law, not only when the statute prescribes its form.
  7. The correct answer is A (A reading is reasonable only if justified by text, context and purpose, with Charter-consistent meanings favoured): Reasonableness review asks whether the interpretation is justified by text, context and purpose, and where the language genuinely allows two meanings, one consistent with Charter values is favoured. This does not let the court select the narrower reading as its own correct answer. Ambiguity therefore leaves the warden no free hand, and the broader reading needs a justification tied to security.
  8. The correct answer is B (Section 11 does not apply, since professional discipline is neither criminal nor truly penal): Section 11 protects persons charged with an offence, meaning proceedings that are criminal by nature or that carry true penal consequences such as imprisonment or a punitive fine. Discipline imposed to maintain professional standards falls outside that category even when the sanction is serious. The section is not a general code of rights for every tribunal that decides misconduct.
  9. The correct answer is D (A senior departmental official may exercise a minister's power under implied authority, absent contrary statutory wording): The maxim against delegation is applied flexibly to ministers, whose operational powers are routinely exercised by officials in the department acting under implied authority. That implication yields where the statute points the other way or where the nature of the decision requires personal exercise. Seniority alone is not the test, and ratification is not the usual route to validity.
  10. The correct answer is B (The closed mind test, suited to elected policy makers, applies, so the challenge likely fails): For elected councillors making land use policy decisions, the Supreme Court has applied a closed mind standard: prior views and public positions are expected, and a challenge succeeds only if the member was not open to persuasion. Councillor Whitehorse listened, asked questions and engaged, so the evidence points away from a closed mind. The strict reasonable apprehension of bias test is for adjudicative decision makers, and no pecuniary interest or independence concern appears on these facts.
  11. The correct answer is A (Clear statutory authority for the overlap prevails over common law fairness, so the argument fails): Under Ocean Port, tribunal structure is a matter for the legislature, and express statutory language prevails over common law requirements of independence or impartiality. Only a constitutional challenge to the statute could change that result. Northgate may still argue actual or reasonably apprehended bias in a particular member's conduct, but the authorized overlap alone does not found it.
  12. The correct answer is B (The regulations are invalid for breaching the mandatory statutory comment period, despite Cabinet making them): The common law imposes no duty on delegated rule-making of general application, but here the legislature itself imposed a mandatory process. Cabinet must follow it, and non-compliance with a mandatory pre-condition goes to the validity of the regulations. Urgency is not a statutory exception.
  13. The correct answer is C (Other remedies are a countervailing factor, but may leave past harm unaddressed, so damages remain possible): Ward treats the availability of other appropriate remedies as a countervailing factor, not an absolute bar. Habeas corpus ended future harm but did nothing for the harm already suffered, so compensation or vindication may still call for damages. Deterrence is a recognized objective, and there is no presumption that a declared breach automatically attracts damages.
  14. The correct answer is D (Refuse, because the question is fact-specific and does not transcend the interests of the parties): A certified question must be dispositive, transcend the parties' interests, and raise an issue of broad significance. A question tied to credibility on particular facts does not. Certification is not limited to Charter questions.
  15. The correct answer is B (Invalidity alone does not ground damages without a recognized cause of action, such as negligence): A finding that an administrative decision was invalid or unreasonable is a public law result and does not itself entitle the claimant to compensation. The Co-op must fit its loss into a recognized private law cause of action, such as negligence or misfeasance, with its own elements. Blanket immunity for licensing is too broad, and the Act does not create a damages remedy on review.
  16. The correct answer is C (A lack of internal rationality, because the reasoning contains an unexplained logical leap): Vavilov identifies two fundamental flaws, one of which is a failure of rationality within the reasoning itself, such as a conclusion that does not follow from the premises. The board did not misread its statute, so the other flaw is not engaged. Adequacy of reasons is now assessed within reasonableness, and palpable and overriding error does not apply on judicial review.
  17. The correct answer is A (It should hear the claim, because prerogative decisions that engage Charter rights are reviewable): Operation Dismantle established that Cabinet decisions made under the prerogative are subject to Charter review, so the justiciability objection does not bar the claim. The court still requires proof on the evidence that the decision actually threatens the section 7 interest, which is where such claims commonly fail. Arguments limiting review to administrative law grounds or to statutory powers are rejected.
  18. The correct answer is A (Fairness and human rights law require reasonable adaptation so she can participate, short of compromising integrity): Procedural fairness is contextual and tribunals must also respect human rights duties, so a disability can require adjustments such as a support person and breaks. Identical treatment can produce unequal participation. Reasonable adaptation does not need express statutory authority or a medical order.
  19. The correct answer is D (The refusal likely breached fairness, because she lacked a real chance to prepare and respond): A regulator has discretion over adjournments, but it must exercise that discretion consistently with the member's right to a meaningful opportunity to prepare and respond. Refusing a short adjournment sought for a good reason, with newly arrived records and a newly retained lawyer, can breach fairness. Public protection justifies timely hearings, yet it does not outweigh a fair chance to meet the case, and fairness requires more than bare notice.
  20. The correct answer is C (A discretionary refusal made for a private purpose, outside the by-law's planning purposes): The mover's stated aim of benefiting a friend is a purpose foreign to the planning objectives that justify the power, so the exercise of discretion is improper. Institutional bias is the wrong frame because the defect lies in the purpose, not in the council's dual role. Motive is not irrelevant to legality, and the legislative character of a council does not immunize decisions made for a private end.
  21. The correct answer is D (It confirms reasonableness review applies but cannot remove the superior court's supervisory jurisdiction over legality): A privative or finality clause shows the legislature did not intend a statutory appeal, which is consistent with reasonableness review, but it cannot oust the superior courts' constitutional supervisory role over the legality of administrative action. Vavilov removed the special deferential tier that such clauses once triggered. Correctness is not applied to every interpretive question, so the clause does matter to the framework.
  22. The correct answer is A (Unreasonable, because the reasons ignore a central argument, so the court cannot follow the reasoning): A decision-maker need not answer every argument, but Vavilov says a failure to meaningfully account for a central issue raised by a party can make the reasoning unintelligible. The unmentioned policy and payment were the heart of the case, so the gap is fundamental. The court cannot cure it by constructing its own justification, which would replace the board's role with its own.
  23. The correct answer is C (A modest duty applies: a fair process and some explanation, but not an oral hearing): A complainant has a real interest in the outcome, but a screening decision does not determine anyone's legal rights, so the Baker factors point to a modest duty. That means a fair and genuine consideration of the complaint and a short explanation. A full hearing is reserved for the later discipline stage, and the claim that no duty exists ignores the complainant's interest.
  24. The correct answer is C (The investigative stage attracted a low duty, and the later full hearing cured any unfairness): At the investigative stage the duty of fairness is low, because nothing is finally decided against the person. Ms. Gagnon later received the allegations, full disclosure and a contested hearing, which gave her the protection that matters. Excluding evidence on a criminal model, or voiding the order because of an early interview, applies a stricter standard than administrative law does.
  25. The correct answer is A (The closer the process is to judicial decision-making, the more trial-like protections the individual can expect): Baker treats the resemblance of the process to judicial decision-making as a reason for more demanding procedure. The committee's formality therefore points toward fuller protections, not fewer. Internal rules do not displace the common law, and no statutory label is needed.
  26. The correct answer is C (Prove a breach, show damages serve a functional purpose, weigh countervailing factors, then assess quantum): Ward sets four steps: a Charter breach, a functional justification in compensation, vindication or deterrence, the absence of countervailing considerations such as alternative remedies or good governance concerns, and then a fair and appropriate amount. Bad faith is not a threshold requirement for executive breaches, and tort measures are not applied mechanically. A prior quashing is not a precondition.
  27. The correct answer is D (The January refusal, because a reply declining to reopen without review is not a new decision): Time runs from the communication of the decision under review, and a refusal to reopen that involves no fresh consideration does not restart the clock. Had the officer genuinely reconsidered new material, the reply could be a fresh decision. Knowledge of legal rights or later disclosure of notes does not move the start date.
  28. The correct answer is B (Officials may exercise a minister's powers in the minister's name unless the statute requires personal exercise): Under the principle of departmental decision-making, which differs from true delegation, officials act as the Minister's alter ego and exercise ministerial powers in the Minister's name unless the statute requires personal exercise. The maxim applies to genuine sub-delegation, not to routine departmental decisions. No written instrument or Cabinet approval is needed.
  29. The correct answer is C (Not strictly bound, but it must explain a departure from longstanding practice or risk unreasonableness): Vavilov treats past administrative practice as part of the context that constrains a decision, without imposing strict stare decisis. A decision maker that departs from established practice must explain why. Treating earlier decisions as irrelevant ignores the rule of law concerns of consistency.
  30. The correct answer is B (Read them sensitively to the tribunal's setting and expertise, without demanding polish or perfection): Vavilov cautions that administrative reasons are not to be assessed against a standard of perfection and must be read in light of the institutional setting and the decision-maker's expertise. A busy tribunal may write briefly and informally. Disregarding the reasons entirely is also wrong, because reasonableness review focuses on the reasoning actually given, not on the outcome alone.
  31. The correct answer is D (She should have received the substance of the report's case, because it shaped the final decision): A preliminary report can attract fairness when it effectively shapes the final decision, as the delegate's practice of adopting such reports shows here. Ms. Nair needed to know the substance of the allegations to answer them. Calling the step preliminary does not remove the duty, though it does not demand an oral hearing at that stage either.
  32. The correct answer is A (The senate offers a fast, full rehearing that can cure the alleged error, making it adequate): In Harelkin the Supreme Court refused review because a full appeal to a university senate was available and could cure the complaint. A quick rehearing with new evidence and counsel is the kind of convenient, effective remedy that favours refusing discretionary relief. Jurisdictional error is no longer the test, and rank in the institution does not displace review by itself.
  33. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, asking whether the college proportionately balanced the Charter protection against its statutory objectives): Where a delegate exercises discretion and the complaint is that the exercise limits a Charter right, not that the statute is invalid, the Doré framework survives Vavilov as a form of reasonableness review. The decision stands only if it proportionately balances the protection with the statutory objectives. Correctness is reserved for challenges to the validity of the enabling law or similar constitutional questions about the legal framework.
  34. The correct answer is D (Correctness, because division of powers questions require one final answer under the rule of law): Constitutional questions, such as which level of government may legislate over an undertaking, are a recognised exception to the presumption of reasonableness because the rule of law requires one authoritative answer. Expertise in labour relations does not displace this. The absence of an appeal clause only means the presumption would apply by default, and the constitutional exception overrides it.
  35. The correct answer is A (The Act does not apply because the hearing was voluntary, though common law fairness may govern): The Act is triggered by a legal requirement to hold a hearing, not by the fact that one was held. A voluntary hearing leaves the common law duty of fairness as the source of duties, whose content is set by the Baker factors. Partial or opt-in application has no basis in the Act.
  36. The correct answer is A (Improper, because members who did not hear the evidence cannot dictate disputed findings of fact): Consultation is confined to law and policy. Findings on disputed facts belong to the members who heard the witnesses, and outsiders cannot dictate them. The board may consult generally, so a bar on all full-board meetings overstates the rule, and a formal retention of authority does not help when the instruction was open.
  37. The correct answer is C (The issue is likely to recur yet evade review, so judicial economy favours deciding it): In the second Borowski step the court weighs the adversarial context, judicial economy and the court's proper role. A recurring issue that evades review is a classic judicial economy reason to hear a moot case. An adversarial context alone does not justify hearing it, and section 7 does not displace the doctrine.
  38. The correct answer is B (Correctness, because it is a general question of law of central importance to the legal system): Vavilov recognises general questions of law of central importance to the legal system as a whole as a category attracting correctness, and the scope of solicitor-client privilege is a leading example. Expertise in the tribunal's own field does not rebut that, and a variable margin of reasonableness is not part of the framework.
  39. The correct answer is B (Ask whether the methodology is justified and intelligible rather than substituting the court's preference): Reasonableness review under Vavilov is robust but deferential: the court examines the reasoning and outcome and does not decide the matter afresh or ask what it would have chosen. Rate design is a policy-laden specialty, not a correctness category. Patent unreasonableness no longer exists, and a regulator is not bound by strict precedent, though it must explain departures.
  40. The correct answer is D (No, the clause protects good-faith conduct, though bad faith would fall outside its protection): Statutory immunity clauses of this kind are given effect according to their terms, and this one covers acts done in good faith, including careless ones. Conduct in bad faith would fall outside the clause. A clause is not limited to intentional torts, and it does not depend on any characterization of inspection as core policy.
Case CHB76

Extract, Child Care Licensing Act (fictional): "The Director may suspend a licence where satisfied that the premises present a risk to children. The Director shall give the licensee written notice of a suspension and its effective date." The Act says nothing about hearings or reasons. Acting on an anonymous complaint, the Director suspended Priya Nair's home daycare licence in Saskatoon. Her letter said only that a risk had been identified. She was not told what the complaint alleged, and no inspection preceded the suspension, which continues until further notice.

  1. The correct answer is D (It arises because the suspension is an administrative decision affecting her rights, privileges or interests): Under Knight and Cardinal, the duty of fairness is triggered by an administrative decision of a public authority that affects an individual's rights, privileges or interests. The old right versus privilege distinction and the judicial or quasi-judicial requirement no longer control. Statutory silence on hearings does not exclude the duty, because the common law supplies it unless the legislature clearly says otherwise.
  2. The correct answer is C (Disclosure of the substance of the complaint and a genuine chance to respond, probably in writing): Baker directs attention to the importance of the decision to the individual, and the loss of a livelihood pushes content above the minimum, though an interim suspension does not demand a trial-type hearing. At least she must know the substance of the allegation and be able to answer it. The Act's prescribed notice sets a floor, and confidentiality of the complainant can be respected by disclosing the substance without the name.
  3. The correct answer is A (Quash the suspension and send the matter back to the Director for a fair redetermination): The usual remedy for a breach of fairness is to set aside the decision and return it for redetermination, because the merits belong to the statutory decision-maker. Reinstatement would go beyond correcting the process. Refusing relief because of a later inspection fails, since the Director has not reconsidered anything and no alternative remedy such as an appeal is available.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, because Vavilov abandoned true questions of jurisdiction as a category requiring correctness): Vavilov held that the jurisdictional label is no longer a basis for correctness; every grant of power is a question of what the statute authorizes, and a tribunal's interpretation of its home statute is reviewed for reasonableness. The only boundary-based exception concerns disputes between two or more administrative bodies. Expertise is not a condition of the presumption.
  5. The correct answer is C (A stay only suspends a decision's operation and cannot compel the officer to grant the permit): A stay suspends the operation of a decision, but a refusal operates only by leaving the applicant without a permit, so suspending it gives her nothing. To obtain what she wants she would need a mandatory interim order, which courts treat more demanding than an ordinary stay. Interim relief is available against refusals in principle, and a stay is decided before the merits, not after.
  6. The correct answer is D (Correctness, since the legislature chose an appeal route and appellate standards govern legal questions): Vavilov holds that where the legislature provides a statutory appeal, the court applies appellate standards: correctness for questions of law and palpable and overriding error for facts. The tribunal's expertise does not convert a statutory appeal into deferential review. The error alleged is a misreading of the statutory definition, which is a legal question and not a factual finding.
  7. The correct answer is D (Weigh convenience, speed, expertise and the nature of the alleged error in exercising its discretion): Whether an alternative remedy bars judicial review is a discretionary question: the court weighs factors such as convenience, expeditiousness, the expertise of the appeal body and the nature of the alleged error. Exhaustion is not a fixed precondition, so refusal is not required. A six month wait is one factor and does not by itself make the appeal inadequate.
  8. The correct answer is A (A fair hearing in accordance with the principles of fundamental justice for determining rights and obligations): Section 2(e) of the Bill of Rights guarantees a fair hearing in accordance with the principles of fundamental justice for the determination of a person's rights and obligations. It does not by its text give a right to publicly funded counsel, secure tenure for adjudicators, or written reasons. Those may arise from other sources, such as common law fairness or the enabling statute, but not from this provision.
  9. The correct answer is C (Remitting would be futile, since the statute and record permit one outcome, and delay prejudices him): Vavilov recognises that a court may decide the merits where remitting would serve no useful purpose, for instance where the statute and record leave only one reasonable outcome and delay weighs heavily. Those features are present here. Remittal is the general practice, not a bar, and the court need not find bad faith before ordering the result.

Practice Test 19 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (No, the finding still carries practical consequences, so a live controversy remains between the parties): The first Borowski question is whether a live controversy still exists. A finding that stays on her file and may affect future discipline gives her a continuing practical interest, so quashing it would still have an effect. The case is therefore not moot, and the court does not need to reach the discretion to hear moot cases.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reasonableness presumptively applies with or without a privative clause, which does not create its own category): Vavilov replaced the contextual analysis with a presumption of reasonableness and removed patent unreasonableness as a separate standard. A privative clause is simply one indication of legislative intent consistent with reasonableness and does not create a more deferential category. Correctness arises only through the Vavilov exceptions, none of which a privative clause triggers.
  3. The correct answer is C (The power remains subject to fairness, and the board's choice is respected but not conclusive): A power to set procedure is exercised within the common law duty of fairness, and the Baker factors treat the tribunal's choices as relevant but not decisive. The question is whether the process was fair in the circumstances, not whether the choice was reasonable under Vavilov. Efficiency alone does not make an unfair rule valid.
  4. The correct answer is B (As a factor deserving respect, but one that yields if written argument proves inadequate): The agency's choice of procedure is a Baker factor that attracts respect, particularly for a specialized regulator managing many applications. It does not bind the court, because fairness is ultimately a question for the reviewing court. The intervenor's stake, including any duty to consult, may show that written argument alone was not enough.
  5. The correct answer is A (Reasonableness, because no exception to the presumption applies to a specialized committee construing its own statute): Vavilov makes reasonableness the presumptive standard whenever a court reviews an administrative decision on the merits, including a tribunal's interpretation of its home statute. The exceptions are limited to legislated standards, statutory appeals and rule of law matters, none of which arise here. The category of true questions of jurisdiction was abandoned in Vavilov, so no preliminary jurisdictional inquiry is needed.
  6. The correct answer is C (Yes, because she is directly affected by the refusal of the application she supported): A sponsor is treated as directly affected by the refusal of a sponsored application, because her own interest in family reunification depends on the outcome. The statutory test asks whether the applicant is directly affected, not whether she is the named foreign national.
