No authorization from the husband is required, and the interest is a legal one, not mere sentiment.
  7. The correct answer is A (Genuine interest, since he shows no real stake, engagement or expertise in the bylaws' subject): The genuine interest factor looks at the applicant's stake, engagement, reputation and expertise in the issue. Mr. Patel has no connection to sign owners and only a general dislike of regulation, so that factor fails. A bylaw challenge can raise a serious issue, personal resources are not what defeats his claim, and public interest standing exists for those who lack a direct interest.
  8. The correct answer is B (The express steps are a floor, and the common law may add disclosure where fairness requires): Express procedural provisions are read as minimum protections, and the common law fills gaps unless the statute clearly excludes further steps. Knowing the case to be met is central to fairness, so disclosure is likely. Requiring proof that the report would have changed the outcome confuses the duty with a remedial discretion.
  9. The correct answer is A (Her security of the person is engaged, because apprehension profoundly affects a parent's psychological integrity): State removal of a child is a serious, state-imposed interference with the parent's psychological integrity, which security of the person protects. The child's interests are central to the merits, but they do not stop the parent's own interest from triggering section 7. Security of the person is not confined to bodily harm, and the trigger does not depend on a finding of unlawfulness.
  10. The correct answer is B (No, because the Act applies only where a law requires the tribunal to hold a hearing): The Statutory Powers Procedure Act is engaged when a tribunal is required by law to hold or afford an opportunity for a hearing. A paper-only licensing decision with no required hearing falls outside it, although the common law duty of fairness still applies. The Act is not a general code for all provincial decisions.
Case CHB77

Extract from the fictional Public Health Protection Act, s 14: 'Where a medical officer of health is of the opinion that a condition constitutes an imminent health hazard, the officer may order a food premises closed with immediate effect. Within five days the operator may make written submissions to the Board of Health, which shall confirm, vary or revoke the order.' On a Friday, Dr Amara Singh finds sewage backing up in the kitchen of Lakeside Grill, owned by Peter Dumont, and orders it closed that hour without speaking to him. The Board hearing is held on Wednesday.

  1. The correct answer is D (No, because the hazard was imminent and the statute provides a prompt post-order opportunity to respond): The statute authorizes immediate closure on an opinion of imminent hazard and gives the operator a hearing within days, which fits an urgent public health setting. Fairness was displaced in timing only, not removed. Treating the officer as exercising a policy function is wrong, because the closure targets one operator and attracts a duty of fairness that was met afterwards.
  2. The correct answer is A (A meaningful post-order hearing requires disclosure of the evidence supporting the closure, so he can respond): Because the prior hearing was displaced by urgency, the Board stage is where fairness is delivered, and a party cannot respond meaningfully without seeing the evidence against him. The Board is not a mere reviewer of the officer's opinion, since it may confirm, vary or revoke. The claim that the closure was itself unfair is rejected on these facts, so it cannot found automatic revocation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Urgency justified acting first, and the prompt, fair Board hearing satisfied the postponed duty of fairness): The legislature built a two-step scheme for emergencies, and the fair, prompt Board hearing delivered the participation that urgency delayed. Whether a later hearing cures an earlier defect depends on the statutory scheme and on how adequate the later process is, so the absolute claim that a later hearing never cures a defect is wrong. Here the statute itself structures the process around urgency. Mootness is the wrong label, because the real point is that no unfairness occurred in the scheme as a whole.
  4. The correct answer is C (Reach its own conclusion on the question, without deferring to the board's reasoning): Under correctness the court undertakes its own analysis and decides the question, showing no deference to the decision maker's reasoning, though it may land on the same answer. This is a different task from asking whether a decision is justified, transparent and intelligible. Palpable and overriding error governs facts on appeal and has no role here.
Case CHB78

Dr Priya Okafor, a Saskatoon physician, was found guilty of professional misconduct by her College's discipline committee over billing irregularities and suspended for four months. The Health Professions Act (an invented extract) gives no right of appeal to a court. She seeks judicial review, arguing the committee misread its governing statute, refused her request to cross-examine the complainant, and applied billing rules that intrude on federal criminal law jurisdiction.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness, as the presumption applies and no correctness exception is engaged on these facts): Under Vavilov the presumption of reasonableness applies when a tribunal interprets its home statute, and nothing here (no statutory appeal, no constitutional question, no true boundary dispute) rebuts it. Calling the issue a pure question of law does not trigger correctness, and appellate standards need a statutory appeal.
  2. The correct answer is B (The court itself decides whether the process was fair in the circumstances, weighing the Baker factors): Procedural fairness is not reviewed on a Vavilov standard. The court asks whether the procedure was fair given the context, using the Baker factors, one of which gives some weight to the tribunal's own procedural decisions. Labelling the refusal as reasonableness review would wrongly turn a fairness question into one about justification of the outcome.
  3. The correct answer is A (Correctness, because a constitutional question about the division of powers is a recognized exception): Constitutional questions, including the division of powers between Parliament and the legislatures, are reviewed for correctness because the Constitution must be applied uniformly. Expertise does not displace that exception, and it does not depend on any statutory appeal. Heightened reasonableness is not a recognized standard after Vavilov.
  4. The correct answer is B (Decline to quash, because remitting would be pointless when only one outcome is legally possible): A court may exercise its discretion to withhold relief where the result on remittal is inevitable, and the expired licence makes refusal the only lawful outcome. Quashing for a fairness breach is the usual response, which makes it tempting, but a pointless remittal serves no purpose here. Mandamus fails because no legal duty remains to be performed.
  5. The correct answer is C (Assess the reasons given, and decline to substitute a rationale the officer did not provide): Vavilov requires the court to review the decision actually made and the reasons actually given, and a reviewing court should not fashion its own reasons or accept a new rationale offered by counsel. The record may help explain what the officer did, but it cannot replace a missing rationale. Where the stated ground is flawed, the usual response is to remit.
  6. The correct answer is B (Policy-oriented members may hold general views; disqualification requires showing a closed mind on the specific issues): Under Newfoundland Telephone, the content of impartiality varies with the board's function, and policy-oriented members may bring general views to the table. The question is whether she is so committed to a position that she cannot be persuaded on the facts. An interest in outcome also disqualifies, but financial gain is not the only ground.
  7. The correct answer is A (The tribunal lacks inherent power over costs, so it needs statutory authority, which is missing here): A statutory tribunal has only the powers its statute confers, expressly or by necessary implication, and it has no inherent jurisdiction over costs. Where the statute is silent and nothing implies such a power, the order exceeds the tribunal's authority. Section 96 does not forbid conferring a costs power by statute, and patent unreasonableness is a pre-Vavilov label.
  8. The correct answer is D (Uphold it if justified by text, context and purpose, though the court prefers another): Reasonableness review of statutory interpretation asks whether the tribunal's reading is consistent with text, context and purpose, not whether it matches the court's preferred reading. The tribunal's reading is still reviewable and must be justified. It need not be the single possible meaning.
  9. The correct answer is A (Unreasonable, because the conclusion does not follow from the premises, so the analysis is flawed): Vavilov requires internal coherence: reasoning that rests on a logical fallacy or an unsupported leap cannot be justified. Reviewing courts do scrutinize whether the path from facts to result is rational. Asking what the court itself would have found improperly substitutes the court's view.
  10. The correct answer is C (Quash the refusal and grant sponsorship, as one outcome is open and remittal is futile): Remittal is the usual remedy, but Vavilov allows a court to decide the matter itself where the result is inevitable and sending it back would only waste public resources or add delay for the applicant. The sole ground for refusal fell away and every other requirement is satisfied, so only one outcome remains. A bare declaration leaves the family waiting, and damages are not an ordinary remedy on judicial review.
  11. The correct answer is C (Relief is ordinarily granted, since a denied fair hearing is not excused by speculation): The right to be heard has value independent of the outcome, and courts hesitate to guess what a hearing would have produced. Only in rare cases of an inevitable result might relief be refused. Strong evidence alone does not meet that bar.
  12. The correct answer is B (The agency has only statutory powers, and nothing in its statute authorizes charging this fee): A public body has no inherent power and can act only where its statute authorizes it, so a fee needs a source in the enabling Act. A reasonable amount or years of practice cannot create authority the legislature did not give. Inherent powers belong to superior courts, not to agencies.
  13. The correct answer is C (Revocation is an individualized decision with serious consequences, so common law fairness applies despite silence): A decision revoking one person's licence after twelve years is an individualized, adjudicative-type decision with serious consequences, so the Baker factors call for real disclosure of the allegations and a chance to respond. Councillors' elected status does not turn an individualized decision into a legislative one. Statutory silence does not oust common law fairness unless the legislature clearly excludes it, and fairness does not depend on the statute naming a hearing.
  14. The correct answer is C (Reasonableness, because the presumption applies even when a tribunal interprets an external federal statute): Vavilov removed the Dunsmuir exception for external statutes, so a tribunal interpreting a statute other than its own is still presumptively reviewed for reasonableness. The presumption yields only to the listed categories, such as a legislated standard, a statutory appeal or rule of law concerns. Expertise is no longer the organizing principle of the analysis.
  15. The correct answer is A (The standard written into the statute, because courts must respect a standard the legislature has prescribed): Vavilov begins from legislative intent: where the legislature prescribes the applicable standard, the court must follow it, subject to constitutional limits. The presumption of reasonableness is a default that gives way to such a direction. That the question is one of statutory interpretation does not matter, since a legislated standard displaces the common law categories.
  16. The correct answer is C (The Tribunal decides individual disputes independently, while the Minister sets policy and answers to Parliament): A tribunal is created to decide disputes independently of the government, whereas a minister makes policy and answers politically to Parliament. The power to give general policy directions does not make the Tribunal the Minister's agent for individual rulings. Tribunals have no inherent court powers and no legislative power beyond what their statute gives.
  17. The correct answer is A (The tribunal must exercise its extension discretion fairly, considering the reason for delay): Tribunals may set and enforce filing deadlines, but when the rules give a discretion to extend time, it must be exercised fairly by actually weighing the explanation, especially for a self-represented party. A rigid refusal to look at the reason ignores that discretion. Deadlines are not merely optional, and the duty to consider the reason does not demand an oral hearing.
  18. The correct answer is A (As a collateral attack, barred where the statute intended the appeal route to be exclusive): Under Wilson and Maybrun, an order remains valid and enforceable until set aside through the proper channel, and a party who ignores a comprehensive statutory appeal cannot attack the order indirectly in a prosecution if the legislature intended that route to be exclusive. The nullity argument misstates the rule, and no jurisdiction-only exception applies here.
  19. The correct answer is B (The officer used outside information, so he was owed notice and a chance to respond): Fairness for visa decisions sits at the lower end of the Baker spectrum, but an officer who relies on concerns arising from outside the application must give the applicant a chance to respond. Mr. Karimi could not have anticipated the call to the employer. Demands for an oral hearing, full file disclosure or reasons like a court judgment go beyond what this setting requires.
  20. The correct answer is D (Costs against it are possible, since it acted as an adversary on the merits): A tribunal that confines itself to defending its jurisdiction or explaining the record is ordinarily not exposed to costs, but one that takes on the role of a partisan on the merits can be. The board's conduct here moves it into the latter category. A presumptive entitlement against every decision-maker overstates the rule, and bias is not a precondition to costs.
  21. The correct answer is C (Whether a public authority made an administrative decision affecting the individual's rights, privileges or interests): Knight confirms a general common-law duty whenever a public authority makes an administrative decision affecting an individual's rights, privileges or interests, so that is the threshold inquiry. The judicial or quasi-judicial label was the older gateway and no longer decides the matter. A statutory hearing right can add to the duty but is not the gate to it.
  22. The correct answer is B (Upheld, because the misstatement is peripheral and does not undermine the core risk analysis): A reviewing court should not set aside a decision for a minor misstep; the flaw must be sufficiently central or significant to the reasoning. The wrong count of convictions does not touch the risk assessment on which the refusal rests. Palpable and overriding error is the appellate standard in Housen, not the test on judicial review.
  23. The correct answer is A (Unreasonable if the reasons ignore the severe consequences, since higher stakes demand justification): Vavilov states that where a decision has particularly harsh consequences for the individual, the reasons must justify it in light of that impact. Procedural fairness concerns are separate. The court asks whether the justification is adequate, not what penalty it would have chosen.
  24. The correct answer is C (Public safety justifies immediate interim removal if a prompt, meaningful hearing follows before final discipline): Where credible evidence points to an immediate safety risk, fairness is reduced and postponed rather than abandoned. The employer may act first and hear the employee soon after, and the hearing set for next week supplies the participation that was delayed. The view that prior notice is needed in every case ignores how urgency shapes the content of the duty.
  25. The correct answer is D (Consultation was inadequate, because the Crown must consult the collective that holds the treaty rights): The duty is owed to the Indigenous collective whose rights may be affected, not to whichever body is administratively convenient for the Crown. Where the rights are held and exercised through hereditary governance that the council does not represent, consulting the council alone cannot satisfy the duty. Requiring a prior court declaration reverses Haida Nation, which created the duty so that claims need not be proven first.
  26. The correct answer is C (No, the duty requires good faith consultation and, where warranted, accommodation, not the Nation's agreement): Haida Nation held that the duty creates a process obligation, and while a strong claim may call for deep consultation and accommodation, it does not give the Nation a veto. Consent becomes a requirement only after title has been proven. Merely informing the Nation falls well short of what a strong claim demands, and a binding agreement is not the test of adequacy.
  27. The correct answer is C (Decline: reviewing courts do not reweigh evidence, and the reasons justify preferring that report): Vavilov instructs reviewing courts not to reweigh or reassess the evidence the decision maker considered, absent exceptional circumstances, and the explained preference is intelligible and justified. Correctness is not the standard for facts on judicial review. The patent unreasonableness option reaches a similar result but rests on a standard Vavilov retired.
  28. The correct answer is D (Uphold it if its reasoning is rational and evidence-based, even though another inference is open): Drawing inferences from the evidence is part of the decision-maker's task, and a court will not interfere because a competing inference is possible. The question is whether the chain of analysis is rational and tied to the evidence. Plausibility findings attract the same reasonableness standard as the rest of the decision.
  29. The correct answer is C (Silence leaves the duty intact, since only clear statutory words or necessary implication can oust it): The common-law duty of fairness is a default that supplements the statute, and only clear statutory language or necessary implication can exclude it. Silence about procedure therefore leaves the duty in place. Reading silence as a complete code confuses a gap to be filled with an exclusion.
  30. The correct answer is D (The presiding members must understand French without interpretation, so the tribunal must assign members who do): Federal tribunals must ensure that those who hear a matter understand the official language chosen by the party without the need of an interpreter. Interpretation for the panel is not equivalent. The right does not depend on the party's inability to use the other language or on regional demographics.
  31. The correct answer is B (Fairness attaches to the board's decision, so she should see the report and respond before it): The board makes the final decision, and the duty attaches there. Because the recommendation will be before the board, Ms. Abara should see it and respond before the board decides. Saying she has no claim until then is wrong, since the final decision is reviewable, and the superintendent's preliminary step does not itself require a full hearing.
  32. The correct answer is A (Statutory discretion is not unfettered, and abuse for an extraneous purpose can ground personal liability): Roncarelli holds that no public discretion is absolute and that a decision made for a purpose foreign to the statute is an abuse that can ground civil liability against the person responsible. The premier's direction, not a mere error, is what made the act wrongful. Quashing a decision on review does not by itself create liability in damages.
  33. The correct answer is B (Unreasonable, because the conclusion does not follow from the findings and leaves a fundamental gap): The officer's own findings point toward strong ties, yet the conclusion states the opposite with no bridge between them. A reasoning chain that does not hold together, or a fundamental gap, makes a decision unreasonable. The absence of an interview raises a different, procedural fairness question that the stem does not engage.
  34. The correct answer is A (Its licensing power is statutory and public, so the Association's private corporate form is irrelevant): Judicial review turns on whether the body exercises a power conferred by legislation for a public regulatory purpose, not on its corporate form. Here the statute makes the Association's licence a precondition to practising, which is a public function. Limiting review to Crown agents or to publicly funded bodies confuses the form of the body with the character of its power.
  35. The correct answer is C (A clear, unambiguous procedural promise can create a legitimate expectation of that promised procedure): In Canada a clear, unambiguous and unqualified representation about procedure can ground a legitimate expectation that the promised procedure will be followed, and this is a factor in the Baker analysis. It protects the process, not the outcome. Detrimental reliance is an estoppel idea and is not required, which is why the reliance-based answer fails.
  36. The correct answer is B (By reading the record, including the report and debate, to discern and test the council's rationale): Where no formal reasons are required, Vavilov directs the court to the record and the context to understand what the decision-maker did and why. Here the planner's report and recorded comments let the court reconstruct and test the rationale. Presuming reasonableness from silence abandons the court's review role, and automatic quashing ignores that the record can supply the justification.

Practice Test 20 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The reasons fail to engage with text, context and purpose, which point to physical upkeep): Reasonableness review asks whether the interpretation is justified in light of the statutory text, context and purpose, and whether the decision-maker grappled with them. Here the reasons do not, and the wording points to the condition of the grounds. A court does not impose its own preferred reading, and no particular formula must be cited.
  2. The correct answer is D (A declaration may issue, since he has a real interest in a concrete, non-hypothetical dispute): A declaration may be granted on a real, concrete dispute even before a decision is made, if the applicant has a genuine interest and the opposing party has an interest in contesting. Mr. Dhillon's conviction places him squarely within the policy. Requiring him to apply and be refused first is a tempting argument for waiting, but it is not required where the question is concrete, and certiorari needs a decision to quash.
  3. The correct answer is A (The label does not control: an order aimed at one named company attracts fairness): Courts look to substance. An instrument that names a single company and orders it to act because of alleged violations is an individual decision dressed as a rule, so fairness applies. The common law does not require consultation for true general rules, which is why the public-consultation option misstates the doctrine.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reject it, because the category concerns systemic legal importance, not stakes for these parties): Central importance is about the legal system as a whole, not the dollars at stake or media attention. A bylaw dispute between a landlord and tenants is a local question and is reviewed for reasonableness. High stakes may affect how fully the reasons must justify the outcome within reasonableness review, but they do not change the standard.
  5. The correct answer is C (Correctness for the extricable legal test, and palpable and overriding error on the facts): On an appeal, a legal error that can be separated from the facts is reviewed for correctness. Applying the proper test to the evidence is a mixed question, reviewed for palpable and overriding error. Expertise does not displace appellate standards where a right of appeal exists.
  6. The correct answer is B (It falls outside the six month period, so he needs an extension from the court): Alberta's rules require an application for judicial review to be filed within six months of the decision, and the court may extend that period for good reasons. Seven and a half months have passed, so Teodor must seek an extension. Thirty days is the federal rule, and the open-ended approach describes Ontario, where no fixed period exists.
  7. The correct answer is C (Set the factors aside and start from the presumption of reasonableness, then check the exceptions): Vavilov replaced the contextual factors with a presumption of reasonableness that yields only to legislative direction or the rule of law categories. The old factors, including the privative clause and expertise, are no longer the way to choose a standard. A court following the framework asks first whether the legislature prescribed a standard or an appeal, and then whether a rule of law exception applies.
  8. The correct answer is A (The decision is unreasonable because it fails to account for a binding statutory directive): Under Vavilov, the governing statute is a legal constraint on any decision, and a decision that does not account for it cannot be justified. The complaint is about substance, not the length of reasons, so fairness is the wrong frame. Vavilov also abolished the true jurisdiction category and patent unreasonableness.
  9. The correct answer is D (Reasons need not resemble a judgment; they are read in the board's institutional context): Vavilov cautions that administrative decision makers are not held to the standard of judicial reasons, and that the institutional setting, workload and expertise of the body inform what is adequate. A court may not impose judicial habits such as citing authorities. Reasons must still show a justified line of analysis, so a bare statement of result is not enough.
  10. The correct answer is D (The Canada Industrial Relations Board, in a decision on an unfair labour practice complaint): The Canada Industrial Relations Board is one of the bodies listed in s 28(1), so its decisions go straight to the Federal Court of Appeal. The Parole Board, the Human Rights Tribunal and individual immigration officers are federal boards but are not on the list, so the Federal Court reviews them under s 18.1.
  11. The correct answer is B (No duty arises at this stage, since the report only recommends and determines no rights): A confidential report that only recommends, and leaves the company with full protections at any later proceeding, falls within the preliminary-decision exception. The serious-effect exception is not engaged because nothing is published and nothing is decided. The label administrative does not itself trigger fairness; the effect on the person does.
  12. The correct answer is B (Because credibility was in issue, fairness required an oral hearing despite the commission's rule): Where a serious decision turns on credibility, as in Singh, fairness generally requires the person to be heard orally, and the agency's own procedural rule does not override that. The word 'hearing' does not by itself mean oral, so the ultra vires argument fails. Deference to procedural choice is a factor only, and the short length of the ban does not remove the credibility problem.
  13. The correct answer is A (A breach of procedural fairness, because the Baker context required reasons and none were given): Where no reasons are given at all, the complaint is about procedural fairness: Baker held that a duty to give reasons arises in humanitarian decisions with serious stakes for the applicant and her children. Vavilov reasonableness review assesses the justification in reasons that exist, so it is the weaker framing here. Incorrectness and bias do not describe the defect.
  14. The correct answer is B (Unreasonable, because the officer treated the guideline as binding instead of considering his circumstances): Guidelines may inform a discretionary decision but cannot be applied as binding rules that stop the decision-maker from considering the person's circumstances. Consistency does not justify ignoring the statutory discretion, and the guideline's form is not the defect.
  15. The correct answer is C (Remit to a differently constituted panel, because the member's remarks taint any reconsideration): Where the apprehension of bias attaches to the decision maker, reconsideration by the same people would repeat the problem. A fresh hearing before the same panel does not remove the taint. Granting parole would usurp a decision that remains the board's to make.
  16. The correct answer is B (Section 7 is not engaged, but his livelihood interest still raises the Baker content of fairness): Economic interests and the right to pursue a particular livelihood are generally outside the scope of liberty and security of the person under s 7. The common law duty of fairness is separate, and under Baker the significance of the decision to a long-time plate holder raises the procedure owed. So the city is half right about the Charter but wrong that nothing heightened is owed.
  17. The correct answer is C (Fairness requires adapting the process so the Nation can meaningfully present its evidence orally): A power to set procedure operates within the duty of fairness, and a community whose knowledge is held orally may be unable to participate meaningfully through written filings alone. The panel's choice deserves respect but is not conclusive. No fixed rule requires oral evidence in all cases, and a breach of fairness is complete once participation is denied, whatever the final result.
  18. The correct answer is B (Whether the union acted arbitrarily, discriminatorily or in bad faith, not merely mistakenly): The duty of fair representation is met if the union considers the grievance genuinely and does not act arbitrarily, discriminatorily or in bad faith. The board does not re-decide the merits of the grievance, and an honest error or imperfect investigation does not by itself breach the duty. A union has discretion not to proceed with a weak grievance and owes no duty to arbitrate every case.
  19. The correct answer is A (Reasonable apprehension of bias arises for that member, so the decision can be set aside): Where a statute does not clearly authorise overlapping roles, a person who helped decide that a referral was warranted creates a reasonable apprehension of bias by then sitting in judgment on the same matter. Institutional overlap in a self regulating body can be valid when the legislature clearly allows it, but this member's own participation is the problem. Actual prejudgment need not be proved, and nothing shows the college as a whole lacks independence.
  20. The correct answer is B (Reasonableness applies; the clause does not raise deference and cannot oust constitutional judicial review): After Vavilov a privative clause is not a reason to alter the standard; the presumption of reasonableness already reflects the legislature's delegation. Patent unreasonableness no longer exists as a standard. Superior courts retain a constitutional supervisory role, so the clause cannot bar review altogether.
Case CHB79

Extract, Rent Review Act, s 12: 'In deciding a rent application the Tribunal shall consider operating costs, capital repairs and market rents.' Mei-Ling Chow, a tenant, challenged a 14 per cent rent increase sought by landlord Brodeur Properties. The Northern Housing Tribunal's four-page decision summarized the landlord's invoices for costs and repairs and approved the increase as 'in line with comparable units in the area'. Both parties had filed written submissions on market rents. Ms. Chow seeks judicial review, arguing the reasons are inadequate.

  1. The correct answer is A (Read the reasons holistically, with the record, to understand the Tribunal's actual reasoning): Vavilov requires a court to read the reasons holistically and contextually, alongside the record, as the starting point for review. It is not a line-by-line hunt for error, and it is not a search only for a defensible result. Merely listing the statutory factors does not establish a justified decision.
  2. The correct answer is C (Silence is not fatal; the court may read the record to discern, not invent, the reasoning): The record can help a court understand what the decision maker meant, and here the phrase about comparable units points to the market rent evidence both sides filed. What the court may not do is supply reasoning the Tribunal never gave. Requiring an after-the-fact affidavit would let the Tribunal rewrite its decision on review.
  3. The correct answer is C (Set the decision aside and remit it for reconsideration, since several outcomes remain possible): Where a decision is unreasonable the usual remedy is to remit the matter to the decision maker, unless only one result is possible on the law and facts. Here the rent could lawfully be set at several levels, so the court should not decide it. Leaving the flawed decision in force while reasons are added would let the Tribunal justify itself after the fact.
  4. The correct answer is B (The thirty-day period ran from communication of the decision, so he needs an extension of time): Under the Federal Courts Act, an application for judicial review must be commenced within thirty days after the decision was first communicated, unless a judge allows more time. Pursuing a parallel complaint does not stop the clock, though a court may grant an extension where the applicant kept a continuing intention to challenge and gives a reasonable explanation. The reasonable-time approach belongs to other regimes and not to s 18.1(2).
  5. The correct answer is C (Doré does not apply as the registrar had no discretion; the attack must target the provision): The Doré framework governs how a decision-maker exercises a discretion that engages Charter protections. Where the statute removes discretion, any Charter complaint is really about the validity of the provision, which must be challenged directly and justified under s 1 by the Crown. The registrar cannot be faulted for applying a mandatory command, and a registrar has no power to refuse to apply it.
  6. The correct answer is B (It is directly affected, and standing cannot depend on proving the right the hearing may affect): The statute's own test is direct effect, and the line crosses the area where the Nation harvests, so requiring proof of the right inverts the process. The duty to consult is a separate doctrine and does not exclude ordinary participation rights. Community-level participation does not require each member to appear, and the patently unreasonable standard is not the current framework.
  7. The correct answer is D (Genuine inability to pay, a prima facie meritorious claim, and issues of real public importance): Advance costs are available only in rare cases where the applicant cannot otherwise bring the case, the claim is prima facie meritorious, and the issues transcend the litigant's interests and are of public importance. The injunction-style test and the standing test address different questions. Relative resources of the Crown are not the criterion.
  8. The correct answer is A (His collective agreement supplies the remedy, so the court will ordinarily decline to review the dismissal): Where a collective agreement provides grievance arbitration for dismissal, that regime is the exclusive route, so a court will ordinarily decline to review the employer's decision. The employer's status as a public body does not override this. Deference and internal appeals are not the reason review is refused.
  9. The correct answer is A (The common law duty of fairness fills statutory silence, with content depending on the decision's importance): Where an enabling statute is silent on procedure, the common law duty of procedural fairness supplies it, and Baker treats the importance of the decision to the person affected as central. Ending a tenancy is serious, so notice and a chance to respond are required. Silence is not read as excluding fairness, and the old judicial-versus-administrative distinction no longer decides the matter.
  10. The correct answer is D (Having acted for the employer in this dispute, he cannot appear to approach it open-mindedly): Prior involvement as adviser to one side on the very matter now to be decided gives an informed observer reason to doubt his impartiality. The test is about appearance, so the worker need not show that confidential information was used. The law society's conflict rules are a separate matter and do not displace the tribunal's own duty of fairness.
  11. The correct answer is B (Delay she caused herself counts against her, and the college's share was not inordinate): In assessing delay, a court looks at who caused it and whether it was inordinate, so delay a respondent causes through her own requests is not counted against the regulator. Only a few weeks were attributable to the college, which is not inordinate. Calling adjournments procedural rights does not move responsibility for them away from the party who sought them.
  12. The correct answer is D (Yes, because the legislature cannot fully remove superior court review of the legality of tribunal decisions): Under the rule of law and the constitutional role of provincial superior courts (Crevier), a legislature cannot completely shield a tribunal from review of whether it acted within its lawful authority. A privative clause can influence deference but cannot oust review of legality. The idea that clear words suffice ignores this constitutional limit.
  13. The correct answer is A (A written process is likely unfair, since a serious credibility conflict calls for an oral hearing): Tribunals control their own procedure, but where credibility is genuinely in issue and the stakes are a professional livelihood, fairness generally requires an opportunity to be heard orally so the decision-maker can assess witnesses. A rule granting choice of format does not displace that requirement. It is also not true that every discipline matter needs an oral hearing, so the outcome turns on the credibility conflict here.
  14. The correct answer is D (Yes, because failing to meaningfully grapple with a central argument is a serious shortcoming): A decision-maker need not address every argument, but reasons must meaningfully account for the central issues and submissions raised by the parties. Ignoring the main argument leaves a gap in justification that the applicant can point to. He need not prove the clause was correctly applied in his favour, and silence does not create a formal presumption.
  15. The correct answer is A (Public interest standing is the likely route, since the club itself has no direct legal interest): The club as an entity has no legal interest of its own in the lake, and no member has applied, so private standing is doubtful. Public interest standing fits: the issue is serious, the club has a genuine interest, and it may be a reasonable and effective way to bring the matter to court. Associations do not sue for members automatically, and no rule makes standing wait for a member.
  16. The correct answer is A (Appellate deference applies, so the finding stands unless it contains a palpable and overriding error): Where the legislature provides a statutory appeal, Vavilov directs the court to use appellate standards: correctness for questions of law and palpable and overriding error for facts and mixed questions. Reasonableness is the presumptive standard only on judicial review without an appeal route. Patent unreasonableness is a pre-Dunsmuir standard that no longer exists.
  17. The correct answer is D (The registrar did not consider whether a minor omission justified a rejection that extinguished the appeal): The word 'may' confers a discretion, and a reasonable exercise weighs the trivial nature of the defect against the severe consequence of losing the right to appeal. Formal requirements are legitimate, so rejection is not unreasonable merely because it rests on form. No oral hearing is needed for a filing screen, and a rule need not define every term for the registrar to have power to act.
  18. The correct answer is D (Mixed fact and law without an extricable legal error is outside the appeal, so it fails): Housen treats an application of a correct test to facts as mixed fact and law, deferential unless a legal error can be extricated. A law-only appeal does not reach it. Palpable and overriding error is a standard of review, not a way of expanding the grounds of appeal.
  19. The correct answer is A (It does not apply, since the Bill binds only federal law and federal decision-makers): The Canadian Bill of Rights is an ordinary federal statute with quasi-constitutional status, and it governs only federal laws and federal actors. Provincial tribunals are subject to the Charter, the common law duty of fairness and provincial human rights and procedure statutes instead. It is not a constitutional document binding every government, and it is not triggered by provincial incorporation.
  20. The correct answer is B (The honour of the Crown, a constitutional principle governing its dealings with Indigenous peoples): The duty to consult is grounded in the honour of the Crown, as Haida Nation held, and the Crown's assertion of sovereignty over lands subject to Aboriginal interests engages it. Section 35 protects the underlying rights but does not itself create the consultation duty. Baker fairness addresses individual rights and does not explain this duty.
Case CHB80

Ms Adaeze Okafor, a citizen of Zandria, has her refugee claim rejected. A pre-removal risk assessment officer then decides she faces no personalized risk, relying on a country report Ms Okafor was never shown. She has credible evidence that, if returned to Zandria, state agents would detain and torture her for political activity. Removal is scheduled in ten days. She files for judicial review and seeks a stay of removal from the Federal Court.

  1. The correct answer is A (Yes, because removal to a substantial risk of torture threatens her security of the person): Section 7 protects everyone physically present in Canada, and Canada's own act of removing a person to a substantial risk of torture is a deprivation of security of the person. The view that harm abroad is not state action ignores that Canada's decision is the cause. Waiting for proof of actual torture would defeat the protective purpose of the right.
  2. The correct answer is C (She must know the case against her and be able to respond to adverse evidence): Where section 7 is engaged by a risk of torture, fundamental justice requires at least that the person know the case to be met and have a meaningful chance to answer it. An oral hearing may be needed where credibility is in issue, but that does not mean a full judicial trial. The low-fairness view and the proof-first view both misplace the trigger, which turns on the risk alleged, not on its later proof.
  3. The correct answer is A (A serious issue, irreparable harm on removal, and a balance of convenience favouring the stay): Interlocutory stays follow the three-part test from RJR-MacDonald: a serious issue to be tried, irreparable harm, and balance of convenience. The first stage is a low threshold, so the Crown's view that the case is weak does not by itself defeat the motion. A merits-style standard or a Vavilov review confuses the stay stage with the final decision on judicial review.
  4. The correct answer is B (Wide wording is reviewable, but upgrades advancing water quality fit the Act's purpose, so it stands): Broad enabling language widens the room for decision but does not oust review: the regulation must still be consistent with the Act's purpose and scope. Compelled upgrades that protect lake quality serve the stated purpose, and a power need not list each means to include it. The court does not ask whether the measure is the most effective or wisest one.
  5. The correct answer is A (Credibility assessments stand if reasonable, and the court will not reweigh the testimony of witnesses): A tribunal that hears witnesses is best placed to assess credibility, and a reviewing court does not reweigh testimony or ask which account it finds more probable. A finding resting on one believed witness is not unreasonable merely for lacking corroboration. Credibility is a factual matter and is not reviewed for correctness.
  6. The correct answer is A (Set aside the refusal and refer the matter back for redetermination after a fair process): A fairness breach ordinarily leads to the decision being set aside and returned to the decision-maker, who retains the discretion. Substitution is reserved for cases where only one outcome is possible, which the open statutory options exclude. Leaving the refusal in place or sending Kofi to reapply gives no relief for the breach already found.
  7. The correct answer is C (Correctness, because division of powers questions require a single final answer from the courts): Vavilov names constitutional questions, including the division of powers, as a category always reviewed for correctness because the rule of law needs a single determinate answer. The presumption of reasonableness is rebutted even though the board interprets its own statute and has expertise. A heightened scrutiny version of reasonableness is not a recognized standard.
  8. The correct answer is A (The promise was honoured, because an expectation covers only the procedure actually promised, nothing more): The content of an expectation is fixed by what was actually promised or practised, so the board met its commitment by holding the meeting. The doctrine does not inflate the promise into richer procedures or into a duty to agree. General fairness may add more in other settings, but not through this doctrine.
  9. The correct answer is D (The decision is set aside and sent back to the officer for fresh reconsideration): When a decision is unreasonable under Vavilov, the usual course is to remit it to the decision-maker, who holds the statutory mandate. A court may decide the matter itself only where remittal would serve no useful purpose because only one result is possible, which is not shown here. The court cannot repair weak reasons with its own analysis.
  10. The correct answer is A (A procedural entitlement to notice and comment, because the settled practice created a clear expectation): Legitimate expectation in Canada is a procedural doctrine: a clear practice or representation can entitle a person to a particular procedure, here notice and comment, before the decision. It does not create a substantive right to a particular outcome, and it does not depend on the statute prescribing the procedure. The usual remedy is to require the promised procedure, not damages.
